# Tasks from the 2026-09-26 reviews

The two reviews merged and de-duplicated: [language-design-review.md](language-design-review.md) (**L**) and [developer-experience-code-review.md](developer-experience-code-review.md) (**D**). Items refer to the reports' own numbering (`L P0-1` is the language review's recommendation P0-1, `D B4` the developer review's bug B4, `D item 9` its action-list item 9, `D S1` its security issue S1).

A second pair of reviews was run on 2026-10-05 from the same prompts: [language-design-review-2026-10-05.md](language-design-review-2026-10-05.md) (**L2**) and [developer-experience-code-review-2026-10-05.md](developer-experience-code-review-2026-10-05.md) (**D2**), cited the same way (`L2 P0-3`, `D2 item 7`, `D2 B2`). Their tasks are appended to the sections below, de-duplicated across the pair as the first pair was: where both reviews found one thing there is one task citing both, which is also the strongest evidence in this list. Neither found a type-soundness hole, and both re-ran the earlier reports' reproductions rather than trusting the ticks here -- 44 of 45 language tasks and all four earlier DX P0s confirmed genuinely closed.

A third review followed on 2026-10-08, from a new prompt pointed at the hand-written C the other two judge only incidentally: [c-implementation-review-2026-10-08.md](c-implementation-review-2026-10-08.md) (**I**), cited the same way (`I P0-1`, `I P1-3`). Its tasks are appended to the sections below. It is the first of the three to read the implementation as C -- sanitizers, a fuzzer, the two collectors' differing `free` -- and the first to find a segfault reachable from pure Ziman (A16) and a crash in the language server (A15); both P0s are small fixes and neither needs a language decision. It re-ran the earlier reports' reproductions as the second pair did: A11's quadratic `build_trace` and A12's spawned-thread hang are genuinely closed, measured not assumed. Two corrections it brought back are recorded where they belong rather than only here -- **A1 overclaims** (the parser guard it reports covers two of the three places that recurse; see A15), and a lead this file's own session formed, that `-fwrapv` was load-bearing for `native_bits.c:96`, it tested four ways and rejected (A21).

With the review backlog closed on 2026-10-09 (all 125 tasks), the next three are the first here that come from no review at all: they are lifted out of [../ROADMAP.md](../ROADMAP.md) (**R**, cited by the roadmap line they came from). Only the items that are *ready to start* were lifted. The ten open entries in the roadmap's "Larger, needs its own design pass" section were deliberately **not** mirrored here: each would flatten paragraphs of design reasoning into a headline, none has a checkable done condition, and keeping one item in two files is how "No map/dictionary type" survived long after `Map` shipped (c83d14c). The roadmap stays the home for a direction; this file holds work with a verifiable end.

Effort: **S** = a day or less, **M** = a few days, **L** = a week or more. Tick a box when the task is done; note the commit beside it.

Suggested order: A and B first (B1, B2, B4 and B5 are each about a day or less, and the web client depends on some of them), then C1, C2 and D1, which remove the most everyday friction, then E.

## A. Ziman runtime and safety (P0)

- [x] **A1** Stack-depth guard: a clean "stack depth exceeded" error instead of a segfault, from a per-thread limit read at run time (`src/stack.c`); the main interpreter thread and each `spawn` run on stacks sized from usable memory, thread count and `--stack-mb`/`--thread-stack-mb`/`--threads` (or the `ZIMAN_*` variables); parser, checker and deep values are guarded too. (L P0-1, D B4; S-M) **Partly wrong as written, found 2026-10-08 (I; see A15):** "parser, checker and deep values are guarded too" holds for expressions and values but not for types -- `parse_type` has no guard, and the language server never runs on the big stack at all, so a 68 KB document kills it. The class this task closes is narrower than this entry claims, which is the one convention this file still lacks: a way to mark a claim as partly closed.
- [x] **A2** Fatal-error boundary per thread and per request: a runtime error in a spawned thread ends that thread (logged to stderr, its defers run) and the process stays up; `spawn f(x) on_error handler` chooses another response per spawn (`os.fatal` keeps the old exit); `server.serve` answers 500 and releases the connection. (L P0-4, D B5; M)
- [x] **A3** Builders stay in their thread (chosen over locking): `#[thread_bound]` marks the type; compile errors for a `Channel` element, a `spawn` argument and a module-level `val`; a runtime owner check on every builder operation; docs corrected. `Iter<T>` is thread-bound too. (L P0-2; S)
- [x] **A4** Numeric holes: range-check int literals (including the negated minimum), saturate `float as int`, convert `float as bigint` exactly, reject `%` on floats in the checker. (L P0-3, D B6; S)
- [x] **A5** Fix the prelude namespace leak: a user struct named `T`, `K`, `V`, `R` or `U` fails to compile and the error names no file. (L P0-5; S-M)
- [x] **A6** Make `arr = push(arr, x)` amortised O(1) (200,000 pushes: 8.5 s, against 0.23 s with `ArrayBuilder`). (L P0-6, D; M). `push` shares spare capacity through a tip check (`ArrayStore`, CAS on `used`); 200,000 pushes: 0.2 s. `--lint` flags the still-quadratic `x = concat(x, ..)` and `x = concat_arrays(x, ..)` in a loop.


- [x] **A7** **`NaN` breaks `Map`/`Set`.** Canonicalise it in `hash` and wherever the collections use equality, so `compare` and key lookup agree; document that `==` and `compare` deliberately disagree on `NaN`. (L2 P0-3; S) A `Set<float>` holds two copies of a value `contains` denies, and `Map<float, _>` grows without bound on repeated `set` of one key while `map_get` returns `None` -- because `compare(nan,nan)==0` while `nan==nan` is false. Worse than Rust (no `Eq` on floats) and Java (normalises NaN). Done 2026-10-06: the trie hashed both NaNs to the same slot already (value_hash normalises -0.0 and now canonicalises every NaN encoding to one quiet NaN), so the defect was entirely in `keys_equal` calling `value_equals`, whose IEEE `a == b` is false for NaN -- the leaf reached its own slot and then compared unequal to itself, so the trie filed a second copy nothing could reach. `value_equals` is also the `==` operator, so it could not simply change: split into a shared `equals_inner(a, b, nan_reflexive)` with `value_equals` (IEEE, for `==`/`!=`) and `value_key_equals` (reflexive on NaN) over it, threading the flag through the recursion so a NaN nested in a struct or array key behaves the same. `contains` uses the key form too, since one builtin serves arrays, maps and sets and must answer "is this present" the same way for each. Verified that `==` stays IEEE at float, array and struct depth. Reachable from untrusted input: `parse_float("nan")` succeeds, and `0.0/0.0` is a runtime error so this was the way in. tests/fixtures/nan_map_set_key.zm; documented in SYNTAX.md's Map/Set bullets.
- [x] **A8** **Invalid UTF-8 escapes through the JSON and HTTP output boundaries.** Validate, or substitute U+FFFD, in the JSON string encoder. (L2 P0-2; S-M) A `substring` that splits a code point flows through `#[derive(Json)]` into a document Python's `json` rejects and Ziman's own `parse_json` accepts -- so it passes every in-language test and fails at the client. The asymmetry is the defect: no fixture can catch this by construction. Done 2026-10-06: reproduced both halves first, including the one that made it invisible -- `{"text":"caf\xc3"}` was refused by Python's `json` with an explicit decode error and accepted by this project's own `parse_json`, so a Ziman round-trip test could not see it. `put_json_string` (src/native_json.c) copied every byte above 0x20 through; it now decodes each sequence and substitutes U+FFFD for an invalid one, resynchronising a byte at a time the way decoders do. Substituting rather than returning a Result was deliberate: the alternative changes the signature of every encoder and of every derived `*_to_json`, for an input the caller usually cannot do anything about, and lossy-but-well-formed is what encoders and browsers already do. No second validator was written -- prelude.c's `utf8_decode_strict` (strict about overlongs, surrogates and out-of-range) moved to a header-only `src/utf8.h` so both ends of the pipe share one judgement about what counts as text. Note the source of the bad text is not `bytes_to_string`, which already refuses: it is `substring`/`char_at`/`index_of`, which work on byte positions by design. tests/fixtures/json_invalid_utf8_output.zm covers valid text of every width passing through untouched, a 3- and a 4-byte character truncated at each point, a derived encoder, and that control characters still escape rather than being replaced; every JSON line it emits was checked to decode in Python. json.zm's own doc comment promised the old behaviour and was corrected. Not changed: `parse_json` still accepts invalid UTF-8 on input, which is lenient-in-what-you-accept rather than a defect that reaches a client, and the HTTP body path, where bytes may legitimately not be text.
- [x] **A9** **`float_from_json` saturates a large JSON integer through `int64`.** (D2 item 2, D2 B2; S) A 30-digit JSON integer comes back saturated rather than as an error or a correct float -- silent data corruption, found on a medical measurement in the reference backend. Done 2026-10-06: the defect was not in `float_from_json` or in the JSON parser, both of which classify and dispatch correctly -- it was `bigint as float` itself (`src/interpreter.c:1506`), which went through `bignum_to_i64_saturating`, so *every* such cast reported a magnitude of about 9.2e18 whatever the number really was. 123456789012345678901234567890 came back as 9223372036854776000.0 against Python's 1.2345678901234568e+29. `float_from_json`'s Bigint arm performs that cast, which is how it reached a client. Fixed with a new `bignum_to_double` going through the decimal text and `strtod`, exactly as `decimal_to_double` already did -- which is why `decimal as float` was correct all along and was the clue. The old comment there reasoned that a huge bigint cannot be represented exactly by a float anyway: true of its low digits, false of its magnitude, and that is the distinction the cast was getting wrong. A magnitude past DBL_MAX is now infinity, the IEEE answer. **tests/fixtures/json_number_kinds.out was asserting the bug** -- `Ok(9223372036854776000.0)`, exactly int64's maximum as a float -- so the suite had been protecting it; corrected, with a comment in the .zm saying why. New coverage in tests/fixtures/bigint_as_float_magnitude.zm (the cast, negatives, zero, 400 digits each way, decimal unchanged, and the JSON path).
- [x] **A10** **`parse_float` rejects every subnormal**, so a legitimate client value becomes a 500 (`src/prelude.c:96` treats `ERANGE` as a parse failure). (D2 item 3, D2 B3; S) `measurementValue: 1e-320` is a valid double and a valid JSON number; rejecting it also breaks the `str`/`parse` round-trip agreement the docs promise. Done 2026-10-06: `strtod` reports two unrelated things with ERANGE and parse_float treated both as a parse failure. On overflow the result is infinity and no double exists -- still refused. On underflow the result is the correctly-rounded subnormal, an ordinary double the language can already hold and print: tests/fixtures/number_limits.out has been printing 5e-324 all along, so the value was representable and simply unreadable. Now gated on `errno == ERANGE && isinf(v)`. Text below every subnormal (`1e-400`) is accepted as 0.0, which is the same rounding that makes 0.1 inexact and what strtod, Python, Java and JavaScript all do. Overflow stays an Err, leaving a deliberate asymmetry now documented: `parse_float("inf")` is Ok because that text asks for infinity, while `1e400` is a finite number this type cannot hold and is better refused than silently turned into infinity. Checked for siblings and found none: `parse_int`'s ERANGE is genuinely out of int64 range, parser.c:396 is the integer-literal path, subnormal *literals* in source always worked, and the JSON number parser was already correct -- so the client 500 came from parse_float on a string field, the only broken entry point. tests/fixtures/parse_float_subnormal.zm; prelude.zm's doc comment now states the round-trip guarantee and the asymmetry.
- [x] **A11** **`build_trace` is quadratic and unbounded, and `GC_set_warn_proc` is never called** (`src/error.c:289`; the warn proc belongs next to `src/main.c:288`). (L2 P1-1; S) A stack-depth error costs 32.5s, 1.26GB and 97,390 stderr lines, of which 5,906 are leaked Boehm warnings -- an input-triggered DoS in a server. Note for whoever takes this: `--locals` (d205cc4) added a second `realloc`+`memcpy` per frame on its own path, so that path is doubly quadratic; the default path is unchanged, and the quadratic predates it. Done 2026-10-06. Measured worse here than L2 reported: 402s, 1.97GB RSS, 198,578 stderr lines, 23,170 of them Boehm warnings. Now 2.2s, 888MB (the 512MB stack and the frame array, not the trace), 43 lines, 0 warnings. Three changes, because the quadratic was only part of it: the buffer grows by doubling instead of a fresh malloc-and-copy per frame; the trace prints the innermost 32 frames, a count of what was elided, then the outermost 8, since an 8MB error message is useless however fast it is built (the deepest trace any fixture expects is 5 frames, so nothing ordinary changed); and GC_set_warn_proc is finally installed, showing the first 3 warnings and counting the rest away behind one line, with ZIMAN_GC_WARN=1 to see them all. Also fixed a defect of my own that only this task surfaced: `--locals` rendered values on an exhausted stack, re-tripping the check inside value_display and raising the overflow again from inside its own report -- 7,373 lines. The first attempt to gate that on `ziman_stack_remaining()` made it a segfault, because ziman_stack_slow sets the limit to NULL while reporting and that function calls back into it, re-raising. Replaced with an explicit `ziman_stack_reporting_overflow()` flag; the trace now says `(--locals omitted: too little stack left to inspect values safely)` rather than silently showing nothing. tests/fixtures/stack_depth_trace_bounded.zm.
- [x] **A12** **A dead `spawn`ed thread leaves a blocked `receive` hanging forever.** Close a channel whose only remaining peer has ended so the `receive` fails with a diagnostic; document the supervision pattern `server.serve`'s `on_error` already demonstrates. (L2 P1-7; M) `tests/fixtures/error_stack_trace_spawned_thread.zm` relies on the harness timeout for exactly this reason and its own comment says so. Independently corroborated during the review itself: a scratch program was still hung 55 minutes later. The actor pitch needs its third leg. The cheap interim mitigation is the `--lint` rule in F9. Done 2026-10-06, by detection rather than by closing channels. Reference-counting senders per channel was the suggested route and is the fragile one: get it wrong and you either invent a deadlock or restore the hang. Instead the test Go uses -- if every live thread is blocked on a channel then nothing can ever send, so no blocked thread can ever wake. Each indefinite wait (`channel_receive`, and `channel_send` on a full bounded channel) uses a 100ms timed wait and re-asks that global question; two atomics count live threads and blocked ones, and the condition must hold across two consecutive polls, so a transient mid-update read cannot fire it. Threads are counted from the parent *before* pthread_create, since counting from inside the new thread leaves a window where it is live but invisible. Deliberately conservative: a thread computing, sleeping or in a syscall is live and not blocked, and `receive_timeout` does not count, so it fires only when every thread really is waiting on a channel. Raising an error rather than closing the channel matters because `receive_option` answers None on a closed channel and would have turned the hang into a silent wrong answer. Verified no false positives: 851 fixtures in all three builds, a worker that sleeps 2s before sending, four workers blocked while main sleeps then sends, and the server at concurrency 1/8/32 (843/1597/943 rps, 0 failed). tests/fixtures/spawn_deadlock_detected.zm; error_stack_trace_spawned_thread.zm's comment and exit behaviour corrected, since it no longer needs the harness timeout. SYNTAX.md documents it.
- [x] **A13** **Replace `type_holds_thread_bound`'s `depth > 12` cutoff** (`src/typecheck.c:56`) with visited-set cycle detection, and make genuine unbounded recursion an error rather than a silent `0`. (L2 P2-2; S) A `StringBuilder` twelve structs deep passes `spawn` statically, which is the one hole found in the `#[thread_bound]` story. Done 2026-10-06: reproduced at 15 structs deep (accepted, where the same StringBuilder one struct deep is correctly rejected), then replaced the cutoff with a path-based visited set of declarations built on the stack -- no allocation, and the bound is the number of distinct declarations rather than a guess. Pruning is per path, not global, so a struct reached twice through different fields is still examined both times. The second half of this task turned out not to be needed: with cycles terminating exactly there is no unbounded recursion left to turn into an error, which is a better outcome than the error originally asked for. Checked that the cycle pruning does not hide a real builder -- a recursive `struct Node { sb: StringBuilder, kids: [Node] }` and a mutually recursive A/B pair with the builder on the far side are both still rejected, while a recursive type holding none is accepted and terminates. tests/fixtures/thread_bound_deep_nesting.zm pins all four.
- [x] **A14** **Check thread-bound captures at lambda construction.** (L2 P2-3; M) Turns the common lambda-capture case from a runtime hang into a compile error; the capture set is already known where the lambda is checked. Done 2026-10-06. The capture set is known at the lambda, but the `spawn`/`send` that must refuse it is usually elsewhere (`val f = fn() { sb_append(sb, "x") }` then `send(ch, f)` three lines later), so the fact is recorded on the lambda's *type* -- `Type.func_captures_thread_bound`, which type_holds_thread_bound now answers for TY_FUNC. It lives in the four bytes of padding that already followed `kind`, so sizeof(Type) is unchanged at 64 bytes, which matters because that struct is copied on every arithmetic result in a running program. `send`/`try_send` additionally check the *value's* type, not only the channel's declared element type: `Channel<fn() -> string>` as written cannot carry the flag, so a lambda literal sent directly would otherwise still have slipped through. Two existing fixtures changed meaning rather than broke -- thread_bound_{,iter_}used_from_another_thread expected the *runtime* owner error and now get the compile-time refusal, which is exactly what this task asked for; their expectations and comments are updated and the runtime checks in prelude.c and iter.c stay as a backstop. New coverage in tests/fixtures/thread_bound_lambda_capture_via_val.zm (via a val, a lambda capturing nothing thread-bound still crossing, and local use still allowed). SYNTAX.md's #[thread_bound] section rewritten, since it said a closure capturing one was caught at use rather than statically.


- [x] **A15** **`parse_type` recurses with no stack guard, and the language server is what pays.** `ZIMAN_STACK_CHECK()` at the top of `parse_type` (`src/parser.c:162`). (I P0-1; S -- one line) A1 reports the parser guarded; this is the third recursive path and it is outside the guard. The severity comes from somewhere else: `src/main.c:384-386` returns `lsp_main()` *before* `:397` configures the stack and `:400` runs the program on the big one, so the LSP runs on `ulimit -s` -- 8 MB here and almost everywhere -- and `--stack-mb` cannot reach it even in principle. Over the real protocol, one `textDocument/didOpen`: a type nested 20,000 deep (39 KB) is fine, 35,000 deep (68 KB) is SIGSEGV with no diagnostic, and an *expression* nested 200,000 deep (390 KB) -- the construct that has the guard -- is fine. That A/B is the finding, and it reproduced identically when re-run independently. Not merely a small-stack problem either: at this machine's default 512 MB main stack a 6.4 MB file still exits 139. About 168 bytes of stack per level; thresholds 50k/200k/400k at `--stack-mb` 8/32/64. **Done 2026-10-08, and the one-line fix in the task was not enough -- that is the finding.** The guard in `parse_type` stops the parser, and then `check_type_well_formed` (`src/typecheck.c:4188`) segfaulted at 35,000 deep on an 8 MB stack: a type the parser accepts is still walked by eleven other recursive functions, each with its own frame size, so the parser cannot be the single gate. All eleven now carry `ZIMAN_STACK_CHECK()`, the house pattern from `parse_unary`/`eval_expr`: `reclassify_type_params` (parser), `self_qualify_type` and `resolve_type_refs` (loader), `substitute_type`, `unify_type`, `type_mentions_any`, `type_fully_bound`, `type_supports_ordering` and `check_type_well_formed` (typecheck), and `type_equals`/`type_name` (ast). `type_supports_equality`/`type_supports_hashing` already had depth caps and were left alone. Swept six type shapes (`[T]`, `Result`, `fn(..)`, `Map`, `Set`, `Channel`) x depths 2,000/20,000/60,000 x `--stack-mb` 1 and 8: **zero segfaults, every case either a clean type error or the depth error**, where before the fix the array shape alone segfaulted at 20,000. **The second half was the error message's own advice.** It says "raise it with --stack-mb=N", which `--lsp` accepted and ignored, because `src/main.c` returned `lsp_main()` before the stack was configured. The server now runs on the big stack through `ziman_run_on_big_stack` like a program does, so a 390 KB document type-checks on the default stack with no limit hit at all, and `--stack-mb=1` trips it cleanly with a diagnostic rather than a dead server. New fixture `stack_depth_nested_type.zm` (20,000 deep at `--stack-mb=1`), the twin of the existing `stack_depth_nested_source.zm` for expressions; 885 fixtures pass, `make check` green.
- [x] **A16** **`add_thread` recurses unbounded with a VLA frame, and allocates quadratically beside it.** Bound it (`src/regex.c:747`): a stack check now, a worklist properly, and a documented cap on `n_inst x ncap`. (I P0-2; M) `regex.compile("()" * 6000)` then `regex.is_match` exits 139 on both `./ziman` and `./ziman-nogc` -- pure Ziman, no natives -- at **every** stack size up to the 768 MB `MAX_STACK_MB` ceiling, so no configuration escapes it. Two independent halves: the 96 KB VLA at `:758` (`int tmp[2 * (vm->ncap + 1)]`, the tree's only `-Wvla` hit) times a depth bounded only by `n_inst`, about 1.1 GB of stack; and `capbuf` at `:789` (`n_inst x ncap x sizeof(int)`, 1.3 GB for this pattern and 2.6 GB for the pair), quadratic in group count, from an 18 KB source file, unchecked like the four allocations around it. The regex compiler's existing nesting-depth limit misses it because `"()" * n` is **wide, not nested**. One diagnosis to carry over: the ASan "heap-buffer-overflow at `:759`" an earlier run reported is mislabelling -- the overflowed region is that `capbuf` and the failing stack pointer is inside it, so this is stack exhaustion walking into an adjacent mapping. A capacity check at `:759` would fix neither half. **Done 2026-10-08, both halves, and the two limits already in the file are why neither was caught.** `MAX_INSTRUCTIONS` (20,000) bounds the program and the nesting limit (200) bounds depth, but the capture buffers are `n_inst x 2 x (n_groups + 1)` ints and **nothing bounded that product** -- 6,000 groups is 12,003 instructions, inside the instruction limit, and wide, so the nesting limit never sees it. The closure walk is now iterative over an explicit worklist on the heap, with the capture scratch bump-allocated from one buffer per call; both bounds come from the `mark` array, which admits each instruction once per generation, so the worklist cannot pass `2 * n_inst + 2` entries and the scratch cannot pass `n_inst * ncap` ints. **Thread order is preserved exactly** -- entries pop LIFO and `I_SPLIT` pushes `y` before `x`, which reproduces the recursion's depth-first, x-first order, and that order is what makes a match leftmost-first. Second half: a documented `MAX_MATCH_BYTES` of 64 MB, checked at the end of `regex_compile` where a pattern is still a reportable error, computed in 64-bit arithmetic over everything a match allocates. The refusal names the size and the way out, and the way out is tested: `pattern needs too much memory to match: 1650 MB for 6000 capture groups over 12003 instructions (the limit is 64 MB; use (?:...) for a group whose text you do not need)` -- and 6,000 *non-capturing* groups compile and match fine. 400 and 1,000 capturing groups still work, the latter at `--stack-mb=1` where it needed ~32 MB of C stack before. `regex_search` now checks all nine of its allocations and returns **-1** rather than 0, because "no match" is a wrong answer to an allocation failure; `native_regex.c` turns that into a runtime error at both call sites. New `tests/unit/regex_test.c` and `make test-regex` (in `unit-tests`), which is where this belongs -- the engine has no dependency on the interpreter, and **the same test under ASan+UBSan is clean**, which is the only check that actually validates the new bounds. Note for anyone sanitizing the whole binary instead: every regex fixture trips `gc_scan_stack_roots` (`src/gc_scan.c:32`) under ASan, the conservative-scanner-vs-redzone conflict the review already recorded as a test-setup fact, not a bug. Also fixed on the way: the new refusal path frees `group_names` and its entries (the pre-existing parse-failure paths still leak them -- A20's territory). 886 fixtures, `make check` green. **Reviewed 2026-10-08 and the number holds:** 64 MB is **712x** the largest pattern anywhere in this repo and ~2,000x the largest realistic hand-written one (a full RFC-5322-ish email with named groups needs 0.02 MB). Two facts settle it -- with **zero capture groups the budget is unreachable** (the largest possible 19,000-instruction group-free pattern needs 1.67 MB), so it is a capture-group cap in disguise and the `(?:...)` advice in the message is therefore always the right advice; and **no pattern with fewer than ~1,170 capture groups can trip it** (`()`x1150 is allowed at 60.8 MB, `()`x1200 refused at 66.2 MB), against a corpus maximum of 3 groups. Compile-time refusal over a product is precisely RE2's design, whose own default is 8 MiB. The one respect in which this differs from both RE2 and PCRE2 is that it is not settable by the caller -- see A28, which is the gap worth closing rather than the number. **Adversarially reviewed 2026-10-08, and the rewrite survived.** The order claim was treated as a hypothesis to falsify, against two oracles: **6,664,545** (pattern, subject, start-offset) comparisons between the recursive engine at `27c5034` and this one -- **zero** capture or match-offset differences -- and **2,257,626** against Python's `re`, with **zero** defects. On the decisive subset, 2.07 M cases where no quantified body can match empty (removing the one shape where a backtracking engine and a Pike VM are known to differ), the two agree on every capture offset, including multi-byte subjects where a group boundary falls inside a code point. All 5,815 divergences from Python were classified from its own parse tree: 5,813 are the empty-iteration rule (`(a*)*` on `"a"` -- and **verified byte-identical in `27c5034`**, so predating the rewrite) and 2 are Python 3.11+ reading `X{m,n}+` as possessive. **The corpus was shown to have power** rather than assumed to: a mark-on-push mutant differs on 45,626 lines and an x-before-y mutant on 365,774 (see A29, A30). The bounds, the per-call scratch reset, the exactness of `needed` and the `-1` contract were each verified by instrumented assertion builds over 868 M calls, not by reading. The one gap in that review -- it could not run ASan or UBSan, the runtimes being absent from its worktree -- is the half this entry already covers, since the unit test and the full 808-fixture UBSan sweep were run here. Two limits it recorded that nothing currently states: the 64 MB budget is **per call**, so N concurrent matchers can each hold one (and `native_regex.c` caches up to 256 compiled patterns), and `saved[in->x] = (int)pos` silently truncates for a subject over 2 GiB, since `regex.h` types offsets as `int`.
- [x] **A17** **One no-op `free` costs 45x memory, in the direction nobody expects.** Give `src/iter.c:133` a real `free` with the `push_macro`/`#undef` idiom already in `src/native_http.c:15-27`; same for the three `strndup` buffers at `src/lsp.c:801`, `:576` and `:621`. (I P1-1; S) The buffer is libc's, from `getline`, so under the collector build `zgc.h`'s macro turns its release into a no-op and Boehm cannot reclaim a block it never allocated: **1.27 GB RSS under `./ziman` against 28 MB under `./ziman-nogc`** -- the build whose own `Makefile` comment says "nothing is ever freed" is the one that holds. 20,000 `fs.lines` iterations over a file with 60 KB lines; the arithmetic (20,000 x 60 KB) confirms the mechanism rather than inferring it. This is the two-collector lifetime divergence the review prompt asks about, in its cheapest form. **Done 2026-10-08. Measured here as 104x, not 45x, on the same program:** 1,293,008 kB of peak RSS before against 12,464 kB after, both on the collector build, the only difference being one `free` -- and 0.9 s faster too (3.50 s to 2.62 s), since Boehm is no longer scanning a heap that grows all run. `ziman-nogc` peaks at 27,492 kB, so the collector build is now the *lighter* of the two rather than 46x the heavier. The before number is from a binary built from `HEAD:src/iter.c` with everything else from this tree, so the comparison isolates the one line. **`libc_free` is the idiom** -- `#pragma push_macro("free")` around a one-line helper, the same suspension `src/native_http.c:15-27` uses to read `malloc.h`. **The `lsp.c` half turned out to be worse than reported and is not fixed the same way.** `dirname_copy` returned `strndup` on one branch and `strdup` on the other; `zgc.h` redirects `strdup` and not `strndup`, so *which allocator owned the result depended on which branch ran* -- the no-op `free` leaked the libc half, and a real `free` would have handed a collector block to libc, which is heap corruption rather than a leak. Both `strndup` calls are now a `copy_prefix` helper over the project's own `malloc`, so the pointer has one owner in both builds and nothing needs a real free. **A guard, because nothing in the compiler can see this class:** `tools/check_libc_free.py`, in `make check` as `libc-free-check`, fails any `src/*.c` that calls a libc allocator zgc.h does not redirect (`getline`, `getdelim`, `strndup`, `asprintf`, `vasprintf`) without reaching a real free. Verified against the bug itself: restoring the old `iter.c` makes it fail and name the file. It is the `check_not_yet.py`/`check_experimental.py` pattern -- a rule a human would otherwise have to remember, written down where it runs. 886 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **A18** **`--fmt` and the LSP's formatter buffer a quadratic result in memory.** A depth or size limit in the formatter, streaming output in `--fmt`, and a reply cap in `handle_formatting` (`src/lsp.c:1403`). (I P1-2; S for the limit, M for streaming) A 24 KB document makes the language server build a 144 MB reply at 1.0 GB RSS, and 80 KB reaches 9.7 GB; on the CLI, 8,000 nested bare blocks cost 1.45 s and 1.2 GB, 20,000 cost 10.5 s and 9.7 GB, and 32 KB of input formats to 256 MB of output. Be precise about which half is the bug: the quadratic **output** is inherent, since the formatter indents every line by its depth and 4 x n^2/2 characters is the correct answer, but building all of it in memory before writing a byte is not, and the LSP path makes it remote-reachable. A fuzzer found this class as ten `--fmt` hangs before it was measured. **Done 2026-10-08, and streaming turned out to be the wrong fix.** The task offered a limit (S) or streaming (M); streaming is the larger half and it would have been wasted work. `format_source` is a string-in, string-out function and the language server needs the whole answer anyway to put it in one JSON reply, so streaming would have helped only the CLI, and only by making a 256 MB file instead of a 256 MB buffer. **Bounding the depth removes the blowup at its source**, because the output is O(lines x depth) and it is the depth that was unbounded: `FORMAT_MAX_NESTING` of 200, the same limit the regex compiler puts on its own nesting, checked once over the token list rather than inside the emission passes, which run up to 60 times. Measured: 20,000 nested braces went from **10.5 s and 9.7 GB** of RSS to a refusal in 0.00 s at 9 MB, and 2,000 and 8,000 likewise; 150 still formats. The error names the depth, the limit and the consequence -- `line 266: nesting is too deep to format (261 brackets open; the limit is 200, and the indentation alone would be 1044 spaces a line)`. Real code does not pass about 15. **The reply cap is now a backstop rather than the fix**: `LSP_MAX_FORMAT_REPLY` of 8 MB in `handle_formatting`, which matters because the reply is escaped into JSON beside itself in a process an editor keeps open all day. Verified over the real protocol with a `textDocument/formatting` request: the 23 KB document that produced a 144 MB reply at 1.0 GB RSS is now refused in 0.00 s at 8.8 MB, while ordinary and moderately nested documents still format. New fixture `fmt_nesting_too_deep.zm`; 887 fixtures, `make check` green -- including `fmt-check` over `lib/`, the backend and the examples, none of which comes near the limit. **Reviewed 2026-10-08: the number holds, the claim about it did not.** Measured over 1,119 `.zm` files with the formatter's own counter: median depth **3**, p99 **8**, deepest real file **10** (`service/testdata.zm` and `lib/core/regex.zm`), so 200 leaves **20x headroom** -- and `opens()` counts only `(`, `[` and `{`, so generics are free (`Map<string, Map<string, [int]>>` measures 2, not 5) and the worry about generated code was unfounded: `--emit-derived` over all 110 deriving files produces a maximum depth of **7**, shallower than the hand-written library. The field agrees with refusing: gofmt refuses at `maxNestLev = 1e5`, Prettier dies at ~250 with an uncaught `RangeError`, clang-format crashes outright, and only rustfmt passes the input through unformatted. What the review overturned is this entry's claim that bounding the depth "removes the blowup at its source" -- it does not, for a large document; see A27.

- [x] **A19** **A real data race in `atomic_add_double`, and a strict-aliasing violation on the same lines.** `__atomic_load_n` for the CAS seed at `src/native_metrics.c:91`, and hold the bits as `uint64_t` instead of casting `double *` at `:96`/`:103`/`:107`. (I P1-3; S, one fix closes both) TSan reports it twice from eight spawned threads doing `metrics.add`/`observe`/`set` 5,000 times each: the compare-and-swap loop seeds itself with a plain non-atomic load. The build carries no `-fno-strict-aliasing`, so the punning through `double *` is a second, quieter bug in the same three functions. Worth recording why a clean 6,037-line TSan run of the real backend under `ab` load does **not** cover this: line 9 of that log is `server.metrics=false`, so `atomic_add_double` was never called -- F10 landed the module, but the server's own metrics are off by default. **Done 2026-10-08, and one change closes both because they had one cause: the value was a `double` that wanted to be a word.** Every atomic operation reached it by casting `double *` to `unsigned long long *` -- undefined by C11 6.5p7, in a build with no `-fno-strict-aliasing` -- and the CAS loop seeded itself with a plain `memcpy` read of a word other threads' exchanges were writing. `Series.value` is now `Series.value_bits`, an `unsigned long long` holding the double's bits, and the three helpers (`bits_add_double`, `bits_store_double`, `bits_load_double`) take that type, so **no cast between the two pointer types remains anywhere in the file** -- verified by grep, zero left -- and the loop seed is an `__atomic_load_n`. Worth stating the reasoning that does *not* excuse the old code: a torn seed would only have cost the loop one more turn, which is why this never produced a wrong number, but a race is not defined by whether you can observe the damage. Verified by rebuilding the TSan binary against this tree and re-running the review's own `metrics_race.zm`: **2 warnings before, 0 now**, exit 0 rather than 66. Correctness checked separately, because the fix touches an update path: 8 threads x 5,000 increments renders exactly `hits 40000` on both the plain and the TSan build, so nothing is lost. `count` and the histogram buckets were already `__atomic_fetch_add` and needed nothing. New fixture `metrics_concurrent_counter.zm` asserts that total -- a lost update is the one symptom of this class a fixture *can* catch. 888 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **A20** **Allocation failure is unhandled almost everywhere.** One `ziman_xalloc`/`ziman_xrealloc` pair, used throughout. (I P1-4; M) 13 NULL checks across 281 `malloc`/`calloc` sites, and 3 across 81 `realloc` sites. Defensible for a CLI and weak for the server runtime this also is -- the worst case is `src/lsp.c:187`, where `sb_grow` loses the old pointer *and* then dereferences NULL, in the one process a user keeps open all day. Filed `[O]`: the sites were counted and read, not driven to failure. **Done 2026-10-08, and driven to failure this time, which is how the interesting half was found.** `src/xalloc.h` holds four wrappers -- `ziman_xmalloc`, `ziman_xcalloc`, `ziman_xrealloc`, `ziman_xstrdup` -- that report the file and line and exit 1 rather than returning NULL, and **485 call sites across 41 files** now go through them. `sb_grow` is among them, so the `p = realloc(p, n)` that lost the pointer and then wrote through the NULL is gone. **Functions, not macros, for a reason the codebase had already paid for**: the size must be evaluated exactly once, and `zgc.h`'s own comment records what the other shape costs -- a macro that expanded the size twice around a realloc read `node->n` after the realloc had moved `node`, which reported a 32 GB allocation site that did not exist. Evaluating once also lets a zero-size request become one byte, so a legitimate `realloc(p, 0)` returning NULL is not read as failure. **Then the test under `ulimit -v 204800` showed the wrappers were not enough.** The `nogc` build reported cleanly; the default build printed Boehm's own `Out of Memory! Returning NULL!` and **still segfaulted**, in `array_push` at `src/value.c:201`, because a packed array's buffer comes from `zalloc_atomic` -- a macro over `GC_MALLOC_ATOMIC`, which is not named malloc and so was not in the 363 the review counted. `ziman_xcheck` now wraps it in `src/value.h` for every caller, and the five explicit `GC_MALLOC_UNCOLLECTABLE` calls too. Both builds now say `ziman: out of memory: ... failed at src/value.c:197` and exit 1. **Deliberately exempt: `src/regex.c`**, marked `xalloc-exempt:` with its reason -- its buffers are the only ones sized by user input up to `MAX_MATCH_BYTES`, so the only ones that can fail on a healthy machine, and A16 made it report that as an ordinary Ziman runtime error rather than exiting a server. Guard: `tools/check_xalloc.py`, in `make check`, fails any bare allocator or unwrapped collector call, verified by unwrapping one in `gc_heap.c` and watching it fail. **The allocation profile still works**, which was the risk of wrapping everything: the wrappers take the caller's file and line and pass them to `zgc.h`'s counters, so `ziman-allocprofile` still attributes to `ast.c:16` and `interpreter.c:160` rather than to `xalloc.c`. The 14 unit-test targets each link `src/xalloc.c` now. 888 fixtures, all three builds, `make check` green.
- [x] **A21** **Shift through `uint64_t` in `native_bits.c:96`, and let the comment say why.** (I P2-1; S) `check_shift_amount` (`:15-19`) permits exactly 63, so `bits.int_shift_left(1, 63)` shifts into the sign bit of a signed `int64_t` -- undefined by C11 6.5.7p4, and the comment at `:86-92` justifies only the *right* shift's sign extension. **The framing this arrived with was tested and is wrong**: the expression yields -9223372036854775808 identically under `-O2 -fwrapv`, `-O2`, `-O3` and `-O2 -fstrict-overflow`, because GCC defines signed `<<` on bit patterns as a documented extension independent of that flag. So `-fwrapv` is not load-bearing here and there is no flag dependency to record; what remains is portability to a compiler not bound by GCC's extension, plus clearing the single finding a no-`-fwrapv` UBSan sweep reports. Also settled on the way in: a table of 64 mask constants, considered and rejected, because the shift amount is a runtime parameter and `MASKS[i]` is an array index and a bounds check in place of one native shift. **Done 2026-10-08.** `(int64_t)((uint64_t)a << b)`, with the comment saying that bit 63 is the reason, that `-fwrapv` is *not* what made the old form work, and that what did -- GCC documenting signed `<<` as acting on the bit pattern -- is an extension no other compiler owes us. Same instructions, and bit 63 still gives the same `-9223372036854775808` it always did; `int_shift_right`, whose comment already justified its own sign extension, needed nothing. Verified by rebuilding the no-`-fwrapv` UBSan binary against this tree and re-sweeping **all 808 fixtures and examples**: `native_bits.c` is gone from the output and the only reports left are the two nonnull sites A22 is about. No fixture for this one, deliberately -- the values never changed, so there is nothing a fixture can observe; the thing that catches this class is a sanitizer run, which is F14.
- [x] **A22** **12 UBSan reports for `memcpy` with a NULL source and a zero length.** `if (argc > 0)` around the second `memcpy` at `src/interpreter.c:1253` and `:2274`. (I P2-2; S) Benign in practice, undefined by the letter of the standard, and reported across 12 fixtures on **both** the plain and the collector build, so not an artifact of one configuration. The reason to fix it is signal-to-noise: 12 reports bury the next real one. Smallest case: a zero-argument lambda that captures. **Done 2026-10-08: `if (argc > 0)` at both sites, and the sweep is now clean end to end.** The two are the same four lines twice -- `eval_expr`'s inline lambda call and `native_call_closure` -- where a captured lambda's arguments are copied after its captures, and `args` is NULL when it takes none. The first `memcpy` of the pair is fine, since it only runs when `ncaptured > 0`. **With A21, UBSan over all 808 fixtures and examples now reports nothing at all** -- 12 before, 0 after, on a build with `-fwrapv` off so signed overflow is checked too. That zero is worth more than the two fixes: it is the baseline a future sweep can be compared against, where before any new report arrived among twelve known-harmless ones.
- [x] **A23** **A use-after-free window against `on_sigprof` in `frames_grow`.** Assign `t_frames = fresh` before freeing the old block (`src/error.c:183-199`). (I P2-3; S) Not triggered -- the window is one `memset` long and needs `--profile` -- but it sits in the one function whose own comment asserts signal safety, which is what makes it worth the two-line reordering. **Done 2026-10-08, and it was in both paths, not one.** Publishing `t_frames = fresh` before releasing the old block closes the collector path. The `nogc` path had the same window *built into it*, because `realloc` frees the old block before it returns, so `t_frames` pointed at freed memory from the moment it came back until the store -- wider than the collector path's window, not narrower. Both now malloc, copy, zero, publish, then free, which also makes the two paths one piece of code instead of two. The cost is an extra copy on a path that runs O(log n) times per thread (the capacity doubles) and **nothing on the hot path**: `ziman_push_frame` is untouched, so the measured 9.5% reason not to put a signal fence there still stands. Verified: 200 rounds of 20,000-deep recursion under `--profile` -- so the array doubles repeatedly while SIGPROF fires at 1000 Hz on the same thread -- three runs on each build, all clean, ~2,200 samples collected each time. Under valgrind on the `nogc` build, with `--profile` and with the three stack-trace fixtures: **zero invalid-free or use-after-free reports**. Stated plainly, since the task was filed `[O]`: the window was not observed before the fix either, and nothing here demonstrates it -- a `memset`-long window needs the handler to land inside it. What the valgrind runs do show is that the restructuring introduced no new fault. One thing worth carrying to F14: both memory checkers object to the conservative stack scanner and nothing else -- ASan calls `gc_scan.c:32` a stack-buffer-overflow and valgrind calls `gc_lookup.c:11` an uninitialised read, 22 times -- which is what a conservative collector looks like to a checker, and is the baseline a sanitizer target has to be built around.
- [x] **A24** **A data race on a socket handle's descriptor: `release_handle` writes `h->fd = -1` while `require_fd` reads it.** (from F14's new `make tsan`, 2026-10-08; S, plus one decision) `src/native_net.c:77` clears the field from the thread calling `net.close_raw` (`:657`) while another thread reads it in `require_fd` (`:136`) on its way into `net.accept_raw` (`:337`) -- a server socket closed on one thread while another accepts on it, which is how a shutdown works. TSan reports one race and **six fixtures fail on it**: `httpclient_basic`, `httpclient_binary` and `http_server` with that trace, and `shutdown_TERM`, `shutdown_INT` and `http_serve` exiting 66, TSan's error code, on the same path. **Worse than the warning suggests, which is why it is not a P2:** the write is `close(h->fd)` and then `h->fd = -1`, so a reader that loses the race holds a descriptor number that is already closed -- and if any thread opens anything in between, the kernel may hand that same number back, leaving the reader working on an unrelated socket, file or database connection. The field, not the set: `g_live`'s `HandleSet` is already locked for membership. Shape of a fix: take the descriptor with one `__atomic_exchange_n(&h->fd, -1, ...)`, so exactly one thread ever closes it and every later reader sees -1, with `require_fd` reading atomically too. The decision is what `accept`/`read` on a socket another thread has just closed should answer -- today it is whatever `NetError` a bad descriptor produces, and "closed" is probably the honest answer. **Done 2026-10-08, and it was two races, not one.** `make tsan` is now **887 passed, 0 failed, 1 skipped**. The descriptor is read and cleared with the `__atomic` builtins at all seven sites, the clear being an `__atomic_exchange_n`, so exactly one thread ever hands the number to `close(2)` and a reader arriving after the close sees -1. **The decision turned out to be already made**: `errno_result` maps `EBADF` to the `closed` category (`native_net.c:108`), so "closed" is what this module already answers for a dead descriptor -- `io_read`/`io_write` now return that deliberately instead of handing -1 to the kernel and discovering it. **The second race was in the shutdown flag, and TSan was wrong about it**, which is the more interesting half. `g_shutdown_requested` is `volatile sig_atomic_t`, exactly what C11 7.14.1.1p5 guarantees a signal handler may write and ordinary code may read -- correct before this change and correct after. But that guarantee lives in the standard, not in the program text, so a sanitizer cannot see it, and three fixtures failed on a handler doing the one thing its type exists to permit. It now goes through relaxed `__atomic_store_n`/`__atomic_load_n`: still lock-free, still signal-safe, and now visible to the tool -- because a checker that cries wolf on correct code is one nobody runs. Two things found on the way: a *second* reader of the flag (`native_os_shutdown_requested`) bypassed the accessor and read it plainly, and the handler did not save `errno`, which TSan also reported -- a handler runs between any two instructions of the thread it interrupts, so anything it leaves in `errno` is read there as the result of an unrelated call. Both fixed. **What is left is written into `native_net.c`'s header rather than claimed as closed**: atomics remove the data race but not the logical one -- a reader that loaded the number *before* the close still holds it, and if any thread opens anything in between the OS may reuse it, so the read lands on an unrelated socket. Closing that needs the descriptor to outlive its last user (a refcount with a deferred close, which would put an atomic pair around every read and write), and a server that closes from its accepting thread -- what `server.zm` does -- never opens the window. No fixture, because no fixture can see a race: the six that exercise this path already existed and passed throughout, and the thing that catches this class is `make tsan`, which is why F14 came first. **Reviewed 2026-10-08 and the judgement in this entry was wrong on both counts; see A31.** The window is not only reachable, it is **demonstrable on demand** -- 10 runs out of 10 produce ENOTSOCK from a pure-Ziman program, and 6 out of 6 of a second one get `accept(2)` to consume another listener's connection. The reason it is reachable is a detail this entry did not have: `accept_raw` holds the descriptor number across an unbounded series of 250 ms `poll` waits, where every other site loads and syscalls in a few instructions. And the cost argument against a refcount is empty: the path already takes a process-wide mutex per operation, so the atomic pair is strictly cheaper than what is paid today, and a working prototype measured inside the noise. **One sentence here to retract outright**: "No fixture, because no fixture can see a race" -- true of the data race, false of the logical window, which a fixture can pin deterministically under `taskset -c 0`. What this entry got right was the data race itself and the ordering fix; what it got wrong was treating the rest as acceptable on reasoning rather than measurement.
- [x] **A25** **`regex_compile` recurses once per atom, so a long pattern is a SIGSEGV before any limit can fire.** A `MAX_PATTERN_BYTES` check at the top of `regex_compile`, before parsing. (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; S) `parse_alt` builds a chain one node deep per atom and `gen` (`src/regex.c:601`/`:613`) descends the whole left spine **before emitting a single instruction**, so `MAX_INSTRUCTIONS` cannot save it and `MAX_MATCH_BYTES`, being post-compile, is never reached -- the process is already dead. `free_node` has the same shape. **Verified here, not inherited:** a 20,000-character pattern of plain `a` exits **139 at `--stack-mb=1`** and is correctly refused with "pattern is too large" at the default stack, which is exactly why this was never seen; the threshold at 1 MB is about 13,000 characters (5,000 compiles, 13,000 dumps core). Worse, a **spawned worker at `ZIMAN_THREAD_STACK_MB=1` takes the whole process down** -- `main alive` never prints -- and `src/stack.c`'s `compute()` sizes spawned stacks from **1 MB** up by itself, so that is what a server's workers get on a small container, with a 20 KB pattern from a request. This is A16's class in the function next door, and it reopens `src/regex.h`'s "a pattern from a request is safe to compile". 4 KB is ~90x the longest pattern anywhere in this repo (46 characters) and well inside the 1 MB recursion margin; `pcre2_set_max_pattern_length` is the precedent. Worth doing in the same pass, though it fixes nothing on its own: `re->n_groups` is final once `parse_alt` returns, so the budget could be folded into `emit()`'s existing `n_inst >= MAX_INSTRUCTIONS` test as `min(MAX_INSTRUCTIONS, MAX_MATCH_BYTES / (24 * ncap + 36))`, which refuses at the crossing point and removes the bespoke refusal free list A16 added. **Done 2026-10-08, and not with the length cap this task proposed.** A `MAX_PATTERN_BYTES` of 4 KB would have refused patterns that work today -- the review's own survey found a plausible 1,000-keyword alternation at **7 KB**, which compiles fine and would have been rejected. The parser is already iterative (`parse_cat` and `parse_alt` are loops), so the recursion was only ever in `gen` and `free_node`, and bounding *them* refuses nothing: **`gen`'s `N_CAT` case now walks the left spine in a loop** -- collecting the right-hand children, emitting the leftmost leaf, then those children in reverse, which is exactly the order `gen(n->a); gen(n->b)` produced -- so its recursion is bounded by the pattern's *nesting*, which the parser already caps at 200. That is A16's move one function over. `free_node` is iterative too, by rotate-and-free, which needs no stack and no allocation -- the latter mattering because it runs on the out-of-memory path. **Verified:** the 20,000-character pattern that dumped core at `--stack-mb=1` now answers "pattern is too large" at 1 MB and at 8 MB; the spawned worker at `ZIMAN_THREAD_STACK_MB=1` prints `main alive` and exits 0 where it used to take the process down; 200,000 characters is refused cleanly. **And the output is unchanged**, which is the part that needed proving: a differential against a binary built before the change, over 15 hand-picked patterns x 14 subjects plus long concatenations at 1/2/7/64/500/3000 atoms bare, inside a group, inside an alternation and inside a quantifier, is **identical across all 234 lines** of match offsets, capture groups and `find_all` counts. New fixture `regex_long_pattern.zm` at `--stack-mb=1`, the stack size this crashed on and the one `src/stack.c` picks by itself for a spawned thread. 889 fixtures, `make check` green. The `emit()` folding this entry also suggested is left for A26, which is the task that touches those refusal paths.
- [x] **A26** **The regex parse-failure paths leak, and a failed compile is not cached -- so it is per-request and unbounded.** (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; S) **Verified here with valgrind** over three malformed patterns through the real `regex_compile`: **154 bytes definitely lost, 602 indirectly**, the largest single record being 600 bytes from `node()` (`src/regex.c:109`) down `parse_cat`/`parse_alt`. `(unclosed` costs 56 + 560, a duplicate named group 58 + 42, `a{3,1}` 40. The reason it matters more than its size: `src/native_regex.c:35` says **"Only successful compiles are cached"**, so a client sending a *different* malformed pattern per request recompiles and leaks every time -- in the path `src/regex.h` advertises as safe to run on request input. A16 noted this as "A20's territory" and A20 did not reach it, because `src/regex.c` is the one file deliberately exempt from the allocation wrappers (`xalloc-exempt:`, for its own good reasons). So it needs doing by hand: the parse-failure returns need the same free list the budget refusal got, including `group_names` and its entries. **Confirmed and enlarged by the A16 adversarial review, 2026-10-08:** there are **three** adjacent refusal blocks in `regex_compile` and A16 taught only one of them to free `group_names`, so a reader now sees one freeing and two not and will read that as deliberate -- that inconsistency is the review's actual point. The biggest of the three is not the parse failure but **`em.failed`, "pattern is too large"**: verified here with valgrind at **48,008 bytes definitely lost in 1 block plus 34,890 indirectly in 6,000 blocks = 82,898 bytes** for `(?P<nN>a|b)` x 6000 -- the whole `group_names` array and every name string. Byte-identical in `27c5034` and `904ed23`, so pre-existing in all three paths and not introduced by A16. Fix all three together, or say in the source why one is different. **Done 2026-10-09, and the second half needed a different fix from the first.** The three refusal blocks now share one `free_partial_regex`, which frees `group_names` and every name string, so they cannot drift apart again -- that alone took the "pattern is too large" path from **82,898 bytes to zero**, and the budget path stayed clean. It did **not** fix the parse-failure path, which still lost 122 bytes, because those allocations are abandoned *inside the parser*: nodes that were never linked into anything a tree walk could reach, and freeing a tree cannot free what is not in the tree. So the ownership moved -- **`Parser` now keeps every `Node` it makes in a list and frees them all in one place**, reached identically whether the parse succeeded or failed. Three things follow, which is why that was worth doing instead of patching the two sites: the leak *class* is closed rather than the instances, since no path owns a node any more; the duplicate-name copy is freed where it is abandoned; and **`free_node` is retired entirely**, so the rotate-and-free recursion A25 had to flatten no longer exists -- a flattened recursion is still better deleted. **Verified with valgrind on all three paths: 0 definitely lost, against 756 and 82,898 bytes before.** The successful-compile path still reports the `Regex` itself, which nothing frees and which has no `regex_free` -- that is A35, deliberately not this task. 889 fixtures, `make check` green, `make test-regex` clean under ASan+UBSan.
- [x] **A27** **The formatter's cost is bounded on the wrong axis: depth is one factor of a product, and the language server's reply cap fires after the whole string is built.** (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; M) **This corrects A18's own claim** that bounding the depth "removes the blowup at its source" -- true for a small document, false for a large one, because the cost is O(lines x depth) and only depth is capped. **Verified here:** at depth 190, inside the limit, 764 KB of input formats to **45 MB of output at 235 MB of RSS**, scaling linearly in lines (68 KB to 3.9 MB, 256 KB to 15 MB, 764 KB to 45 MB). The review drove a 10 MB document within the limit through the real `textDocument/formatting` and reached **4.78 GB of RSS over 6.17 s** before the 8 MB cap refused it -- because `src/lsp.c` calls `format_source` first (`:1424`) and tests `LSP_MAX_FORMAT_REPLY` against the result afterwards (`:1433`), so the cap bounds what is *sent*, not what is *built*. Amplification is reduced from ~120,000x to ~480x, not removed. Two things to fix together: bound the formatter's *output* as it is produced rather than its input's depth, and give the language server a `didOpen` document-size limit, which it has none of today. Doing the first also makes A18's over-refusal harmless -- a one-line deeply nested expression costs nothing to format (a 150-deep one-liner formats to exactly its 328 input bytes, verified) and is refused today purely because the limit cannot tell the two shapes apart. **Done 2026-10-09, both halves, with the cost bounded where it is produced rather than after the fact.** `FORMAT_MAX_OUTPUT` is 16 MB, checked in `put` -- the one place the formatter appends -- so a pass that would exceed it stops appending and `format_source` refuses with a message naming the ceiling and the reason. 16 MB is about **180x** the largest file in this project or the backend (`lib/http/server.zm`, 89 KB, which formats to exactly its own size). Measured at depth 190, *inside* the nesting limit, which is the shape this is about: 20,000 lines still formats (15 MB out, 80 MB RSS); **60,000 lines is now refused at 70 MB of RSS where it used to produce 45 MB of output at 235 MB**, and 200,000 lines at 131 MB rather than multiple gigabytes. Second half: `LSP_MAX_DOCUMENT` of 4 MB, ~45x the largest real file, applied in a `set_doc_and_analyze` that **both `didOpen` and `didChange` go through**, so the limit cannot apply to one and not the other. Over it the text is still stored -- so hover and formatting answer rather than behaving as though the file were empty -- the analysis is skipped, and **one diagnostic says why**, because a file with no diagnostics otherwise looks like a file with no errors. Verified over the real protocol: a 4.3 MB document gets that diagnostic and costs 74 MB of RSS in 0.09 s, where a *smaller* 2.1 MB one that is analysed costs 207 MB in 0.43 s. And the shape that reached **4.78 GB** through `textDocument/formatting` now comes back refused with the output-ceiling message, total reply **4,682 bytes**. The new ceiling is documented in README beside the nesting limit. **What this does not do, deliberately:** the nesting limit stays, so A18's over-refusal of a one-line deeply nested expression stays with it -- the output ceiling makes that limit redundant for everything *except* failing early and cheaply, and relaxing it would mean changing what F18 just documented for a case nobody writes.
- [x] **A28** **Nothing limits how much *work* a match may do -- only how much memory it may allocate.** (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; M) The 64 MB budget is a poor proxy for cost: a pattern just inside it costs about **9 ms per input byte** -- 1.38 s on a 200-byte subject and 7.17 s on 800 bytes, measured by the review -- which is roughly 2.5 CPU-hours for a 1 MB subject in a single request, because 64 MB of capture buffers means millions of int-copies per character. `search_all` pays it once per match position. PCRE2 has `match_limit`, `depth_limit` and `heap_limit`, each settable per match and in-pattern; RE2 bounds the compiled program instead and defaults to **8 MiB, 8x tighter than this**. Ziman has no equivalent of any of them. The decision this needs first is what to count -- steps, or `n_inst x ncap x len` -- and whether the limit is fixed or settable by the caller, since a caller handling untrusted patterns wants it tighter than one running its own. **Done 2026-10-09. What to count was settled by measuring, and the shape of the limit by getting it wrong once.** The unit is **capture-vector int-copies** -- `ncap` ints per thread parked and per `I_SAVE` -- because that is where the time goes. Measured against a 1 MB subject, real patterns in this repo cost **7 to 34 copies per byte** (`\w+` 7, `(.*)dog` 12, a three-named-group date 28, `(\w+)@(\w+)\.com` 34), while 400 empty groups on 200 bytes costs **647,226 per byte** and 1,000 empty groups **4,030,056** -- four to five orders of magnitude of separation, which is what makes a limit possible at all. **Shaped as a floor plus a rate**, not a flat number: the first position pays a full epsilon closure whatever the subject's length, so a flat cap punishes a short subject for a big pattern, while the per-byte term is what a long subject legitimately spends. 256M copies plus 100,000 per byte leaves ~3,000x headroom over anything real. **The mistake worth recording: the first version put the budget on one `regex_search` call, and `find_all` walked straight through it** -- 201 searches over a 200-byte subject, each comfortably inside a per-call budget, 8e8 copies in total. A limit has to bound the operation the caller asked for, not one of its steps, so the counter is now carried across a scan (`regex_search_budgeted`, with `search_all_raw` keeping one counter for the whole loop). Verified end to end: `find_all` with 200 and 400 capture groups over 200 bytes still works, **1,000 groups is refused** with `regex: this pattern needs too much work to match a subject of 200 bytes`, and the realistic end is untouched -- 60,000 dates and 60,000 emails found by two `find_all` scans over a 2.58 MB subject. Not settable, as PCRE2's are; if that is ever wanted the **rate** is the number to expose, not the floor. Two cases in `tests/unit/regex_test.c`, which also pass under ASan+UBSan. 889 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **A29** **`add_thread`'s comment justifies the thread order with a condition that is necessary but not sufficient, and omits the one that matters.** (from the A16 adversarial review, 2026-10-08; S) The comment says order is preserved because "entries pop LIFO, and `I_SPLIT` pushes `y` before `x`". Both true, and **mark-on-push is also LIFO and also pushes `y` first** -- the order claim actually holds because `vm->mark[p]` is set when an entry is *popped*, not when it is pushed. The review demonstrated the difference rather than arguing it: a mutant that marks on push -- "the natural way to shrink the worklist, and what the `2*n_inst+2` slack invites" -- differs from the real engine on **45,626 of 1,500,958** output lines, smallest detector `c*?|` against `"cb xa"` (real `0 0`, mutant `0 1`, and Python's `re` agrees with the real engine). So the next person optimising this reaches for mark-on-push, the comment does not warn them off, and the 888 fixtures would not necessarily catch it. Name mark-on-pop in the comment, and say why. **Done 2026-10-09.** The comment now gives three conditions rather than two, says which one is easy to lose, and names mark-on-push as the tempting wrong answer with the measurement attached -- including the smallest disagreeing case, `c*?|` against `"cb xa"`, where this engine and Python's `re` both say `0 0` and mark-on-push says `0 1`. It is written as a refusal rather than a description, because the thing that makes it dangerous is that it looks equivalent.
- [x] **A30** **`add_thread`'s worklist is allocated about twice the size its own bound requires.** (from the A16 adversarial review, 2026-10-08; S) The comment reasons "at most `n_inst` entries are processed and each pushes at most two, so `2 * n_inst + 2`" -- which double-counts, because it adds the two pushes without subtracting the pop that preceded them. A processed entry is net **+1**, so `top <= 1 + n_inst` and the highest index ever written is `n_inst + 1`. Verified by an instrumented build over **868,810,166 `add_thread` calls**: `top` peaks at **0.4902** of the allocation, almost exactly half, while the capture scratch peaks at **0.9995** -- so that bound is exactly tight and this one is loose by 2x. Not a bug; the allocation is correct, just larger than needed, and the slack also inflates `needed` in `MAX_MATCH_BYTES` by `(2n + 2) * 16` bytes, which is the one place it costs something real. **Done 2026-10-09: the allocation is `n_inst + 2` and the budget term with it.** Halving a buffer on reasoning alone is not good enough, so the bound was asserted rather than argued: an instrumented scratch build checks `top <= n_inst + 2` and `save_used <= n_inst * ncap` on every loop iteration, and was run under ASan and UBSan over 20 hand-picked patterns x 18 subjects at every start offset, plus concatenations, alternations and `(a?)` runs at 1/2/7/64/500/1000, plus the shape that actually drives the worklist deep -- runs of `a*`, `(a*)` and `(|a)` up to 2,000, whose epsilon closure walks every split in one generation without consuming. **Nothing fired and both sanitizers were clean.** Worth recording which evidence is the strong one: my corpus peaked at **0.200** of the new allocation, so it does not demonstrate tightness; the review's own instrumented run over **868,810,166** `add_thread` calls peaked at 0.4902 of the old `2n + 2`, which is **0.98 of the new `n + 1`** -- that is what shows the bound is nearly tight and never exceeded. The refusal fixture's printed size is unchanged at 1650 MB, since the term removed is ~0.4 MB of it. 889 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **A31** **The descriptor-reuse window A24 documented is demonstrable, and the reason given for leaving it open does not survive measurement.** A reference count on the handle, with `close(2)` deferred to the last release and the acquire folded into `handleset_has` under `g_live`'s own lock. (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; M) **Demonstrated, and reproduced here independently:** a pure-Ziman program with a thread in `net.accept`, a close from main, and 40 regular files opened to reclaim the number gives `network error: Socket operation on non-socket` -- **ENOTSOCK, 10 runs out of 10** under `taskset -c 0`, which a listener cannot produce: `accept(2)` ran on a descriptor that had become a file. A second program, which reopens the number as an unrelated listener, gets `accept(2)` to **succeed and consume a connection addressed to the other listener, 6 runs out of 6**. **What A24 missed is an asymmetry in one function:** `native_net_accept_raw` (`src/native_net.c:371`) loads the descriptor *once*, before its `for(;;)` loop, and then uses that local for every `poll(&pfd, 1, 250)` and for the final `accept(fd)` -- so the number is held across an unbounded series of 250 ms waits, not the few instructions the header comment describes. Every other site loads and syscalls within a handful of instructions; this one does not, and that is what makes the window reachable in practice. **The cost argument was wrong in shape, not just in size:** `require_handle` already goes through `handleset_has`, which takes a **single process-wide `pthread_mutex_lock`** (`src/handles.c:95`) on every `read`/`write`/`accept`, so an atomic pair is strictly smaller than what is already paid, and the increment can live inside that existing critical section. Measured end to end on a working prototype built outside the repo: **+2.2% at c=8 and -0.5% at c=16**, against a round-to-round spread of +/-7% -- unmeasurable -- with 888 fixtures passing and both reproductions ceasing to reproduce. The design point that matters: the increment must happen **under `g_live`'s lock**, not after a plain `handleset_has`, or a close can drop the last reference between the test and the increment. Known remaining work from the prototype: `ziman_error` longjmps out of `accept_raw`'s inner path and skips the release, leaking a reference (see A33); `accept` on a shut-down listener yields EINVAL and should map to the `closed` category; and it needs the three sanitizer targets and a `nogc` run. Add the reproduction as a fixture, skippable where `taskset` is absent. **Done 2026-10-09, with A33 folded in because it is the same code.** `handleset_has_then` (`src/handles.h`) is the one new primitive: `handleset_has` with a callback run **while the lock is still held**, so a caller takes its reference with no window between the check and the acquire -- a concurrent close has to win the whole lock. `NetHandle` gained `refs` and `closing`; `handle_retire` takes the handle out of the live set, after which no new reference can be taken, and closes it either immediately or from the last `handle_unuse`. **What makes this simple is A24 and A32**: `release_handle` is already idempotent -- the descriptor leaves through an atomic exchange and the `SSL` under `tls_mu` with a NULL check -- so both sides of the race may call it and whichever arrives second does nothing. **Scope, stated rather than implied:** a reference is held wherever a descriptor is held across a *wait* -- `accept_raw`'s poll loop, `recv`, `send`, and the TLS handshake, where `SSL_set_fd` hands the number to OpenSSL -- and the instant-syscall sites (`listener_port`, `peer_address`, `set_timeout`) take one too, since they were one line each. `shutdown` still happens before retiring, in that order deliberately: it wakes a blocked reader and, unlike `close`, does not free the number. **Both reproductions now answer honestly:** `ENOTSOCK` 10 runs out of 10 becomes `connection closed: the listener was closed` 10 out of 10, and the connection theft -- `accept(2)` succeeding on a recycled number, 6 out of 6 -- becomes `closed` 6 out of 6. A failed `accept` consults this handle's own `closing` flag, so a shutdown reports `closed` rather than the EINVAL the kernel gives, while a genuine EINVAL elsewhere still reports as itself. **The opposite failure was tested for too**, since a leaked reference means the descriptor never closes: 600 listen/connect/accept/close cycles hold at 4 open descriptors on both builds. **Cost, measured here rather than inherited:** GET /health at c=16 against a binary built from the previous commit, three interleaved rounds -- 3311/3032/3185 before against 3308/3131/3006 after, a mean difference of **-0.9% inside a +/-4.4% round-to-round spread**, so unmeasurable, exactly as the review found. **One real bug I introduced and TSan caught**: `ref_inc` incremented plainly under the set lock while `handle_unuse` decremented atomically without it -- the lock orders the increment against *removal*, not against a decrement -- which failed four fixtures until the increment became atomic too. 889 fixtures, `make check`, `make test-tls`, `make asan` and `make tsan` all green.
- [x] **A32** **A TLS socket's `SSL` object is read plainly while another thread frees it -- a use-after-free on heap, which is worse than the recycled integer A24 was about, and which A24's fix does not touch.** (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; S-M) `release_handle` does `SSL_free(h->ssl); h->ssl = NULL;` (`src/native_net.c:96-98`) while `io_read`/`io_write` read `h->ssl` plainly at `:192`, `:205`, `:207`, `:219` and `:222`. **Two reasons it stayed invisible.** `test-tls` is not in `make check` (`Makefile:45`), so **no TLS fixture has ever run under a sanitizer** and `make tsan` being green says nothing about this path. And A24's own guard does not reach it: `SSL_set_fd` copies the descriptor number into OpenSSL's BIO, so every later `SSL_read`/`SSL_write` uses the number captured at `start_tls` time and never re-reads `h->fd` -- `io_read`'s `if (fd < 0)` check is not even consulted on the TLS path. A31's refcount fixes this by construction, which is one of the arguments for choosing it; `h->ssl` additionally wants to be set-once or atomic to close the field race itself. Worth doing together with A31 and worth testing deliberately, since getting `test-tls` under a sanitizer is a prerequisite for believing any of it. **Done 2026-10-08, and demonstrated before and after rather than argued.** The handle already had a per-operation TLS mutex, so the fix is to tear the `SSL` down under it: `release_handle` takes `tls_mu`, clears `h->ssl`, and calls `SSL_shutdown` and `SSL_free` while still holding it -- so a thread inside `SSL_read` finishes first, and one arriving afterwards finds NULL and is told the socket is closed. `io_read` and `io_write` now read the pointer **once, under the lock**, into a local and use that throughout, because the field can be cleared the instant the lock is released. **The plain-socket path keeps its cost**, which is why this is not simply "lock everything": a new set-once `h->tls` flag, released on install and never cleared, decides TLS-or-not with an atomic load, so an ordinary socket read still takes no mutex -- A34 is about what the one lock already on that path costs, and this adds none. The two `SSL_shutdown` callers that read `h->ssl` plainly to decide whether to send a close_notify are gone: `release_handle` does it, under the lock. `start_tls`'s "already using TLS" check reads the flag, not the pointer, which is also the better answer for a connection whose TLS has since been closed. **The evidence:** the project's TLS tests never make this interleaving -- verified, the pre-fix build runs the whole TLS suite under TSan with **0 warnings** -- so a test had to be written for it. `tests/tls/tls_close_while_reading.zm` puts one thread inside a TLS read and closes that connection from another. On the pre-A32 build under TSan it produces **3 data races in 3 runs out of 3**, naming `release_handle` and a racing `free`, and the suite comes out **4 passed / 1 failed**; on the fixed build, **0 warnings and 5 passed**, five runs out of five. 889 fixtures, `make check` green, `make test-tls` 5 passed. Still open and worth its own task: `test-tls` is not in `make check` and not in the sanitizer targets, which is the reason this class was invisible -- the test now exists but nothing runs it under TSan automatically.
- [x] **A33** **`accept_raw` looks the handle up a second time after `accept(2)` has already succeeded, and leaks the accepted descriptor when that lookup fails.** (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; S) `src/native_net.c:385` re-reads the handle only to fetch `server_ctx`, after `c = accept(fd, ...)` has returned a real descriptor. If the listener was closed in between, `require_handle` raises `net.accept: invalid or closed handle` and **`c` is never registered and never closed** -- verified, it is the failure mode the A31 reproduction lands in 6 times out of 6. The fix is to read `server_ctx` through the reference the function already holds rather than looking it up again, which removes the leak and one of the two windows at once. **Done 2026-10-09 as part of A31**, which is what gave the function a reference to read through. The second lookup is gone, so the path that leaked an accepted descriptor no longer exists -- and with it the only place in this file where `ziman_error` could longjmp while a reference was held, which is what the A31 review recorded as its prototype's known defect.
- [x] **A34** **Every socket operation takes one process-wide mutex, and it is the only thing on that path that gets *worse* as threads are added.** (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; M) `require_handle` -> `handleset_has` locks a single `HandleSet.mu` shared by every socket in the program (`src/handles.c:93-105`) and then probes an open-addressed table. Measured per simulated socket operation on a 16-core machine: **21.6 ns at one thread, 90.2 at two, 102.9 at four, 166.6 at eight, 248.0 at sixteen** -- against **1.7 ns at sixteen threads** for the atomic load of `h->fd` with the mutex removed. That is the F7/A19 shape exactly: a lock per operation on a hot path, making throughput fall as concurrency rises, and it is already in the tree rather than being proposed. It came out of measuring A31's supposed cost and is the larger finding of the two. The table exists so a Ziman program cannot pass an arbitrary integer to `close(2)`, which is worth keeping -- so the question is how to keep that property without a global lock: a per-handle lock, a sharded set, or a generation counter in the handle value itself. **Done 2026-10-09 by sharding -- and the end-to-end claim in this entry does not survive measurement, which is worth recording as carefully as the fix.** `HandleSet` is now 64 independent shards, each with its own mutex and table, chosen from the *top* bits of the hash because the slot index uses the bottom ones (sharing bits would make shards correlate with table positions and fill unevenly). The API is unchanged; nothing outside `handles.c` sees a shard. **The microbenchmark, nanoseconds per `handleset_has`, best of three, 256 handles:** one mutex 13.5 / 86.0 / 158.4 at 1 / 4 / 16 threads against 12.9 / 15.3 / 18.6 sharded -- **8.5x at sixteen threads, and nearly flat**, where before it was the only thing on the socket path that got worse as threads were added. The constant was measured, not guessed: 4 shards only halves the problem, 16 gets to 61.8 ns, 64 gets to 19.6, and 256 reaches 13.5 for four times the memory (a shard is a mutex and four words, so 64 is ~2.5 KB per set against 10 KB, paid by every program whether or not it opens a socket).
