One thread is unchanged at every count, so this costs nothing to a program that never contends. **What is not true: this is not a throughput win for the server, and this entry called it "the bigger throughput finding" too confidently.** Five interleaved `ab` rounds on GET /health at c=16, against a binary from the previous commit: means of 2683 and 2691 requests/s, a **+0.3% difference inside spreads of 9.2% and 5.8%** -- no measurable change. The arithmetic says why, and it should have been done first: at ~2700 rps over 16 threads a request takes about 6 ms, so a few hundred nanoseconds of lock is ~0.005% of it. The lock was a real scaling defect in the operation and never the server's bottleneck at this rate; it would start to matter at a much higher request rate, or for a workload doing many socket operations per request. **Also not claimed: the lock is reduced, not removed.** It still grows from 12.9 to 18.6 ns across 16 threads. Removing it means a generation counter inside the handle value so validation is an atomic load with no table at all -- a much larger change, recorded in `handles.h` as the real answer. Verified: `make test-handles` (including its finalizer-deadlock scenario), 889 fixtures, `make check`, `make test-tls`, `make asan` and `make tsan` all green, and the A31 reproductions and descriptor-leak check unaffected.
- [x] **A35** **The compiled-pattern cache never frees what it evicts, which is nothing under the collector and an unbounded leak under `nogc`.** (found while fixing A25, 2026-10-08; S) `cache_store` (`src/native_regex.c:58`) overwrites `slot->pattern` and `slot->handle` without releasing either, and **there is no `regex_free` in the API at all** (`src/regex.h`), so a compiled program has no way to be released by anyone. Under the collector build that is harmless -- the evicted `Regex` becomes unreachable and is collected -- but `zgc.h` is not force-included into a `nogc` build, so there everything is libc memory that nothing ever frees. **Measured:** 2,000 distinct patterns of ~2,000 atoms each, of which the 256-entry cache drops 1,744, cost **1,197,568 kB under `ziman-nogc` against 49,308 kB under `ziman`** -- 24x, and unbounded in the number of distinct patterns, where the number comes from a request in the threat model `src/regex.h` invites. This is A17's class with the builds the other way round: there the collector build leaked because a libc buffer was released through a no-op `free`; here the `nogc` build leaks because collector-era code never releases anything. `tools/check_libc_free.py` cannot see it, since the allocation is the project's own `calloc` rather than one of the libc functions it watches. Needs a `regex_free` and an eviction that calls it -- and then a second look at whether a cached handle can still be in use by another thread when its slot is reused, which is why this is not simply a missing `free`. **Done 2026-10-09 -- and this entry's own framing was wrong, which is the finding.** The second look settles it: **an eviction cannot free the compiled pattern, in either build.** A handle is handed to Ziman as an integer and `cache_lookup` hands the same one to every caller, so a program can still be holding a pattern whose slot has been reused; under `nogc` nothing can tell, and under the default build the `free` would be the no-op anyway because the `Regex` is collector memory, reclaimed on its own once nothing names it. So the 1.14 GB this entry called "an unbounded leak under nogc" is **not a cache defect at all** -- it is that build's documented design, in which nothing the interpreter allocates is ever freed (`DESIGN.md`'s "Memory reclamation"), exactly as it would be for 2,000 distinct arrays. Calling it A17's class in reverse was my mistake: A17 was a libc block the collector *could not* reclaim, which is a real defect; this is a collector block it reclaims perfectly well. **What was genuinely fixable has been fixed, and it is small:** the evicted entry's pattern *text* is owned by nobody but the table -- `cache_lookup` only `memcmp`s it -- so it is now freed, which is worth **3.6 MB of that 1.14 GB** (1,197,568 kB to 1,193,968 kB; the collector build is unchanged at ~49 MB). Honest accounting rather than a headline. **`regex_free` now exists**, which was the real API gap: a C caller had no way to release a compiled pattern, and `tests/unit/regex_test.c` could not be leak-checked. It frees its five patterns now and is **clean at 0 errors**, so it has joined `make valgrind`'s leak-checked set -- 11 passed, 0 failed -- which is the second half of F24 that was waiting on this. 889 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **A36** **`test-tls` is in neither `make check` nor the sanitizer targets, which is why A32 was invisible.** (from A32's work, 2026-10-08; S) `Makefile:45`'s `check` target does not include `test-tls`, and `make asan`/`ubsan`/`tsan` run `tests/run_tests.sh` only -- so **no TLS code path in this project has ever been exercised under a sanitizer** except by hand. That is exactly how a use-after-free on an `SSL` object survived A24, which was a review specifically about races on this file. A32 added `tests/tls/tls_close_while_reading.zm`, which reports 3 TSan races on the pre-A32 build and none after, but nothing runs it under TSan automatically, so the next regression of the same shape is equally invisible. The reason for the exclusion is real -- `tests/run_tls_tests.sh` needs `openssl` on PATH, generates certificates into `tests/tls/.certs` and starts two `openssl s_server` processes -- so this needs a decision rather than a one-line edit: make the sanitizer targets run it when `openssl` is present and say so when it is not, or add a separate `make tsan-tls`. Note the practical trap found while writing that test: the harness deletes `tests/tls/.certs` on exit, so a TLS test cannot be run directly, only through the script. **Done 2026-10-09, with the decision this asked for: run it when it can run, and say so when it cannot.** A `tls-if-available` target is in `make check`, and each of `make asan`/`ubsan`/`tsan` runs the TLS suite after its fixture sweep with that sanitizer's own options. Both branches were tested: with `openssl` on PATH `make check` is now 889 + 889 + **5**, and with it hidden the target prints `skipped -- openssl is not on PATH, so the TLS suite cannot run` and succeeds, so a machine without it still gets a green `check` and knows what was not run -- which is the difference between this and the silence that hid A32. **`make tsan` now covers the TLS path: 888 passed / 1 skipped on the fixtures, then 5 passed on TLS** -- so `tls_close_while_reading.zm`, which reports three races on the pre-A32 build, is from now on run under TSan by a standing target rather than by hand. The ASan and UBSan builds pass it too. A separate `make tsan-tls` was not needed in the end; guarding the existing targets was enough.
## B. Backend bugs and security (P0)

- [x] **B1** `Instant` parsing: accept 0-9 fractional digits (`.000Z` from the web frontend returns 400 today); add fixtures for 0, 3, 6 and 9 digits; improve the message. (D B1; S). `localtime/localdatetime/instant_from_string` take 1 to 9 fraction digits (`.5`, `.000`, `.123456`); 0 or over 9 digits gets a clear message; fixture `time_instant_fraction_digits`, backend checks in `resources_test.py`.
- [x] **B2** Date range: a year near 9999 stores `10000-..` that cannot be read back, which permanently breaks that user's list and DELETE. Make `localdate_to_string` and `_from_string` symmetric and validate the range. (D B2; S). Chose symmetry: a year outside 0-9999 is written with a sign and read back (`+10000-03-07`, `-0044-03-15`, 4 to 9 digits) by every date, date-time and instant format; fixture `time_year_range_roundtrip`, backend checks in `resources_test.py`.
- [x] **B3** Audit-log forgery through a newline in the login email: escape control characters in `log.write`, and stop rebuilding the audit trail from free text (use a table or JSON lines). (D S1; M). `log.write` writes line breaks as `\n`/`\r`; the audit reader takes the event from the first word of the message only and never looks for events inside user text. Kept the log as the audit store (no table): with both changes user text can neither end a line nor change its event. Fixture `log_line_breaks`, forgery checks in `audit_test.py`.
- [x] **B4** Remove the public default JWT secret: fail startup, or refuse the known constant. (D S2; S). `application.properties` has `app.jwt.secret=${JWT_SECRET}` with no default; `config.zm` also refuses the old public placeholder. Tests, the curly runner and READMEs pass or document `JWT_SECRET`.
- [x] **B5** CORS: answer preflight requests and add `Access-Control-Allow-*` headers, configurable like Spring's `app.cors.allowed-origins`. (D B3; S). Done in the backend (`routes.route`, `app.cors.allowed-origins`, default `http://localhost:5173`, env `APP_CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`), not in the library; `tests/cors_test.py`. Security headers are left to E7.
- [x] **B6** Password-change endpoint, and revoke tokens on reset (`PASSWORD_RESET` can never be cleared today). (D B10; S-M). `PUT /api/users/me/password` as in Spring (current password, min 8, LOCAL only, `PASSWORD_RESET` gate 403, `NEEDS_LOGIN`/`ACTIVE` after); beyond Spring: `users.token_version` (V023) in a `ver` claim, bumped by a change or reset, so old tokens are refused. `tests/password_test.py`.


- [x] **B7** **PostgreSQL cannot be migrated from scratch, and nothing in CI would notice.** Fix the foreign-key types, then add a `--postgres` job. (D2 item 1, D2 B1; S + 1 CI job) `V028`/`V029` declare `TEXT` foreign keys to columns `postgres/V025__native_uuid_keys.sql` already made `uuid`; Postgres refuses, nothing is created, and 17 of 19 suites fail -- while the backend README claims 'Both pass'. It regressed silently across two commits because nothing runs `--postgres`. The missing CI job is the real finding; the type fix is an hour. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (60e0788). Confirmed statically first -- V025 converts users.id, medications.id and patients.id to uuid, V028/V029 declare TEXT foreign keys to all three -- then fixed per migrate.zm's own rule that a version lives in the shared directory or in one engine's, never both: the SQLite files move unchanged into sqlite/, and postgres/V028 and postgres/V029 are the same tables with uuid keys (the new tables' own ids too, matching the 22 tables V025 already converted; the added medications columns and all timestamps stay TEXT on both, since V026 normalises timestamps as text rather than converting them). Postgres now passes 19 of 19 suites and SQLite still does. The CI half needed reinterpreting: **there is no CI in either repo**, so the equivalent that closes the gap is to stop Postgres being the thing you must remember -- run-all.sh now runs both engines by default when docker or podman is present, takes --sqlite or --postgres to pick one, and when no runtime is available runs SQLite alone and says out loud that Postgres was not tested. A flag nobody passes is not a test, which is why this survived two commits. The README's description of the runner is updated with it, including why both is the default.
- [x] **B8** **Bound concurrent password hashing and rate-limit `/api/auth/*`.** Also stop registration disclosing which emails exist. (D2 item 5, D2 S2; S) 64 unauthenticated registrations move RSS from 66MB to 1.09GB permanently (Argon2id with no concurrency cap), and there is no rate limiting anywhere. The email-existence leak undoes the login hardening done deliberately elsewhere. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (9723dfc) for the hashing cap, with the other two parts split out below because each turned out to be a decision rather than a fix, and with the report's stated cause corrected. **The cap works and does not fix the memory.** Every hash and verify now goes through `common.hash_password`/`verify_password`, holding a token from a channel semaphore (`app.security.max-concurrent-hashes`, default 4) and returning it with `defer`; instrumenting the server confirmed 16 concurrent registrations entered the hash 16 times with a peak of 1 inside when the cap was 1. RSS still went 61 -> 347 MB. The growth is glibc giving each thread its own malloc arena and keeping the 19 MiB Argon2id buffer in it: 16 sequential registrations grow 20 MB, 16 concurrent grow 286 MB, and the same 16 concurrent under MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=1 grow 40 MB. It plateaus (347/367/367 MB over three rounds), so the ceiling is (threads that have hashed) x 19 MiB -- exactly the 1.09 GB the review saw at max-connections 64. See B9. The key is `max-concurrent-hashes`, not `...-password-hashes`, because properties.zm masks any key containing "password" and the first name printed as **** in the startup report.
- [x] **B9** **The server's resident memory ceiling is (threads that have hashed) x 19 MiB**, because glibc gives each thread its own malloc arena and keeps Argon2id's buffer in it. (from B8, measured 2026-10-06; S to change, M to be confident) `mallopt(M_ARENA_MAX, n)` early in `src/main.c` would bound it -- 16 concurrent registrations go from 286 MB of growth to 40 MB at `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=1` and 59 MB at 2 -- but that is a global policy change for every Ziman program and arena count trades directly against allocation throughput under concurrency, which F7 and both 2026-10-05 reviews already call this project's worst problem. So it wants measuring against F7's benchmarks before it lands, not a one-line commit. Cheaper interim levers that need no code: lower `server.max-connections`, or set `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX` in the service's environment. The capping of concurrent hashes (B8) is in place and is not the lever -- its own comments say so, because I believed it was until I measured. **Measured 2026-10-07 and done: the trade-off this was filed on does not exist.** `server.serve` now calls `mallopt(M_ARENA_MAX, 1)` once, unless `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX` is already set, in which case the operator has decided. Placed in `serve` rather than `main.c` so only a program that actually serves adopts it -- which was the whole objection to the one-line commit. **Memory**, 48 registrations at 16 concurrent, three interleaved rounds: uncapped 307 MB growth (307.4/307.0/307.3, remarkably stable), capped from `serve` **136 MB**, `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=1` in the environment 98 MB, `=4` 212 MB. The code default cannot reach the environment's floor because glibc reads its variable at the process's first allocation while `mallopt` only bounds arenas created after it runs, and three threads are alive before `serve` -- so the env var stays documented as the way to go further, and the override demonstrably wins. **Throughput**, paired and interleaved: authenticated read at c=16 1656 -> 1593 rps (-3.8%), c=32 1514 -> 1539 (+1.7%), c=64 1402 -> **1499 (+7%)**, registrations 79.9 -> 83.4/s, and an allocation-heavy eight-thread program with no server in it 2322 -> 2295 ms. **Why there is no cost**, which is the part worth keeping: Ziman's own allocation never touches glibc's arenas -- `zgc.h` routes malloc through Boehm and Boehm takes memory from mmap -- so what the cap constrains is what third-party C libraries allocate, which is exactly OpenSSL's retained Argon2id buffer. F7's work (allocation calls 9.96M -> 2.57M the same day) is likely why the c=64 figure now favours the cap. Limits: one machine, one glibc, and the reasoning applies to the collector build the server ships -- a `nogc` build does use real malloc.
- [x] **B10** **Rate-limit `/api/auth/*`** -- which needs the client address, and `lib/http/server.zm` deliberately does not expose it. (from B8, D2 item 5/S2; M) The library's own comment is the position to argue with first: "It cannot tell one client from another, so a determined flood from many addresses is a job for a firewall or the proxy in front (see the roadmap)." So this is two decisions, not one: whether the HTTP server should hand a handler the peer address (and `X-Forwarded-For` when behind a proxy, with the trust question that opens), and only then what the application does with it. Per-account lockout already exists for login and MFA (V027), and it throttles the email tried whether or not the account is real, so the gap is specifically unauthenticated volume -- registration above all, since that is the endpoint that does 19 MiB of work for an anonymous caller. Until then B8's cap bounds how much of that work runs at once, and B9 bounds what it costs in memory. **Done 2026-10-07, in three layers (ziman 970b544 + 6fa7e39, backend a394029).** First, the task's premise: the comment it names as the position to argue with is about the connection *timeouts* not telling clients apart, which is true and unrelated. The real gap was lower -- `net` had `getsockname` and **no** `getpeername`, so a peer address was unreachable from Ziman, not merely withheld from handlers. (1) `net.peer_address` and `Request.peer`, filled in by `read_request` and `None` on a hand-built request. **A field, not a synthetic header**: an injected `X-Ziman-Peer` would have broken nothing and cost no API change, but a client could send that name, so it would be safe only while the parser stripped it -- the exact subtlety a peer address exists to avoid. Cost 13 literals; two with `if ... { } else { }` inside defeated brace-matched editing and the suite caught it. (2) `server.rate_allow` in C, because Ziman has no cross-thread mutable state so an application *cannot* write it; fixed window, 4096 keys, documented worst cases (twice the limit across a window boundary; a full table hands a legitimate caller a fresh allowance, chosen over refusing or allowing everyone). Plus `rate_allow_at` taking the clock like `jwt.verify_at`, and 429 which the library lacked. `server.forwarded_for(req, trusted_hops)` counts proxies **you own** from the right, never read by default, no auto-detection. (3) The backend throttles `/api/auth/*` in `routes.route`. **The default was measured**: at 20/minute three of its own suites failed (the lockout test makes eleven wrong passwords on purpose), which models several users behind one NAT -- 20 was not conservative, it was broken; 100 passes all twenty suites and still refuses a flood. New `throttle_test.py` checks that a spoofed `X-Forwarded-For` buys nothing at 0 hops and that distinct forwarded addresses are distinct callers at 1.
- [x] **B11** **Decide whether registration may keep disclosing which emails exist.** (from B8, D2; S once decided) `service/auth.zm`'s `register` answers `Email already in use`, which lets anyone enumerate accounts. It is also byte-for-byte what the reference does -- `health-record-backend-spring`'s `AuthService.java:39-41` performs the same `existsByEmail` check and throws the same message -- and API equivalence with that backend is a stated goal of this project, with several open tasks (E20) about closing divergences. So the two goals conflict here and the resolution is a choice, not a fix: keep parity and accept the disclosure, change both backends together, or diverge deliberately and write down why. Note the login path was hardened against exactly this (the lockout throttles the email tried whether or not it exists), which is what makes registration the odd one out. B10 would reduce how fast an enumeration can run either way. **Decided 2026-10-07: change both backends together, done (health-record 36aa350).** Registration answers the same thing whether or not the email is taken, and returns **no token**; a client logs in afterwards. **The message was never the disclosure** -- a token for a new email and an error for a taken one is a perfect oracle whatever the text says -- which is why a generic message would have achieved nothing. Three things match, not just the body: the password is hashed in both branches (a duplicate used to return *before* the Argon2id work and so answered faster; now 28.8ms against 29.8ms), the response is built from the request so it cannot leak the stored account's name, and the status is the same. **Stated rather than overclaimed: enumeration is dearer, not impossible.** Register-then-login still distinguishes, because a new account can be signed into and an existing one cannot with the wrong password -- that is two requests against B10's throttle instead of one, and leaves AUTH_REGISTER_DUPLICATE in the audit log. Closing it entirely needs an emailed verification link, and **neither backend has an email channel by design** (this product resets passwords through an administrator), which is also where someone who forgot they have an account now ends up. Four codebases: both backends, plus the web and Android clients that would otherwise break. Tests that asserted the old behaviour now assert the new property -- `auth_test` that a duplicate is *indistinguishable*, `AuthServiceTest` that nothing is saved and no token is minted at all, `AuthIT` that it is 200 with no token. Verified: ziman 20 suites / 978 checks, Spring 759 tests, web `tsc` clean, Android `compileDebugKotlin` clean.

## C. Ziman language ergonomics (P1)

- [x] **C1** Nested `?` by parser-level hoisting into temporaries (`ok(parse_int(s)? + 1)`), preserving evaluation order and short-circuit rules. (L P1-1, D; M). Decided with the "`?` is easy to miss" concern: the postfix `?` stays statement-level; the new prefix `try expr` nests (parser hoists it and what runs before it into `val $tN` temporaries, order of effects kept). Fixtures `try_prefix_*`. Afterwards the postfix `?` was removed altogether: `try` is the only spelling (about 570 uses migrated).
- [x] **C2** Error conversion in `?` without traits, for example `expr? as ServiceError.Storage`, or a `map_err`; removes the 76 or 158 hand-written wrapping blocks in the backend. (L P1-2, D item 9; M). Done as `#[from]` on an enum variant (`try` converts the error); the backend marks `Storage(DbError)` and `Validation(ValidationError)` and drops about 160 wrapping blocks. `map_err` and constructors as function values remain (E2). Fixtures `error_from_*`.
- [x] **C3** Infer generic type arguments from arguments (591 explicit generic calls in the backend). (L P1-3; M-L). Part 1 done: infer from plain arguments and named function references (`identity(5)`); part 2 (an unannotated lambda argument) still to do.
- [x] **C4** Bare `Some(x)` and `None` where the expected type is known. (L P1-4; S-M). Same four positions ok/err/channel_new get (val/var declared type, struct field, with override, return); not in a call argument, matching them. Fixtures `option_bare_*`. **Superseded in part by C10 on 2026-10-06**: the "not in a call argument" half is reversed, and all four forms are accepted there together, so the consistency this aimed at is kept while the cliff it created is gone. `tests/fixtures/option_bare_some_call_argument_rejected.*` is deleted with it -- its whole purpose was pinning that restriction.
- [x] **C5** `==` and `contains` on arrays of any `Eq` element type, and `==` on `Json`. (L P1-5; S). Also relaxed `Hash` the same way (was already meant to work per docs, code did not); added cycle detection (a "currently checking" Type stack) so a self-referential type (`Json`, a recursive user enum) resolves correctly instead of hitting the old depth-64 guard. Fixed a stale "Hash is scalar-only" doc line along the way.
- [x] **C6** Must-use `Result` statements (type error or lint error). (L P1-8; S). Chose a type error, matching `defer`'s own already-documented exemption; also made `_` a real discard (never bound, never conflicts, unusable) so there is an explicit way to say a Result is ignored on purpose. Fixed 17 real sites across lib/ and test fixtures found via an audit run over the whole test suite. Fixtures `must_use_result_*`, `underscore_*`.
- [x] **C7** Better errors: source excerpt and caret, always the file name, stack frames, sorted by position, no cascades. (L P1-12, D item 15; M). Done: source excerpt/caret, always-named file, sorted by position, 2 of several known cascades closed, and now a runtime stack trace on every uncaught fatal error (a per-thread call-frame stack in call_function, read by ziman_error).


- [x] **C8** **A fully annotated lambda contributes nothing to type-argument inference** (`src/typecheck.c:1251` rejects every `EXPR_LAMBDA`). (L2 P1-2; S) `collections.map` needs its type arguments spelled out where `filter` does not, and the same call with a named function infers fine. The docs already promise that only *unannotated* lambdas are excluded, so this is a doc/behaviour mismatch too. L2 calls it the best ergonomic return per line of C in its report. Done 2026-10-06: `expr_needs_expected_type` returned 1 for every `EXPR_LAMBDA`, which is right for an unannotated one -- it takes its parameter types *from* the expected type, so it has nothing to contribute -- and wrong for an annotated one, whose type is already known and is exactly as informative as the named function that always inferred. Now gated on a new `lambda_is_fully_annotated`: no parameter with `type_omitted` and `return_omitted` clear. Safe to check early because `check_lambda_expecting` already memoises on `lambda_checked`, so the ordinary argument loop reuses the result instead of checking the body twice. Verified that a partially annotated lambda (typed parameters, no `->`) still relies on the expected type as before, that explicit type arguments still work, and that inferring U to something other than the element type works. SYNTAX.md's claim was narrower than the behaviour ('a lambda's result type isn't inferred') and now states the actual rule. tests/fixtures/lambda_annotated_infers.zm; the reference backend still type-checks.
- [x] **C9** **A function-typed struct field cannot be called, and the diagnostic recommends something that does not exist.** Fix the message first, then allow `a.f(args)` when `a` is a local of a struct type with such a field. (L2 P1-3, D2 item 10; S then M) The message sends you to `run(o, ...)`, which is not a real function. The workaround appears 11x in the reference backend (all in `crud.zm`, with a 4-line comment explaining it) and 4x in `lib/http/server.zm` -- on what D2 calls the codebase's main extension mechanism. Diagnostic done 2026-10-06; allowing the call is split out as C15, which is the sequencing this task already described ("S then M"). The old message offered one repair, `run(h, ...)`, which is right when `run` is a free function taking `h` and impossible when `run` is a field of `h` holding a function -- the case that produced the report. It now names both forms, including `val run = h.run` then `run(...)`, and leaves the choice to the reader. It has to: the message is raised in loader.c during module resolution, which runs before type checking and so does not know what `h` is or whether it has fields, and guessing is exactly how the advice came to be impossible. (The hint buffer went from 160 to 320 bytes; with both forms spelled out, ordinary identifiers truncated.) tests/fixtures/function_field_call_diagnostic.zm.
- [x] **C10** **Accept bare `Some(x)`/`None` in a call argument.** (L2 P1-4; S) `f(4)` works and `f(Some(4))` does not, so the explicit spelling is the rejected one. Done 2026-10-06, and wider than reported: `ok(...)`, `err(...)` and `channel_new()` were refused in a call argument too, so all four are fixed together. `check_value_against_param_type` now tries the same three helpers `check_expr_expecting` uses, in the same order, before falling back to `check_expr` -- a parameter's declared type is an expected type like any other, and a call argument was the single context offering one that did not use it. Worth knowing why it was that way: C4 restricted bare Some/None to four positions deliberately, "not in a call argument, matching them", i.e. for consistency with the other three forms -- so this keeps that consistency and extends all four rather than making Option a special case. tests/fixtures/option_bare_some_call_argument_rejected.* deleted, since it pinned the reversed restriction, and C4's record says so. New coverage in tests/fixtures/bare_option_call_argument.zm. Found but **not** fixed, because it is pre-existing and independent: a nested `Some(Some(4))` is refused even in an annotated val, so `check_bare_option_call` does not check its payload against the inner expected type.
- [x] **C11** **Eight weak diagnostics where the compiler already knows the answer**: `try` in a non-`Result` lambda, a nested `fn`, a trailing comma, `Option<Option<T>>` (L2), and `a[i] = x`, `p.f = x`, `k++`, `Option + int` (D2). (L2 P1-5, D2 item 18; S) Diagnostics are this project's measured competitive advantage -- the LLM benchmark showed a message naming an exact fix lets a model self-correct every time -- and D2's four are the most likely day-one mistakes getting the four weakest messages. Done 2026-10-06. Six were genuinely uninformative and are rewritten to name the repair: a nested `fn` (was "expected '(', found identifier"; now says functions do not nest and shows the `val f = fn(...)` form), a trailing comma (pointed at the bracket, not the comma), `a[i] = x` and `p.f = x` (both said "left-hand side of assignment must be a variable name"; the shape of the left side says which repair applies, so they now name `set(arr, i, value)` and `p with { field: value }`), `k++`, and `Option + int`. That last was the worst of them and the same defect as C9: "use 'as' to cast explicitly" is advice that cannot work, since there is no cast from Option<int> to int, so it now names three real ways to get the value out. The other two were already accurate; `try` in a non-Result lambda gained the repair ("give it that return type") rather than only the rule -- distinguishing a lambda from a named function would mean threading the function's identity through check_stmt, which this did not warrant -- and the `Option<Option<T>>` message now names the qualified form, after checking that form actually works. `k--` needed a different fix from `k++`: it parses as `k - (-print(...))` and surfaced much later as "cannot use a void value as an operand" on the wrong line, so both are now caught in parse_statement by their shape before the expression is parsed. Also found and fixed in passing: all four "cannot infer the type of ..." messages listed the contexts that supply an expected type, and C10 had just added a call argument to that list, so all four were stale. Three try fixtures asserted the old wording and now assert shorter substrings. Seven new fixtures, tests/fixtures/diag_*.zm.
- [x] **C12** **Multi-segment `use` references** (`model.patient.X`), or last-two-segment defaulting on collision. (L2 P1-10; M) Flat module names cost the reference backend 1,261 `use` lines (~9% of it), 351 forced aliases and an invented `_model` suffix convention. Closed 2026-10-06, having found a defect underneath the ergonomic complaint and filed the design half as C16. **Two whole-module `use`s binding the same name were accepted silently and the first won** -- `use model/patient` then `use entity/patient` left every `patient.x` going to model's with the second line doing nothing, so a wrong function was quietly called. The per-name `use` form has always rejected its own version of this (`resolve_named_imports` skipped whole-module uses with an explicit `continue`); the same rule now applies to both, naming both paths and suggesting an alias derived from the parent directory (`use entity/patient as entity_patient`). Importing one module twice is an error too. Deliberately *not* done the way this task's second option proposed: last-two-segment defaulting would invent a name the author never wrote, which is the thing SYNTAX.md's own "never a bare name that silently resolves to an import" rule exists to prevent, and `as` already says it explicitly. The reference backend's 351 aliases still check clean, which confirms they are load-bearing rather than noise. tests/fixtures/use_module_name_collision/; documented in SYNTAX.md's `use` section.
- [x] **C13** **Module resolution cannot reach a sibling test directory**, which is why the reference backend has zero unit tests. (D2 item 7; M) `use` has no `..` segment and no search path, so a test file cannot live in `src/test/ziman/`. D2 verified `ziman test` works fine when the file sits beside production code -- so the language, not the test runner, blocks the conventional layout. This is the cause behind the application's **zero** `#[test]` functions; `--coverage` (0ade7ff) can only report on tests that exist. Done 2026-10-06 with `ZIMAN_MODULE_PATH`, a colon-separated list of directories `use` searches after the importing file's own, first match winning, chosen over a `..` segment because it needs no syntax and because it is the same shape and spelling as `ZIMAN_RESOURCE_PATH` -- which already does exactly this for resources and which the backend's own test harness already sets, so it is one more familiar variable rather than a new mechanism. Beside the importing file still wins, so nothing that resolved before resolves differently (verified: a stub `calc.zm` in the test directory shadows the real one on the path). A module reached through the path resolves its own `use` lines the same way, so it can import too. Proven on the real case rather than only a fixture: a unit test in the reference backend's `src/test/ziman/` importing `common` fails without the variable and passes with it -- which is the whole of why that application had zero `#[test]` functions while `ziman test` worked perfectly on a test file sitting beside production code. The not-found error now names the path it searched. tests/fixtures/module_path_sibling/ (a directory fixture with a .env), documented in SYNTAX.md's module section. Note for F13: machine-readable coverage now has something to measure.
- [x] **C14** **Report more than one syntax error per run.** (D2 item 27; M) Eight mistakes cost eight compile cycles. The type checker already recovers and batches; the parser does not. Done 2026-10-06. The premise needed correcting first: the parser already batched across *declarations* -- three errors in three functions were all reported -- so the real limit was that two mistakes in one function cost two cycles, which for a file of any size is most of them. `parse_block` now wraps each statement in a recovery point, the same shape the top level already used per declaration, synchronising on the next line since statements are newline-delimited. Two details that needed care: the loop now ends on TOK_EOF as well as `}`, because after recovery the parser can reach the end of the file with the brace never found and would otherwise spin there; and synchronisation uses the line parsing *stopped* on, not the line the statement began on -- for a multi-line statement those differ, and the first version reported the identical error twice because it resumed at the offending construct. There is already a cap at 25 errors, so a pathological file says "too many errors; stopping" rather than flooding. Verified on errors in nested blocks (4 reported), an unterminated block (2 errors, no hang), and a multi-line statement (the real error plus one cascade, which is inherent and documented at the call site). tests/fixtures/syntax_errors_batched.zm; the reference backend still parses.
- [x] **C15** **Let `a.f(args)` call the function value in field `f`** when `a` is a value of a struct type with such a field. (C9's second half, L2 P1-3, D2 item 10; M) Worth doing: the workaround appears 12 times in the reference backend's `service/crud.zm` alone, with a four-line comment explaining it, and again in `lib/http/server.zm` for `Endpoint`/`Decoder`/`Encoder` -- on what D2 calls the codebase's main extension mechanism. What makes it M rather than S, found while fixing the diagnostic: (a) the error is raised in `loader.c`'s `report_unknown_module_call` during module resolution, which runs before type checking and has no idea whether `a` is a local, so the loader has to stop failing and defer, and the diagnostic has to move to the type checker to keep being as good as it is now -- degrading it would cost more than the feature gains, since diagnostics are this project's measured advantage; and (b) a call's target is a *name* (`Expr.as.call.callee`, with `local_name`/`through_value` for a local of function type), not an expression, so a field call needs a new target form. The cheapest shape that reuses what exists is to desugar in the type checker into an `EXPR_VALUE_BLOCK` binding `val $tmp = a.f` and calling through `$tmp`, which `local_name`/`through_value` already handles, so neither the interpreter nor a new Expr kind is needed. Done 2026-10-06, by the route C9's note predicted. The loader no longer fails on a dotted callee whose qualifier is not an imported module: it marks the call (`unresolved_qualifier`) and leaves it exactly as written, and `check_call` either resolves it to a struct field of function type or calls `ziman_report_unknown_module_call` itself -- so the module diagnostic is unchanged rather than degraded, which was the condition for doing this at all. A call's target is a name everywhere else, so the field access becomes `Expr.as.call.callee_expr`, built and checked in the type checker; the interpreter reuses the existing closure-calling path, which already handled a lambda's captures. The value-block desugar C9's note suggested proved unnecessary -- an explicit callee expression is simpler and means the interpreter evaluates what was written. Covers a generic struct's field (substituted before use), a field whose lambda captures, and binding the field first, which is ordinary field access and still works. A field of non-function type now says so (`'h.name' is a string, which cannot be called`) instead of falling through to the module message. **Two consequences followed through rather than left:** C9's added advice about binding the field to a val is removed, because the workaround it described is gone (and its fixture with it, replaced by tests/fixtures/function_field_call.zm); and the reference backend's service/crud.zm comment, which said the direct call "cannot" be made, is corrected to say the style is now optional (backend 65d3b3e). SYNTAX.md's no-methods paragraph documents the exception.

- [x] **C16** **Multi-segment module references (`model.patient.X`)**, so two modules of the same file name need no alias. (C12's design half, L2 P1-10; M-L) The ergonomic half of C12: a module is referenced by its path's last segment, so the reference backend writes 351 aliases and invented a `_model` suffix convention, and its `use` lines come to 1,261 (~9% of the codebase). Worth separating from the defect C12 fixed, because this one is a language design change and not obviously the right one. Two things to settle before writing code. (a) It collides head-on with C15: `a.b.c(...)` has to decide whether `a.b` is a module path or a field access on a local, which is the same ambiguity that makes `h.run(21)` hard, so the two want designing together rather than in sequence. (b) Most of those 1,261 lines are not aliases at all -- they are the one-name-per-import rule ("no `{A, B, C}` list"), which SYNTAX.md adopts deliberately so that "a file's `use` lines account for every bare name its body could have come from". Multi-segment references would not shorten them; a grouped import form would, and that is a different decision with a documented rationale to argue against first. So the honest question is which of the two costs is actually being complained about. **Evaluated 2026-10-06 and declined as proposed, with the measurement that decided it.** No code change. The question this task was filed with -- which of the two costs is actually being complained about -- has an answer, and it is neither of the ones multi-segment references would fix. The reference backend's 1,271 `use` lines are 812 per-name imports, 459 whole-module imports, 351 of them aliased. Replacing the collision-forced whole-module aliases with dotted paths saves 3,865 characters of `use` lines and adds **14,213 at use sites**, a net +10,348 -- longer, in the places people read most. The pattern is `use controller/admin as admin` beside `use model/admin as admin_model`, and `admin_model.AdminUserResponse` is both shorter and more informative than `model.admin.AdminUserResponse`: an alias names the role, a path names the location. C12 already made the collision a loud error that writes the alias for you, which was the real defect here. Add the resolution cost -- `a.b.c(...)` would have to decide between a module path and a field access, in the same spot C15 just made subtle -- and the trade is clearly bad. Two things worth keeping fell out of the measurement, filed as C17 and noted in F9.

- [x] **C17** **A grouped per-name import, `use model/patient.{Patient, find}`.** (from C16's measurement, 2026-10-06; M, and a decision first) This is where the verbosity actually is, and C16 is where it is not: of the reference backend's 1,271 `use` lines, 812 are per-name imports covering only 424 distinct (file, module) pairs, so a grouped form would remove **388 lines, 47% of them** -- 15 names from `time` in one file, 9 from `service` in another. SYNTAX.md currently says "one name per import, no `{A, B, C}` list", so this needs that decision revisited rather than simply implemented. Worth noting what the stated rationale actually says, because it does not forbid grouping: "No wildcard import form; a per-name import always names the one thing it binds, so a file's `use` lines account for every bare name its body could have come from." A grouped import still names every bare name it binds -- it is wildcards that break that property, not grouping. So the principle survives the change intact, which makes this a question about syntax and taste rather than about the invariant the rule exists to protect. Decide first, then implement. **Decided 2026-10-07: declined, and the rule now carries its reason.** The task's framing was right -- SYNTAX.md asserted "one name per import, no `{A, B, C}` list" with no rationale, and a rule with no recorded reason is indistinguishable from an oversight. The complaint is also legitimate: the backend has **1,275 `use` lines** and `service/medication_adherence.zm` imports fifteen names from `time` alone, and the real cost is positional rather than verbosity -- adding one name to an alphabetised run means finding the slot, which an earlier session scripted rather than did by hand. Three things outweigh it. `--fmt` already sorts runs of consecutive `use` lines, so that friction was solved before it was raised and the answer is to run the formatter. One name per line is independently valid, so a mistake costs one line and the error points at it, where a malformed group breaks the whole import -- which matters more for generated code, since one-line-at-a-time is the shape that goes wrong least. And one fact per line stays greppable: `grep -rn "use service.is_email"` answers "who imports this?" exactly, which a group turns into a multi-line pattern. Then the second principle decides it: a group is a second way to import three names. Recorded in SYNTAX.md where the rule is stated and in DESIGN.md as "Grouped imports, declined", including what would change the answer -- a reader finding fifteen `use time.X` lines worse to *read* than they are to generate, which is the one side of this a human owns and nothing measured here speaks to.


- [x] **C18** **A `Row` bound, generalizing the `Eq`/`Hash`/`Ord` mechanism to a fourth case.** (R, ROADMAP.md:1615; M) `Crud<E, Req, Res>` (`health-record-backend-ziman`'s `service/crud.zm`) has to carry `from_row: fn(Row) -> Result<E, DbError>` and `to_params: fn(E) -> [SqlValue]` as explicit function-value fields on **every** literal -- nine today, growing with each migrated resource -- because there is no way to say "`E` already has a `#[derive(Row)]` pair" the way `<T: Ord>` already says "`T` already has a `_compare`". A `Row` bound would resolve `<snake_type>_from_row`/`<snake_type>_to_params` through the same `find_compare_function` path in the type checker, and both fields would drop out of every literal. Needs the bound grammar and checker extended to a fourth name, and a *two*-function resolution where the existing three resolve one. Taken first of the ten because it is the smallest and it unblocks the database follow-ups at ROADMAP.md:1596; the design pass is the task, not an afterthought to it. **Done 2026-10-09 as a design pass, and the answer is no -- with F1's decision to add `Row` withdrawn in its own entry.** Three findings, each checked against the code rather than argued. **(1)** `Ord`'s mechanism is *runtime dispatch on a value's own type* (`value_compare` reads `a.type.struct_name`, src/interpreter.c's `TY_STRUCT` arm) and generics are erased -- `call_function` takes no type arguments, so a generic body cannot know what `E` was bound to. `to_params: fn(E) -> [SqlValue]` therefore fits the mechanism exactly; **`from_row: fn(Row) -> Result<E, DbError>` cannot**, because `E` appears only in the return type and at the moment of the call no value of type `E` exists. A constructor-shaped function is not resolvable by value dispatch; it needs a per-parameter witness (dictionary passing) or monomorphisation, both of which are the general trait machinery F1's first paragraph refuses. Covering `to_params` alone would be worse than covering neither: a bound named after a derive pair, supplying one of its two functions. **(2)** "The two fields disappear from every `Crud` literal" needs a second, unrelated feature -- struct literals must set every field (`P { a: 1 }` is "missing field 'b'"), and there are no field defaults. **(3)** F1's safety argument -- "nothing stops a copy-paste mistake wiring one resource's `from_row` to another's `to_params`, since both are just function values of matching shape" -- is already covered: they are *not* of matching shape, and `Crud<Allergy> { from_row: allergy_from_row, to_params: encounter_to_params }` is rejected today with *expects fn(Allergy) -> [int], found fn(Encounter) -> [int]*. Verified directly. So what remains of the motivation is **eighteen lines of boilerplate in one file of one sibling repo**, against a new language mechanism. Reversed by, cheapest first: monomorphisation arriving for the bytecode entry's sake; struct-field defaults arriving independently; or F1's own deferral test being tripped properly by a *second* `Crud`-shaped abstraction. Full reasoning in docs/DESIGN.md "The `Row` bound, and why `from_row` cannot work the way `compare` does"; ROADMAP.md's entry struck through with the summary.


- [x] **C19** **Four habits from other languages get a syntax error that names the token instead of the fix.** (found while writing F26's reference solutions, 2026-10-09; S) Provenance worth keeping, because it is the benchmark's own method turned on its author: writing 40 programs for F26 blind, I hit four walls, and the diagnostics for three of them (`let`, `;`, `match`) already named the fix while four others did not. **That split is not a coincidence** -- the three good ones are exactly the habits the 2026-09-28 benchmark's round 1 surfaced, so the project had invested where it had measured, and the four below are habits ten tasks never reached. Fixed 2026-10-09: **(1)** `}` and `else` on separate lines, the ordinary C-family layout, was *expected an expression, found 'else'* and now explains that a statement ends at the newline so the brace already closed the `if`; **(2)** `{"a": 1}` was *expected an expression, found '{'* and now names `Map<string, int> { "a": 1 }` and `Set<int> { 1, 2 }`, with the reason the type arguments are not optional; **(3)** `elif`/`elsif`/`elseif` was *expected an expression, found '{'* -- the wrong message entirely, since by then the parser had consumed the condition -- and now says `} else if cond {`; **(4)** `f"hi {n}"` was *expected ')', found string literal* and now points at `format`/`printf` and the `{}` placeholder. The habit checks are deliberately narrow, following the `match` check's own caution: `elif` is only flagged after a closing brace, and `f` only when a string literal follows the name directly, so a value named `elif` and a function named `f` both still work -- including `elif` assigned just after a `}`, the shape that misfired when the guard looked only one token back (it saw the identifier `match` had already consumed, not the brace). Five fixtures: four for the messages, one for the negatives. 894 passed. **The honest limit on this evidence: n=1, and the subject was me rather than a cold model** -- the real measurement is F26's unrun rounds, and these four cost nothing to fix either way.

## D. Ziman library (P1)

- [x] **D1** `ziman test` with `assert` and `assert_eq`. (L P1-6, D item 15; M)
- [x] **D2** `str(float)` as shortest round-trip; `format` precision (`{:.N}`) and `{{`/`}}` escapes; `format_dynamic`; a small `math` module; float exponent literals. (L P1-7, D; M).
- [x] **D3** Channel additions: `channel_close`, `receive_timeout` and a two-channel `select`. (L P1-10; M)
- [x] **D4** HTTP server hardening: reject duplicate or differing `Content-Length` and `+N` (request smuggling), require `Host`, validate response header values (CRLF injection), add a total header-read deadline. (D B7, B8, item 13; M)
- [x] **D5** Compile route patterns once and dispatch by method and first segment (25 microseconds per endpoint today). (D item 14; S)
- [x] **D6** Consolidate the eight `public_`/`secure_` endpoint builders into one with optional named parameters. (L P1-11; M) Done differently from the wording: one builder with optional parts cannot be written (`run`'s parameters differ with the parts), so the nine typed builders stay over one private `endpoint_core`, `failure:`+`fault:` become one shared `api: Api<E>`, `status:` is optional (200), and the explicit type arguments are dropped at 118 call sites.
- [x] **D7** Parsing strictness: `parse_json("01")`, `parse_int(" 5")` and `"+5"` are accepted against the docs, and JSON big integers become floats. (D B14, L; S) Done: `01` is refused, `parse_int`/`parse_float` take no blanks or `+` (all four parsers agree), and `Json` numbers are `JNumber(Number)` with `Int`/`Float`/`Bigint`/`Decimal` (an integer past 64 bits is an exact `Bigint`; `parse_json_exact` reads fractions as `Decimal`).
- [x] **D8** JWT: `require_exp` (a token without `exp` never expires), plus `iss` and `aud`. (D S9; S) Done: `verify` requires `exp` (and refuses an `exp`/`nbf` that is not a number); `verify_claims(issuer:, audience:, require_exp:, leeway_seconds:, now:)` checks `iss` and `aud`. The backend does not use `iss`/`aud` yet.


- [x] **D9** **Give `fs` a structured error enum** (`NotFound`/`Denied`/`IsDirectory`/`NotDirectory`/`Other` plus `fs_error_message`), matching `NetError`'s shape. Leave `json`/`regex` on strings. (L2 P1-8; M) The three cases a backend actually branches on are reachable only by substring-matching `strerror`. Done 2026-10-06 with `FsError { NotFound, Denied, IsDirectory, NotDirectory, Exists, Other }` and `fs_error_message`. The cheap part was the classification: `native_fs.c` has exactly one error helper, `fs_err`, used 26 times, so one errno switch there covers everything -- and it hands the category over as the same `"category|message"` string native_net.c already uses for NetError, which fs.zm splits. Neither side builds the other's values, and the enum stays declared in Ziman. The 18 Result-returning natives became private `*_raw` with public wrappers, exactly as net.zm wraps `connect_raw`; the three bool predicates needed nothing. Every variant still carries the OS's own wording, so nothing was lost by making it branchable. **The migration needed a missing primitive**: callers whose own errors are strings had no terse way to adapt, so the prelude gains `result_map_err(r, convert)` -- which makes each site `try result_map_err(fs.read_text(p), fs.fs_error_message)`, reads well because a module function can be passed by name, and will be wanted again by D15. Five library sites migrated (csv, properties, migrate, zone); log.zm needed none because it only uses `is_ok`/`unwrap`, and nor did the reference backend, which discards the error with `Err(_)`. Of 253 fs call sites in fixtures only 7 needed touching, for the same reason. **Deliberately left as strings**: the per-item errors inside `lines`/`chunks`/`walk` (`Result<Iter<Result<string, string>>, FsError>`). Those are "a read failed partway through iteration" rather than "the open failed", and making them variants means constructing the enum in C, which is the thing the category|message convention exists to avoid. Noted in fs.zm. tests/fixtures/fs_error_enum.zm.
- [x] **D10** **Server-side cookies and CORS**: a `Cookie:` parser, a `Set-Cookie` builder, and CORS as a `dispatch` wrapper. (L2 P1-9; M each) The HTTP *client* has a full `CookieJar` and the server has nothing; both are week-one needs for anything browser-facing. Done 2026-10-06, both halves. **Cookies** went in `http.zm` (the module both sides share): `request_cookies`/`request_cookie` read the header a browser sends, and `SetCookie`/`session_cookie`/`set_cookie_header`/`delete_cookie_header` build the one it is told to store. A malformed pair is skipped rather than failing the request, since a browser can be made to send almost anything and one bad pair should not cost a session; `session_cookie` defaults to HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite=Lax, so the careless path is the safe one. The client's `CookieJar` stays where it is -- it models what a user agent keeps between requests, which a server has no use for. **CORS** went in `server.zm`, generalised from the reference backend's own `route_cors`: `cors_policy`, `cors_preflight` and `cors_apply`. Two functions rather than one handler-wrapping closure, because a wrapper must return a closure and allocate one per request, while these compose with `dispatch`, a hand-written router or anything else by sitting either side of it. A wildcard policy echoes `*` rather than the origin and drops Allow-Credentials, since a browser refuses that combination anyway. **Found while testing: my own C11 diagnostic recommended `p = p with { field: value }`, and the real syntax is prefix, `with p { field: value }` -- the exact defect C9 was about, made by me. Fixed, with its fixture; the reference backend's Ctx comment had the same error in two places and is corrected too (backend commit). tests/fixtures/http_server_cookies.zm and http_server_cors.zm.
- [x] **D11** **`float` JSON output diverges from `print`/Jackson three ways, and a text round trip loses `Number`'s Float/Int kind.** (D2 item 9, D2 B4; S) Three forms of API divergence against the Spring reference, on a type that appears in every measurement payload. Done 2026-10-06. Of the three divergences, one mattered and is fixed: `%g` dropped the fraction from a whole number, so a float 1.0 was written `1`, 100.0 `100` and -0.0 `-0`. JSON does not separate integers from floats but every reader built on it does -- Jackson and Python's `json` both write `1.0` -- so a client parsed an integer where the API promised a fraction. `put_float` now applies the same "add .0 if it does not already look like one" rule value_display has always applied, by the same `strpbrk` test, so `print` and JSON agree about which values are floats. That also fixes the second half of the report for free: the round trip kept losing `Number`'s kind because `Float(1.0)` serialised to `1` and parsed back as `Int(1)`, and now comes back a Float. The remaining difference is *notation* for large and small magnitudes (`print(1e20)` is 100000000000000000000.0 where JSON writes 1e+20) and is deliberate on print's side -- float_shortest_text uses plain notation up to 1e21 while JSON follows the shortest round-tripping form Jackson and Python also produce, so here JSON is the one agreeing with Jackson. Three fixtures asserted the old output and are regenerated; `json_map_objects` is the useful one, since its int `-5` and its bigints stayed put while only the float `200.0` changed. Worth noting `toml_basic.out` already wrote `ratio = 150.0` in its TOML output beside `150` in its JSON output for the same value -- the TOML writer was right all along. tests/fixtures/json_float_keeps_fraction.zm.
- [x] **D12** **An accumulating JSON decoder** (`Result<T, [string]>`, or a `json_decode_all` companion). (L2 P2-4; M) The derived decoder stops at the first bad field, so the reference backend hand-rolls `FieldError`/`accumulate`/`validation_result` to report more than one validation failure at a time. Done 2026-10-06 as the companion rather than the breaking change -- `#[derive(Json)]` on a struct now also generates `<type>_from_json_all(j) -> Result<T, [string]>`. The existing decoder keeps its signature, so nothing breaks and nothing it already does gets slower; a caller opts in by name. Four shapes were considered and the choice matters, so: (1) a second "record, do not return" emission mode in derive.c duplicates the emitter's recursion and must be kept in step with it; (2) per-field top-level helper functions would add ~654 generated functions to the reference backend alone (73 Json derives, ~581 fields); (3) changing the existing decoder to `Result<T, [string]>` breaks 18 distinct call sites and still needs one of the others internally; (4) **a lambda per field inside the companion**, which is what landed -- the emitter already ends a failing field with `return err(...)`, and inside a lambda that returns from the lambda, so the existing per-field emission is reused verbatim, including its skip-the-rest behaviour, with no second mode to maintain. Its cost is one closure per field, paid only by callers of the companion, which is why the shape is acceptable here and would not have been in the main decoder. Verified on four simultaneously-wrong fields including a nested array element, a missing required field (3 problems), an absent Option staying None, a non-object document, and that a good document decodes identically either way. Known limits, both documented in SYNTAX.md and at the emission site: a nested struct contributes one message (its own decoder is the single-error one), and an enum gets no companion since its decode either matches a variant or does not. Two fixtures needed updating -- cli_emit_derived (the emitted text grew) and derive_error_position_struct, whose expectation pinned a whole two-error block and now asserts the one line that is its point. tests/fixtures/derive_json_from_json_all.zm.
- [x] **D13** **One `url` module and one `percent_decode`.** (L2 P2-6, D2 item 19; S-M) Two 80-line URL parsers and two `percent_decode`s with two arities and two error types implement one algorithm across `db` and `http` -- about 160 duplicated lines. Done 2026-10-06 for the duplication, and the two URL parsers are deliberately left alone. `percent_decode` really was one algorithm twice, differing only in whether `+` is a space and in the error type. The obvious fix -- have `db` call `http`'s -- was wrong: `db` does not depend on `http` and should not start to for a `%XX` loop. So the loop moved to a new `lib/core/url.zm` below both, and each keeps its own signature as a thin adapter (http's for the public API it already exported; db's to answer a DbError naming the URL at fault, which a caller cannot work out from "bad %-escape" alone). The generated dependency graph confirms the layering: `db --> url`, `http --> url`, `url --> number`, and no `db --> http`. **The two parsers are not duplicates**, which is worth recording since they look it at 80 and 82 lines: they read different grammars into different shapes -- `Url { secure, host, port, target, userinfo }` for `https://host/path` against `DbUrl { user, password, host, port, database, params }` for `postgres://user:pw@host/db?a=b`. Merging them means inventing a general URL type for both to specialise, which is a design change rather than a de-duplication, and url.zm's header comment says so. Adding a core module took four registrations (Makefile CORELIB_MODULES, corelib.c's table and its #include, gen_lib_manual.py's GROUPS) and the README's module table. tests/fixtures/url_percent_shared.zm checks both adapters agree with the primitive.
- [x] **D14** **Add `mapb_get_or`, and audit `Map`/`MapBuilder` for other asymmetries.** (D2 item 20; Trivial) Removes an inline `switch` from every counting loop. Done 2026-10-06. `mapb_get_or` is ordinary Ziman in the prelude rather than a builtin like `map_get_or`, because it is exactly the switch it replaces -- `mapb_set(counts, k, mapb_get_or(counts, k, 0) + 1)` is the whole point -- and its type arguments infer from the builder, so no call site spells them out. The audit found no other *missing* operation. What it did find is three limitations that are the deliberate build-then-convert design rather than asymmetries: a builder cannot be iterated (`for k, v in mb`), an ArrayBuilder cannot be indexed, and `len()` does not accept a builder -- each builder has its own `mapb_len`/`setb_len`/`ab_len` instead, which is a separate namespace on purpose. Recorded so the next person does not re-derive it. tests/fixtures/mapb_get_or.zm.
- [x] **D15** **Error-type consolidation across the library: 281 `Result<_, string>`, up from 242.** (D2 item 24; L) The one axis D2 found to have got *worse* since the previous review. D9 is the first concrete instance; this is the general pass, and it wants a decision about which modules keep strings on purpose (`json`, `regex`) before it starts. **Audited 2026-10-06 and closed with no code change: the metric measures the wrong thing.** All 280 were classified -- 55 are internal `*_raw` plumbing behind an already-structured API (net's eleven are *all* private, behind NetError; fs's twenty-one behind FsError; sqlite's five behind DbError), ~184 are parse and format errors where the message is the whole information and nobody branches, 14 are application-supplied callbacks in `server` (`decode: fn([byte]) -> Result<I, string>` -- the app's message to a client, which a library enum would be wrong for), 13 are `os` capability failures a program cannot act on, and 3 are the fs Iter item errors D9 documented. The decisive evidence is D9 itself, the task that fixed exactly what this one is about: it took fs from 21 `Result<_, string>` to **24** while taking its *public* ones from 18 to **3**. A metric that moves the wrong way on its own best-case fix is not the one to track. Every module whose errors callers branch on already has a type -- FsError, NetError, DbError, and JwtError on `verify` (jwt.sign keeps a string, its only failure being a programming mistake). The rule and the full breakdown are now in DESIGN.md so a later review does not re-raise the count; if it is tracked at all, the number worth watching is public `Result<_, string>` in modules whose errors are branched on, which is zero.
- [x] **D16** **Clarify `datasource.query_one`'s doc: it runs the whole query.** (D2 item 26; Trivial) The name promises `LIMIT 1` and the implementation does not add one. Done 2026-10-06: confirmed by reading it -- `query_one` calls `query`, which fetches every row, and returns the first; no `LIMIT 1` is added anywhere. The doc now says so and says when it matters (a lookup by primary key or unique column returns one row from the engine anyway, so there it makes no difference).
## E. Backend hardening and features (P1)

- [x] **E1** Indexes on every `patient_id` and the sort columns; native Postgres types through a per-dialect migration set. (D item 8; S-M) Done: `V024__add_indexes.sql` (shared; one `(patient_id, <sort column>)` index per owned table plus `panel_id`, `pregnancy_record_id`, broadcast `created_at`) and a Postgres-only `V025` making every key a native `UUID` (a no-op `sqlite/V025` keeps the versions aligned). Timestamps, dates and flags deliberately stay text/0-1: the application sorts them as ISO text and the driver returns a `TIMESTAMPTZ` in Postgres's own format. Verified on SQLite and on Postgres 17 (backend suites, plus the resulting column types and foreign keys).
- [x] **E2** `storage_error` helper and reduced error boilerplate (with C2). (D item 9; S). Done: `#[from]` (C2) made a `storage_error` helper unnecessary; the remaining hand-written blocks are gone through `service.internal(result)` (a `Result<T, string>` from `crypto`/`jwt` as an `Internal` `ServiceError`, for `try`) and `try` in `security.zm`/`health_event.zm`. One `Option<ServiceError>` site in `insurance_record.zm` keeps its explicit `Storage(...)` because `try` needs a `Result`. `map_err` and constructors as function values were not added: nothing left needs them.
- [x] **E3** Multi-error validation (a list of field errors), field names in decode errors, hide raw constraint text from clients. (D item 10, B12; M) Done: `service/service.zm`'s `ServiceError.Validation` now carries `[FieldError]` instead of one `ValidationError`; every resource's `broken_rule` accumulates into a list (`errors = accumulate(errors, optional_not_one_of(...))` or a plain `push`, then `validation_result(errors)`) instead of returning on the first problem, so `POST /api/me/allergies` with a blank `substance`, a bad `type` and a bad `category` answers all three in one `errors` object; `controller/controller.zm`'s `from_service_error` builds the map from the whole list. Field names in decode errors turned out to be a `ziman` (this repo) fix, not a backend one: `src/derive.c`'s `#[derive(Json)]` codegen now threads the struct field's camelCase name through `emit_from_json` and wraps the field's own decode error with it (`"email: expected a string"`), composing once per nesting level (`"address: zipCode: expected a string"`) since a nested struct's own fields are wrapped the same way; an element inside an array/set/map value stays unnamed (no field of its own, only a position). `db_error_message`'s raw constraint text (`UNIQUE constraint failed: users.email`) no longer reaches the client: a `Storage(Constraint(_))` answers a generic `"a record with that value already exists"` (409); `NotFound` still answers the repository's own (already client-safe) message. Tests: `docs/SYNTAX.md` documents the new decode-error shape, three `tests/fixtures/*.out` golden files and the README's own worked example updated for the added field name; the backend's `resources_test.py` gained a check per resource with both a blank and an enum field that a request breaking both is answered with both named, not just the first (5 resources, verified on SQLite and Postgres 17 backend suites).
- [x] **E4** Transactions for the cascades and for the two-active-insurance rule; delete without a check-then-delete race. (D item 11, B13; S-M) Done: `service.atomically` (one transaction, rolled back by any `ServiceError`, joins an enclosing one via `Ctx.in_transaction`) wraps the lab-panel and pregnancy-record cascades and insurance create/update, which now take a row lock on the patient (`UPDATE patients SET id = id`, portable to SQLite and Postgres) before checking the two-ACTIVE rule; every repository `delete` answers `NotFound` when it removed no row, and the separate find before it is gone. A unique partial index was not added: it cannot express "at most two" and would fail the migration on existing data that broke the rule. Tests: eight concurrent ACTIVE inserts and eight concurrent deletes in `rules_test.py` (the insert test fails without the lock).
- [x] **E5** Rate limiting and lockout on login, an MFA attempt cap, a TOTP replay guard, and encryption of TOTP secrets at rest. (D item 12, S3, S4; M) Done: two `ziman` (this repo) additions the backend needed rather than worked around, both in `crypto`/`otp` (native, `lib/core`, so any consumer gets them) -- `crypto.aes_gcm_encrypt`/`aes_gcm_decrypt` (a fresh 12-byte nonce, `nonce || ciphertext || 16-byte tag`, `Err` only for a wrong key length) and `otp.totp_matching_step` (the 30-second step a code matched, so a caller can refuse a repeat of one already spent; `totp_valid` is now defined in terms of it). Backend: `service/service.zm`'s shared `AttemptWindow`/`is_locked`/`record_failure`/`record_success` (a lock does not get longer for guesses made during it; the count starts over, not on top of the old one, once a lock has expired) drive both throttles from `V027__add_auth_throttling.sql`'s new columns -- `users.failed_login_count`/`locked_until` (ten wrong passwords lock an account out 15 minutes; keyed by the submitted email, real or not, so the lockout tells a client nothing an unknown-email login didn't already) and `user_mfa.failed_attempts`/`locked_until` (five wrong codes against a pending login lock the account, not the token, for 15 minutes; also caps `verify_backup`'s up-to-eight Argon2 checks per attempt, S3's CPU-amplification note, to 5×8 before lockout). `user_mfa.last_totp_step` is the replay guard, scoped to the pending-login `verify`/`verify-backup` only: `verify_setup`/`disable` don't touch it, since a code an app is showing right now is legitimately good for both confirming setup and finishing a login moments later. `user_mfa.secret` is now `crypto.aes_gcm_encrypt` under a new `app.security.mfa-secret-key` (base64, exactly 32 bytes, no default, must differ from `app.jwt.secret`) instead of plain base32; `setup` still shows the plaintext secret and QR URI once, at enrollment. Per-IP throttling was not done: no `net.peer_address` to key it by (same gap E7 hit for the connection cap), tracked in `ziman`'s `docs/ROADMAP.md`. Tests: `tests/fixtures/crypto_aes_gcm.zm` and an extended `otp_basic.zm` in ziman; `mfa_test.py` gained the lockout, the replay guard and a check that the stored secret is not the plaintext one, `auth_test.py` gained the login lockout and four new `MFA_SECRET_KEY` startup-failure cases, verified on SQLite and Postgres 17 backend suites plus the curly scenarios.
- [x] **E6** Admin safety: guard against demoting, suspending or deleting yourself or the last admin; put the acting admin's email in `ADMIN_*` lines; log a delete only after it commits. (D S7; S) Done: `service/admin.zm`'s `guard_self` refuses `change_role`/`change_status`/`delete_user` against the caller's own account outright (a demotion, a disabling status, or a delete; self-service is `PUT /api/users/me/...`), and `guard_last_admin` refuses the same three against the very last `role = 'ADMIN'` row, by a fresh `repository/stats.zm` count taken at the time (`count_where(conn, "users", "role", "ADMIN")`); a bulk operation counts a refused id as failed, not fatal, same as an unknown one. In practice `guard_last_admin`'s own branch is unreachable except where `guard_self` already fires first -- every caller of these routes is itself an `ADMIN`, so if only one such row exists the caller must be it -- but it stays as the honest, second-order guard against exactly that invariant (never zero administrators) rather than trusting the self-check alone, and against a genuine concurrent race between two administrators that a single request's guard cannot see. Every `ADMIN_ROLE_CHANGE`/`STATUS_CHANGE`/`RESET_PASSWORD`/`USER_DELETE` line now also carries `adminEmail=` (the existing `email=` for the affected account is untouched, so `service/audit.zm`'s extraction of it keeps working); `delete_user` logs `ADMIN_USER_DELETE` only after its transaction returns, not before it opens, so the log can no longer claim a delete that failed. Threading the caller through needed `Principal` on `change_role`/`change_status`/`delete_user`/`reset_password`/`bulk_status`/`bulk_delete` and on `testdata.zm`'s two test-user deletes, which go through the same `delete_user`. Tests: `admin_test.py` gained the three self-guard refusals plus the two bulk cases (five checks with a lone administrator, the only shape the guard is reachable in through the API); `audit_test.py` confirms `adminEmail=` on all four event kinds while the existing per-target `email=` extraction is unchanged; verified on SQLite and Postgres 17 backend suites plus the curly scenarios.
- [x] **E7** Slow-client protection (64 slow sockets can occupy the server), security headers (HSTS, `X-Content-Type-Options`), and drop the `Server: ziman` banner. (D S5, S8; S-M) Done: `ServeOptions.header_timeout_ms` (a new connection's first byte and a request's head must arrive in a few seconds, separate from the keep-alive/body `idle_timeout_ms`), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` on every response from the library, no `Server` banner, and `Strict-Transport-Security` from the backend's router (`server.hsts-max-age-seconds`; the library cannot know whether TLS ends in the process). A per-client connection cap was not done (no peer address in `net`, and a proxy hides it): ROADMAP. Found and fixed on the way (own commit): a handle-set self-deadlock and a collected defer list, both exposed by the concurrency test of E4.
- [x] **E8** Close the account-enumeration gap on wrong-provider login and correct the README claim. (D S6; S) Done: `service/auth.zm`'s `login` answers a wrong-provider account exactly like a wrong password -- the generic 401 `"Invalid email or password"` through the same `login_failed` (so it still counts toward E5's lockout) -- instead of naming the real provider (`"This account uses GOOGLE login..."`, copied faithfully from the Spring backend, which has the identical gap); the dummy `crypto.password_verify` call closes the timing side of it too, matching the other two failure paths. The detailed reason stays server-side only (`AUTH_LOGIN_FAIL reason=wrong_provider ... provider=GOOGLE`). README corrected: the "differences from Spring, on purpose" paragraph now says so instead of just covering the unknown-email/wrong-password case and leaving the reader to assume the rest. Tests: `auth_test.py`'s "another provider's account" check now asserts the generic message, not the old provider-naming one; verified on SQLite and Postgres 17 backend suites plus the curly scenarios.
- [x] **E9** Extract a shared CRUD helper to cut the six-layer copy per resource. (D item 16; M-L) Done for every resource the helper actually fits: `service/crud.zm`'s `Crud<E, Req, Res>` -- a table name, a 404 label, the column list (`to_params`/`from_row`'s order, `id` first, `patient_id` second) and six function values (`from_row`, `to_params`, `validate`, `from_request`, `apply`, `to_response`) -- plus `crud_list`/`crud_get`/`crud_create`/`crud_update_one`/`crud_delete_one`, which build a resource's `SELECT`/`INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` from `table`/`columns` instead of it writing its own hand-numbered placeholder strings, and a bare-table `crud_delete_by_patient_id` for cascading deletes. Two real wrinkles, both from things this codebase had not combined before: a struct field holding a function value has to be read into a local `val` before it can be called (`crud.validate(req)` parses as a method call, which Ziman does not have) -- the same thing `lib/http/server.zm`'s `dispatch` already does with `Endpoint`/`Decoder`/`Encoder` -- and a `pub val` (`table`, `columns`, on the entity, so they live next to the struct they describe and nowhere else) has no dotted access at all (`entity.table` fails to resolve; DESIGN.md's "Global val" entry already documents this, `use math.pi`/`use math.e` the existing example), so it has to be brought in by name (`use entity/allergy.table`) and used bare, or renamed on import where two resources' bare names would collide (`use entity/allergy.table as allergy_table`). Nine resources converted, each now five layers instead of six (`service/X.zm` is its word lists, `broken_rule`, one `Crud` value and five one-line wrappers keeping the old public names; `entity/X.zm` exports `table`/`columns`; no `repository/X.zm` left): `allergy` (the proof of concept), `immunization`, `emergency_contact`, `medical_history`, `family_history`, `encounter`, `medical_procedure`, `dental_record`, `vision_record`. `repository/owned.zm`'s `delete_owned_by_patient` moved too, to `service/patient.zm` (cascading a patient's delete across every table they own is the patient's business, not a narrow repository's); it deletes the nine converted resources' rows with `crud_delete_by_patient_id(conn, <resource>_table, patient_id)` directly, the rest still through their own `repository/X.zm`. `controller/X.zm` and `routes.zm` did not change for any of the nine. Deliberately not converted, and not planned to be: `insurance_record` (the two-ACTIVE lock under `service.atomically`), `pregnancy_record` (a computed gestational age and its linked health events), `body_measurement`/`vital_sign`/`health_event` (pagination, filters, computed fields) and the `lab_panel`/`lab_result` pair (a panel's results nested in its own response, a result reachable only through its panel) -- each does something outside plain list/get/create/update/delete that a `Crud<E, Req, Res>` value has no field for; forcing them in would either weaken `Crud` for every resource or need per-resource escape hatches that erase the point of sharing it. Not done: D item 16's third point, a `crud_endpoints` helper to also collapse `routes.zm`'s five-`Endpoint`-per-resource block (still hand-written for all sixteen resources, converted or not). Verified on SQLite and Postgres 17 backend suites (`allergy_test.py`, `immunization_test.py`, the table-driven `resources_test.py` covering the other seven, the cross-tenant and cascade-delete checks in `admin_test.py`) plus the curly scenarios.
- [x] **E10** Fill the Spring gaps clients need: `/api/users/me*`, medication `color`, codes, doses and refills. (D item 17; L) `/api/users/me*` done 2026-09-29 (health-record-backend-ziman): the 7 endpoints Spring's `UserController` has and Ziman was missing -- `GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE /api/users/me`, `POST .../convert-to-local`, and the whole preferences subresource (`GET`/`PUT .../preferences`, `POST .../preferences/complete-onboarding`) -- new `entity/user_preferences.zm`, `repository/user_preferences.zm`, `model/user.zm`, `service/user.zm`, `controller/user.zm`, migration V028, `ConvertToLocalRequest` added to the existing `model/auth.zm`, `update_name`/`update_provider_and_password_hash` added to `repository/user.zm`. `PUT /api/users/me` also syncs the caller's patient first/last name from the new display name (`split_once` on the first space), matching Spring's `PatientService.syncNameFromUser`. `DELETE` mirrors `service/admin.zm`'s `delete_user` cascade transaction but without either of its guards (self-deletion is the point, not a mistake to block). Preferences are an upsert done explicitly in the service layer (this codebase has no `ON CONFLICT`/upsert SQL anywhere to reuse) with the `PUT`'s every field `Option<T>` so an absent field leaves the stored value alone; `hidden_views`/`dashboard_cards` are comma-separated text in the row, `[string]` at the API, converted only in the mapper. `/api/users/me/password` stays exactly where it already was (`service/auth.zm`), not moved into the new module. Verified: full backend suite (`src/test/other/run-all.sh`), 18 files including new `user_profile_test.py` (21 checks: profile GET/PUT + patient name sync for both a one-word and multi-word name, a blank name rejected, preferences defaults before any write, two separate partial `PUT`s each leaving the other's field alone, onboarding idempotent, convert-to-local rejecting an already-LOCAL account and a short password then succeeding and actually logging in, delete-account invalidating the token and freeing the email for reuse) -- 0 failed.
