Medication half done 2026-09-29 (health-record-backend-ziman, migration V029): the 7 fields left out of `Medication` when it was first written (`rxNormCode`/`atcCode`/`snomedCtCode`/`prescribedBy`/`prescriberNpi`/`indication`/`color`), color propagation across same-named records (`service/medication.zm`'s `propagate_color_to_same_name`, matching Spring's `propagateColorToSameName`), and the whole dose-log/refill-log subsystem that didn't exist at all: new `entity/service/repository/model/mapper` files for `MedicationDoseLog`/`MedicationRefillLog`, 6 new endpoints (`POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE` on `.../doses` and `.../refills`, using a new `named_uuid_param`/two-id-path helper in routes.zm since these are the first routes here with two `{}` captures), and `service/medication_adherence.zm` (`next_dose_due_at`, `is_dose_overdue`, `adherence_rate_percent`, `next_refill_due_date`, `is_refill_due_soon`), a faithful port of Spring's `MedicationAdherenceService` onto Ziman's own `time` module (`instant_diff`/`instant_add`/`duration_of_seconds`/`localdate_add_days`). `MedicationResponse` now embeds `dose_logs`/`refill_logs` plus the five computed fields, assembled fresh on every read by a new `service/medication.zm` `build_response` helper -- never stored. Caught and fixed the one easy-to-miss integration point: the account-delete cascade (`service/patient.zm`'s `delete_owned_by_patient`, shared by self-delete and admin delete-user) now deletes both new child tables before `medications` itself, so deleting an account with logged doses/refills doesn't leave orphans or hit a foreign-key violation. Verified: full backend suite, 19 files including new `medication_details_test.py` (27 checks: new-field round-trip, color propagation to a same-named sibling but not a different medication, dose/refill log CRUD with 404s across medications and 400s for a bad status/`daysSupply`, adherence fields null/false with no `expectedIntervalHours` and correct once one is set and a dose is logged, an overdue dose, a refill's due date and due-soon flag, no orphaned logs after a medication delete, and account deletion with logged doses/refills present) -- 0 failed. `handle_interaction_check`'s 501 stub is untouched -- interaction-check was never part of E10's wording. E10 closed.
- [x] **E11** Small ones: `interaction-check` captured by `/{id}`, timestamp text ordering when nanos is 0, pagination and log rotation for high-volume lists. (D B9, B11, item 21; S-M) Done: `GET /api/me/medications/interaction-check` is a route ahead of `/{id}` answering 501 (the feature is Spring's, not built here); `instant_to_sql` always writes the nine-digit fraction (so text order is time order) with a backend migration V026 that rewrites stored whole-second values; `?page=&size=` on health-events, vital-signs and body-measurements answers a Spring-shaped `Page` (no `page`/`size`: the array as before); `log` rotates a file at `max_bytes` keeping `keep_files` (backend `logging.max-bytes`/`logging.keep-files`), and the admin log/audit reads stream the tail instead of loading the file. Found on the way: a loader bug with aliased type arguments of a generic enum (fixed, fixture).


- [x] **E12** **A `ServeOptions.fault` hook, so 413/431/408 use the application's error shape and headers.** (D2 item 6, D2 B6; S) Protocol-level rejections never reach the application's renderer, so they answer a different body shape with no CORS or HSTS -- a browser client cannot read the body of its own rejection. Done 2026-10-06. `ServeOptions.fault: Option<fn(int, string) -> Response>` renders the rejections the server makes before a handler is reached. It is given the status and the sentence rather than the `HttpError`, so an application needs no match on a library enum to answer a 413, and the mapping now lives once in `error_status`/`error_text` with `error_response` built from the same pair. `write_error_with` takes the hook; `write_error` stays as the `None` case. The backend passes `routes.protocol_fault` (a closure over `ctx`), which renders the API's own body and adds the HSTS header `route` adds to everything else. **CORS is not answered there and cannot be**: these failures happen while the request is still being read, so there is no parsed `Origin` to echo -- the review's "error shape and headers" is achievable for the shape and for static headers, not for a per-request one. Verified end to end against the running backend: a 431 from a 20 KB header and a 400 from a malformed request line both answer the same `timestamp`/`status`/`message` shape as an ordinary 404, both carrying HSTS. tests/fixtures/http_serve_fault_hook.zm serves the same two requests with a hook and with `fault: None`, so it pins both the new behaviour and the unchanged default. Adding the struct field broke four existing `ServeOptions` literals, which now pass `fault: None`. 876 fixtures in all three builds.
- [x] **E13** **Extract `json_encoder<O>`, `list_encoder<O>` and `crud_endpoints` from `routes.zm`.** (D2 item 8; M) 2,328 lines for 132 endpoints, including 105 near-identical encoder blocks; the extraction removes about 600 lines and the drift risk across 105 copies. D2's verdict on the rest of the layering is that it paid off -- per resource, after the existing `Crud` extraction, Ziman is only about 30% heavier than Spring. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (5a361f0): **routes.zm went from 2,345 lines to 1,501**, more than the ~600 estimated. `json_encoder`/`list_encoder`/`listing_encoder` replaced 104 spelled-out encoder blocks (the list form was seven lines of `collections.map` each), following the `json_decoder` helper already in the file; one `server.encoder` stays written out, the identity encoder for a raw `Json` body. `crud_endpoints<Req, Res>` produces the five entries for the **eleven** resources with exactly the standard shape -- three of the fourteen (`body-measurements`, `vital-signs`, `health-events`) are left written out because their list endpoint answers a paged `Listing`, which the selection check rejected on its own rather than my having to spot it. The ordering risk was checked before any of it: `dispatch` takes the first match, so a literal path must precede a `{id}` pattern, and none of the eleven has another registered path under it -- which is why the generated sets can follow the hand-written ones, and `all_endpoints`'s comment records it. Verified by comparing the route table as a set: **the same 132 (method, pattern, handler) triples before and after**, which is the check that catches a silent shadowing or a handler wired to the wrong route, since the type checker cannot. Then `--check` clean and 19 suites / 971 checks on SQLite.
- [x] **E14** **`HEAD` on a GET route answers 405**; add a HEAD-to-GET fallback in `server.dispatch`. (D2 item 11, D2 B7; S) RFC conformance, and free for every Ziman server rather than per application. Done 2026-10-06: reproduced first (`HEAD /thing` on a GET route answered 405 with `Allow: GET`), then `dispatch` retries an unmatched HEAD as GET. No body handling was needed there -- `write_response` already withheld the body for a HEAD request while writing the `Content-Length` the body would have had, which its own comment documented, so the gap was only in routing. The exact-method loop still runs first, so an application that registers its own HEAD route keeps it. `Allow` now also names HEAD wherever GET is served, because after the fallback it really is allowed and listing only GET would state the opposite of what the server does. **tests/fixtures/http_dispatch_routes was asserting the bug** (`HEAD / -> 405`); its expectation is corrected and its comment records what it used to claim. New tests/fixtures/http_head_falls_back_to_get.zm checks the promise on a real socket rather than through dispatch: `HEAD /thing` answers 200 with `Content-Length: 12` and zero body bytes, a POST-only path still answers 405 with `Allow: POST`, and an unknown path still answers 404. 875 fixtures in all three builds.
- [x] **E15** **Escape all control bytes in `log.single_line`** -- ANSI escapes still reach the operator's terminal. (D2 item 12, D2 B5, D2 S4; Trivial) The newline-forgery half of this was fixed in the right layer (`lib/core/log.zm:127`); the remaining escape bytes are terminal injection into whoever reads the log. Done 2026-10-06: `single_line` escaped only `\n` and `\r`, so an ESC byte went through raw and `ESC [ 2 J` from a user-controlled value cleared the screen of whoever was reading the log (reproduced, along with BEL, BS and DEL). It now writes out every C0 control byte and DEL -- `\n`, `\r` and `\t` in their readable forms, the rest as `\xNN`. Bytes from 0x80 up are deliberately untouched: they are UTF-8, and escaping them would corrupt every non-English log line to guard against nothing, since a terminal acts on C0 and DEL rather than on text. tests/fixtures/log_escapes_control_bytes.zm, which logs to a file and reads it back -- with no file a Logger writes to stderr, which the harness does not compare, and that cost a first attempt at the fixture.
- [x] **E16** **Index `medication_dose_logs` and `medication_refill_logs`.** (D2 item 13, D2 B8; Trivial) V029's two tables are the only ones in the schema with no index. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (4cc6fcb) as V030. Verified the claim first by listing every CREATE TABLE against every CREATE INDEX: seven tables have no explicit index, and five of those get an implicit one from a UNIQUE or primary key, leaving exactly the two V029 added. Both are read and deleted the same two ways -- `WHERE medication_id = $1 ORDER BY <date> DESC` for the list a client asks for, and `WHERE patient_id = $1` for the cascade on patient delete -- so each gets `(medication_id, <date>)`, which serves filter and order together as V024's `(patient_id, created_at)` pairs do, plus `patient_id` for the cascade, which Postgres does not index from the foreign key alone. Shared migration directory, since CREATE INDEX is the same SQL on both engines. Confirmed by migrating a scratch database through the whole chain and listing sqlite_master.
- [x] **E17** **Four statements in the backend README are now false** (Scope, 'Both pass', 'Not done: authentication', the lockout sentence). (D2 item 15; S) A reader learns the wrong system, and the 'Both pass' claim is what hid B7 for two commits. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (32ce6e5). Three of the four were false and are corrected: "Scope"'s "no real HTTP ... handlers are plain functions standing in for one" (the backend has served real HTTP for a long time, and the testing section contradicted the scope section in the same file); "Not done: TLS, authentication, and any rate limiting" (TLS and rate limiting are genuinely open, but the auth bullets 150 lines above describe the JWT, bcrypt upgrade, MFA and lockout that the suites check); and the lockout sentence's claim that ten failures lock an email out "real or not" -- the counters live on the user's row, so an unknown email cannot be locked, and the real reason a client learns nothing is better: an attempt against an unknown email already answers exactly like one against a locked account, same generic 401 and same dummy bcrypt against a fixed hash, which `service/auth.zm`'s login does on every branch. The fourth, "Both engines pass, 19 suites and about 950 checks each", was **not** changed: B7 made it true and the numbers check out (a SQLite run gives 19 suites and 958 checks); the Postgres half was not run in this session, so it rests on B7's fix. Each correction states in the file what it used to claim.
- [x] **E18** **`INSERT ... RETURNING *` / `UPDATE ... RETURNING *` in `crud_save`/`crud_update`.** (D2 item 21; S) Halves the round trips and pool borrows on every write -- which matters more given the contention in F7. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (47ba390). `returns_written_row` asks `datasource.dialect`: sqlite and postgres take `RETURNING *`, mysql keeps write-then-read since MySQL has none. Checked rather than assumed -- the bundled SQLite reports 3.50.2 (RETURNING needs 3.35) and a probe confirmed `INSERT ... RETURNING *` comes back through `query_one`; Postgres could not be run, so `pg.zm` was read instead, where `execute` and `query` are `.affected` and `.rows` over one shared `run`, so no statement-kind special case can trip on an INSERT that returns tuples. The missing-row cases are the part worth reviewing: an UPDATE matching nothing returns the same `not_found(crud, id)` the old SELECT produced rather than an empty success, and an INSERT reporting no row is a Sql error rather than a silent read-back. Verified on SQLite -- 19 suites, 958 checks -- which is coverage of the new path rather than the fallback, since sqlite takes RETURNING, so every create and update went through it. The latency saved is still not measured: in-memory SQLite has no round trip to save and would understate it, and Postgres was not run; the claim made is only the structural one, one statement and one pool borrow instead of two. **Verified on Postgres 2026-10-06** (the gap this note previously recorded is closed): 19 suites, 971 checks, 0 failed. Confirmed the new path is the one that ran rather than the fallback -- a probe against a Postgres 17 container shows `datasource.dialect` answers `postgres` for a pool as well as a direct connection, so `returns_written_row` is true and `resources_test.py`'s 483 checks drove every create and update through `RETURNING *`. The probe also shows `INSERT ... RETURNING *` returning a server-defaulted column, which is the reason to use `*` at all, and that a cross-tenant `UPDATE ... WHERE id = $1 AND patient_id = $2 RETURNING *` returns no row -- so `crud_update`'s None branch answering `not_found` is right rather than theoretical.
- [x] **E19** **`AND patient_id = $2` in `crud_update`'s `WHERE`; `crud_delete_one` returning `Result<unit, _>`.** (D2 item 22; Trivial) Defence in depth on the tenant check (the live behaviour is already correct), and a dead return value. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (4415e3b). `crud_update` now has `AND patient_id = $2`, matching `crud_delete` right below it; nothing cross-tenant could reach it today since the only caller loads the row by id and patient_id first, so this is defence in depth, and the asymmetry was the only reason to wonder which statement was right. For the dead return value: before changing it I checked all 27 endpoints whose handler returned a bool, resolving the import aliases, and none has a non-empty `encoders` list -- the value is never encoded into a body, and an absent row is already an Err. So 27 handlers and the 30 service functions behind them return `Result<unit, _>`, 18 `return true` statements are gone, two account deletes use `with_transaction<unit>` instead of binding a `deleted` that holds nothing, and two `atomically<bool>` became `atomically<unit>`. The five non-delete handlers in that set (change_password, verify_setup, disable, dismiss, deactivate) were included because the same evidence covers them and leaving half the layer on bool would be worse. A first mechanical pass regexed each file and changed a `return true` inside an inner `with_transaction<bool>` lambda; the type checker caught it, and it was redone walking function boundaries. Verified on the type check and the SQLite suite, and **on Postgres 2026-10-06**: 19 suites, 971 checks, 0 failed, plus a direct probe showing that a cross-tenant `UPDATE ... WHERE id = $1 AND patient_id = $2` matches no row there.
- [x] **E20** **Three API divergences from Spring**: `@Email`-grade email validation, the 404 message, and accepting a numeric string where Spring does. (D2 item 23; S) Found by comparing matching endpoints side by side; each is small and each is visible to a client. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (50bc293): **two matched, the third left divergent on purpose.** `service.is_email` now applies Hibernate Validator's `@Email` rules, which is what `@Email` means in Spring -- split at the last `@`, local part 1-64 with no dot at either end and no doubled dot, domain 1-255 in dot-separated labels with no hyphen at a label edge. Two details exist to avoid being *stricter* than Spring, which would be the same class of divergence: `user@localhost` passes (Hibernate needs no dot in the domain) and `a@b_c.d` passes (its domain charset allows punctuation that is not a valid hostname), as does a non-ASCII domain. For the 404s, all 24 hand-written messages and the 9 `Crud` labels take Spring's form `<Label> not found: <uuid>` with Spring's own label words read from its source, which is why `procedure` became `Medical procedure`; one message was already right, which is why the single test asserting this text kept passing. **The numeric string stays a 400**: matching Jackson's coercion means loosening ziman's `float_from_json`, which every `#[derive(Json)]` program inherits, and since Jackson coerces for every numeric field nothing smaller gives parity -- a language's JSON decoder should not get looser to match one framework's legacy coercion. 13 new assertions in auth_test.py cover the email cases both ways and the 404 wording, which nothing checked before; 19 suites, 971 checks on SQLite.
- [x] **E21** **Housekeeping**: validate `server.idle-timeout-ms` and `server.port`, add `--fmt --check` to CI, remove the two unused bindings. (D2 item 25; Trivial) The `--fmt --check` job is the same shape of gap as B7's missing `--postgres` job. Done 2026-10-06 in the backend repo (1745d9a). `server.idle-timeout-ms` takes the same rule as `header-timeout-ms` beside it (at least 1), and `server.port` must be 0-65535 with 0 documented as any free port, which is how a test starts a server. The two unused bindings were found by looking for a binding whose name never appears again in its file -- exactly two, matching the review's count: `config.zm`'s `val checked = try properties.check_unknown(...)` (a dead bool, since check_unknown always answers `ok(true)` and the `try` was the point) and `service/testdata.zm`'s `val made_of_kind = switch make_kind(...)`, now `if Err(failure) = make_kind(...)`. For the `--fmt --check` job: **there is no CI in any of these repositories**, so the gate went where the project's checks live, `src/test/other/run-all.sh`, the counterpart of ziman's `make check`. It runs before any database work and counts towards the exit code without stopping the tests. It had not existed because it could not pass -- 19 backend source files were unformatted, all of it closing-paren placement, formatted in the same commit after diffing a formatted copy first. Verified by deliberately unformatting a file: the gate names it, the tests still pass, and the script exits 1.
## F. Longer term and documentation (P2)

- [x] **F1** Decide the traits stance (a function-typed bound would unify `_compare`, `_to_json` and `_from_row`). (L P2-2; L) Decided 2026-09-28, no code change: still no general trait/interface system (coherence/orphan rules, supertraits and dynamic dispatch it would need are exactly the class-hierarchy complexity Principle 1 already rejected, and no concrete program needs one). `Eq`/`Hash`/`Ord` are named for what they already are -- a closed, compiler-known "function-typed bound," a fixed signature resolved by the type's own name -- and the list grows by one, `Row`, on the strength of a concrete pain point E9 hit: `Crud<E, Req, Res>` has to plumb `from_row`/`to_params` through every literal as explicit function-value fields because `E: Row` cannot be expressed as a bound. `Json` deliberately stays out (no generic-over-Json function exists anywhere in the library or the reference backend to justify it). `?`'s error conversion (P1-2) needed no part of this: `#[from]` (C2, done) already solved it a different way. See DESIGN.md "The traits/interfaces stance"; the `Row` bound's actual implementation is deferred and tracked in ROADMAP.md's "Larger, needs its own design pass".
- [x] **F2** Interpreter speed: resolve identifiers to slots or compile to closures (a loop is about 28 times slower than CPython; 500-650 requests per second that does not scale with clients). (L P2-1, D; L) Diagnosed 2026-09-28, not fixed: this is two separate problems, not one. The "does not scale with clients" half is the Boehm collector, not the connection pool the developer review also suspected -- `tests/bench/server_concurrency.sh` (new) shows the collector build flat-to-declining across concurrency (347/584/429/361 req/s at c=1/4/8/16) while a `make nogc` build of the same code climbs the whole way (500/1319/1711/2552), 7x faster at c=16; a real multi-connection pool (file-backed SQLite instead of `:memory:`) made no difference, ruling that suspect out. See DESIGN.md "The collector, not the connection pool...". The raw-speed half (28x slower than CPython, `strcmp` identifier lookup) is untouched by this finding and still needs slot resolution or closures/bytecode, independently of whatever the collector ends up costing under concurrency. A tuning pass (same day): a bigger initial heap is a free 30-80% win at every concurrency level with no rebuild (`GC_INITIAL_HEAP_SIZE=512M`), more marker threads add nothing on top of it, and incremental mode is not just slower but catastrophic under concurrent load (~8x, reject it) -- but no configuration closes the gap to `ziman-nogc` (still 4.8x at c=16 even with the best config found), so this needs either less per-request allocation or the deeper memory-reclamation redesign already on the roadmap, not more tuning. See DESIGN.md "A tuning pass on the follow-up above...". Not done: an actual fix for either half; a decision on whether to ship the bigger initial heap as the new default given its RSS cost. Raw-speed half, first fix, 2026-09-28: profiling the loop with `perf record` before writing a resolver found the reviews' own diagnosis was wrong -- `strcmp`/`env_lookup` were under 5% of total time; ~70% was the Boehm collector's own allocation machinery (`GC_build_fl`, `GC_allochblk_nth`, mutex lock/unlock), from a brand-new `Env` (plus its binding array, plus a `strdup`'d loop-binder name) allocated and freed on *every single loop iteration*. Fixed by giving `STMT_WHILE`/`STMT_FOR` one scope for the whole loop, `env_reset` (new, `env.c`) between iterations instead of `env_new`/`env_free` around each one. `tests/bench/loop_bench.zm` (new, the reviews' own 3M-iteration loop): 1.4s to ~0.3s, about 4-5x; `scalar_arrays_bench.zm`: 3.99s to 3.06s, about 25%; `fib_bench.zm` (new, recursive, no loop): unchanged, as expected, since `call_function`'s own per-call scope allocation is a separate, still-open cost. See DESIGN.md "Interpreter speed (F2): the GC, not strcmp, was the loop's real cost". Raw-speed half, second fix, 2026-09-28: slot resolution, done -- `Expr`/`Stmt` gained a `(resolved_depth, resolved_slot)` pair, computed once by the type checker (`env_lookup_resolved`) and used by the interpreter (`env_at_slot`) to jump straight to a name's binding instead of re-comparing strings down the scope chain every time the expression runs. Found three genuine ways the type checker's `Env` tree and the interpreter's independently-built one can disagree on a slot's meaning (a lambda capture, a global val, a capturing lambda's own declared parameter -- each excluded from the fast path rather than fixed, since excluding them costs nothing the benchmarks care about) and one bypass worth closing at the source instead (`defer`'s hidden variables, never `env_define`d at check time though the interpreter defines them at run time -- fixed by making the type checker define them too). Each bug was caught by the full test suite, not a design review: `global_val_basic`, `lambda_basic`, `migrate_basic` each failed with a confidently wrong answer (not a crash) before its respective fix. See DESIGN.md "Slot resolution, and three ways two independently-built Env trees can disagree". `loop_bench.zm`/`fib_bench.zm` re-measured pinned to one core (this machine is a hybrid P-core/E-core Intel chip; an earlier unpinned reading looked like a 2x regression that pinning showed was pure scheduling noise): loop-heavy code (`loop_bench.zm`) improves further on top of the scope-reuse fix (12.964s to 3.385s for 6 pinned runs); call-heavy code with few local lookups per call (`fib_bench.zm`) is statistically unchanged (4.951s vs 4.990s for 6 pinned runs), as expected -- `fib` was never lookup-bound. Raw-speed half, third fix, 2026-09-28: `call_function`'s own costs, done -- profiling `fib_bench.zm` (unchanged by slot resolution, as predicted, since it never crosses that fix's pattern) found `find_function`'s linear scan over every loaded function re-run by name on every single call, and a fresh `Env` allocated per call, the exact per-iteration allocation problem the loop fix above already solved, just for calls instead of iterations. Fixed with `call_fn` (new `Expr` field, ast.h): `check_call` already runs `find_function` once at type-check time to validate the call; caching that result there instead of discarding it means the interpreter never re-scans. Second fix: a per-thread `Env` free-list pool (`env_pool_acquire`/`env_pool_release`) that `call_function` takes from and gives back to instead of `env_new`/`env_free`, safe for recursion since call/return is strictly LIFO per thread. Carefully mirrors `t_defer_envs`'s own existing GC-root pattern (the collector doesn't reliably scan thread-local storage, so the pool array is `GC_MALLOC_UNCOLLECTABLE`, released at the same point `defer_envs_release()` already runs) -- verified safe with `valgrind --leak-check=summary` on the `nogc` build (zero errors for both a plain recursive `fib` and a `spawn`-heavy fixture exercising the thread-exit cleanup path). A third, smaller fix found along the way: skip the ~48-branch builtin-name `strcmp` chain entirely once `call_fn` says definitively this isn't a builtin. See DESIGN.md "Call dispatch: cached, not re-scanned, and a per-call Env pool" -- including a methodological note on a mid-session false signal (system drift on this session's hybrid P-core/E-core machine made an unrelated benchmark look like it was responding to fixes it couldn't have been). Trustworthy final numbers (true pre-session baseline vs. final state, back-to-back, six pinned runs each): `loop_bench.zm` 7.212s to 1.513s (about 4.8x), `fib_bench.zm` 2.650s to 0.660s (about 4.0x). Still not done: the concurrency half (already separately diagnosed above). Re-diagnosed and re-scoped 2026-09-29: re-measured against today's binary (the three raw-speed fixes above narrow the c=16 gap to `ziman-nogc` from 7.1x to about 3.5x combined with heap tuning, real free progress) but the structural problem is unchanged -- confirmed with `GC_PRINT_STATS=1` that collection pauses both get longer and freeze more concurrently-handling threads at once as client count rises, a compounding cost no tuning removes. Also re-verified the collector build does not leak (flat ~65 MB over 10,000 requests vs. `nogc`'s unbounded 3.2 GB) and isn't slow for a single client (1 ms median either way). Design work for the fix -- superseding both prior "per-thread arena" plans in ROADMAP.md, corrected to one independently-collected heap per thread with no dependency on any library's usage pattern -- is written up in full in docs/MEMORY.md. Implementation Phase 1 done 2026-09-29: `spawn`'s `args`/`handler` and `Channel` `send`/`try_send` deep-copy instead of aliasing a pointer across threads (new `value_deep_copy`, `src/value_copy.c`) -- safe to land ahead of the rest of the design since it cannot change observable output under today's allocators, only what crossing costs; verified with the existing suite (828 fixtures, one new covering every `Value` kind) on both builds plus `valgrind`. See DESIGN.md's "Deep-copy at the two thread-crossing points" entry. Phase 2, first increment done 2026-09-29: a standalone `mmap`-backed bump allocator (`src/arena.c`/`.h`, `make test-arena`) -- the per-thread heap itself, landed inert (nothing in the interpreter calls it yet) before the much larger, riskier step of routing allocation sites through it. Its own test caught two real bugs before this went anywhere near the interpreter (an alignment bug from assuming the block header's `sizeof` was already 16-byte aligned, and a zero-size allocation handing out a duplicate pointer). See DESIGN.md's "A standalone arena allocator, landed inert before anything calls it" entry. Also found, before writing any allocation-site-rewiring code: a crossing-point deep copy (Phase 1's `value_deep_copy`) cannot live in either thread's own arena once those exist, since an arena freed "at thread exit" would free memory a still-running thread on the other end of a `spawn`/`Channel` still needs -- resolved in the design (docs/MEMORY.md). Phase 2, second increment done 2026-09-29: `value_deep_copy` now actually allocates into a real destination that resolves that correction -- a second, per-thread "outbound" arena, created lazily and never destroyed, needing no lock since only its own thread ever writes to it. Every `Value` kind except `Map`/`Set` (an explicitly flagged, not-yet-done gap) has its own arena-aware constructor in `src/value_copy.c`. See DESIGN.md's "`value_deep_copy` gets a real, safe destination" entry. A real, serious bug found running the actual backend suite (not this interpreter's own tests): a plain `mmap`'d arena silently breaks native resource handles (a database connection cast to a disguised `int64_t`, kept alive only by Boehm's conservative scanning finding that bit pattern somewhere it looks -- invisible in unscanned arena memory by construction), reproduced by `admin_test.py` hanging then failing migration startup with "invalid or closed connection handle." Fixed with `arena_create_scanned` (`GC_add_roots`/`GC_remove_roots` around every block, in a `ZIMAN_GC` build only) -- binding on every future arena this design creates, not just this one (see MEMORY.md's own new permanent subsection on this). Verified directly: a minimal reproduction (`tests/fixtures/thread_crossing_native_handle.zm`) confirmed to fail with the original error when reverted, pass once fixed. Verified overall: full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds; two stress tests (5,000 sequential sends from one thread, 8 concurrent spawned workers sending 16,000 messages total, 5 repeated runs) clean under `valgrind`; `db_pool_sqlite.zm` run 8 times back to back with identical output; the full `health-record-backend-ziman` suite passes, including the exact `admin_test.py` case that first caught this. Sequencing decision 2026-09-30: skip Phase 2's remaining allocation-site rewiring (a working arena freed only at thread exit doesn't help `server.zm`'s actual thread-per-connection, keep-alive workload at all -- it needs Phase 3's real per-thread collection to reclaim anything during a long-lived thread's life, not just at its end), go straight to Phase 3's design instead of building the rewiring once for no payoff or twice for the same result. Phase 3 design substantially worked through 2026-09-30, not yet implemented: resolved why object headers are unavoidable for precise tracing from a bare pointer; the interior-pointer problem this interpreter's own view-sharing optimizations (string/array slices, `ArrayStore`) create, resolved by making Phase 3 a copying collector that re-copies a view's own bytes rather than reproducing Boehm's `ALL_INTERIOR_POINTERS` block-bookkeeping; and that a from-scratch collector inherits this codebase's own already-documented gap (thread-local storage isn't reliably scanned, why `t_defer_envs`/`t_env_pool` are already `GC_MALLOC_UNCOLLECTABLE`) for its own `__thread` state, meaning roots need explicit enumeration, not a stack scan alone -- the same class of mistake as the native-handle bug just above, recorded before it's found the same way twice. Root-finding and the collection trigger reuse existing code (`stack.c`'s stack-bounds computation, `arena_reserved`) rather than inventing new bookkeeping. See MEMORY.md's "Phase 3 design, worked through before writing any code" for the full reasoning. Still comparable in size to writing a small garbage collector from scratch -- this is that collector's actual shape now, not a placeholder description of one. Phase 3, first implementation increment done 2026-09-30: object headers (`src/gc_object.c`/`.h`, `make test-gc-object`) -- a thin layer on `arena.c` giving every allocation a `{kind, size, marked}` header the eventual mark/copy phases will read from a bare pointer, landed inert (nothing calls it yet) with its own 11-check standalone test, same discipline `arena.c` itself was built with. See DESIGN.md's "Object headers, the foundation Phase 3's mark/copy phases will need" entry. Phase 3, second implementation increment done 2026-09-30: the copy/relocate primitive (`src/gc_copy.c`/`.h`, `make test-gc-copy`) -- recognized this is mostly `value_copy.c`'s own per-kind recursive-copy logic already, generalized onto `gc_object.c`'s headers (`gc_alloc` instead of bare `arena_alloc`) with a dedup map (`GcDedup`, same hash/probe scheme as `src/handles.c`'s `HandleSet`) added to preserve sharing across a copy -- needed here and not in `value_copy.c` because a value surviving many collection cycles over a thread's life would otherwise duplicate a shared substructure a little more every cycle. Found a second, broader instance of the interior-pointer problem while designing this (not debugging it): `array_obj_view` shares its parent's buffers at an offset the same way a substring shares its parent's bytes, so string bytes, a packed array's own buffers, and any `Value[]` array (`boxed_items`/`fields`/`captured`) are all deliberately never deduplicated, always copied fresh, extending the same tradeoff already accepted for strings uniformly rather than re-litigating it per case; a packed array's raw buffers turn out not to need this in practice since `gc_copy_array` always reads through `array_get` and rebuilds boxed, same simplification `value_copy.c` already made. `Map`/`Set` inherit the same not-yet-`gc_alloc`-routed gap `value_copy.c` already had; `Channel` is shared by identity, never copied, same as `value_copy.c`. See DESIGN.md's "The copy/relocate primitive" entry. Verified: a new 35-check standalone test (`tests/unit/gc_copy_test.c`) exercising every `Value` kind, the interior-pointer ambiguity directly (a string and a same-start-address shorter view of it both copy correctly and independently), and sharing preservation directly (a shared struct/array reachable from two places dedups to one copy, not two) -- all pass; `valgrind` reports zero self-introduced leaks (only the pre-existing, by-design, permanent-AST `Type` allocations remain, same category every `Type` the real interpreter builds also leaves in place); full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds, confirming this compiles into the wildcarded source tree with no effect yet, since nothing outside its own test calls it. Still ahead, not yet scoped into an implementation estimate: root-finding (a real stack scan feeding real roots into this), the collection trigger, and wiring a full cycle end to end. Phase 3, third implementation increment done 2026-09-30: `src/gc_lookup.c`/`.h` (`make test-gc-lookup`), resolving a bare candidate address back to the object it falls within or inside -- the piece root-finding actually needs, since a stack scan finds untyped words with no length/kind attached, unlike `gc_copy.c`'s tracing which always starts from an already-typed `Value`. Found a real gap while scoping the stack scan itself, not while debugging it: `Value.as.str.ptr` (a string view) is the one heap-referencing `Value` field embedded with no wrapper object around it, so a transient string-view `Value` sitting only as a C-stack local could present a scanner with a bare interior pointer that, unresolved, would make the collector discard a buffer a live view still points into -- a real memory-safety gap, not a missed optimization. Resolved without reproducing Boehm's own general interior-pointer machinery (only this one shape needs it) and without a separate index (new `gc_object.c` primitive `gc_next_object` walks an arena's own contiguous, self-describing `gc_alloc` layout directly, via new `arena.c` accessors `arena_first_block`/`arena_next_block`/`arena_block_content`). See DESIGN.md's "Resolving a bare stack word back to an object" entry. Verified: 18-check standalone test covering exact/interior matches, non-matches (before any content, NULL, an unrelated stack address, a masquerading integer), alignment-padding correctness, and walking across multiple `mmap`'d blocks, all passing first run; `valgrind` clean; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds, confirming this and `arena.c`/`gc_object.c`'s new accessors have no effect on anything yet. Phase 3, fourth implementation increment done 2026-09-30: the conservative stack scan itself (`src/gc_scan.c`/`.h`, `make test-gc-scan`) -- scans the calling thread's own C stack, word by word from near the current stack pointer up to the thread's real upper bound (new `stack.c` accessor `ziman_stack_bounds`, reusing the codebase's own existing "address of a local is the stack pointer" idiom rather than a new technique), feeding each word through `gc_lookup` and reporting whatever resolves to a real object. Always the calling thread's own stack, never another's -- this design's entire premise (one heap per thread, collected on its own time) means there is never a need to pause or inspect any other thread's stack, so no locking or suspension machinery exists here at all. The one module in this effort that cannot be fully `valgrind`-clean, and deliberately documented as such rather than glossed over: conservative scanning reads stack memory the current call chain may never have written and treats it as a possible pointer, which valgrind correctly flags as "uninitialised value used" -- the same interaction Boehm's own collector has with valgrind, not a defect. Verified this is the only thing found by re-running with `--undef-value-errors=no` (every other memcheck category left on): zero errors. See DESIGN.md's "The actual conservative stack scan" entry. Verified overall: a 6-check standalone test (using `volatile` locals so a real pointer is actually written to stack memory rather than kept only in a register at this codebase's own `-O2` setting), covering an exact-start root, an interior-pointer root (the scenario `gc_lookup.c` exists for), and the same object referenced twice, run five times deliberately since stack contents vary run to run, all passing; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead, not yet scoped into an implementation estimate: the collection trigger and wiring a full cycle end to end -- deciding when to run, walking from real interpreter roots instead of a test's synthetic ones, and actually relocating survivors before discarding the old arena. Design reversal 2026-09-30: Phase 3 switches from copying to mark-sweep, found while scoping the collection trigger, not while implementing it. A conservative stack scan's own roots can never safely be relocated -- rewriting an ambiguous stack word that turns out not to be a real pointer would corrupt real data, the identical wall Boehm hits, resolved the same way (never move anything). A cheaper-looking alternative (precise root-finding via this interpreter's own existing call-frame tracking, restricting collection to statement boundaries) was worked through and ruled out on an ordinary case, not a contrived one: in `f(x) + g(y)`, while `g(y)` runs, `f(x)`'s already-computed value sits only as a transient C local in the outer expression evaluator, invisible to an `Env`-chain-only root walk -- fixing that properly would mean an explicit operand-stack VM instead of tree-walking recursion, far outside scope. Confirmed with the project owner across two rounds of discussion given the size of this fork. `gc_object.c`'s header (the `marked` bit especially) and `gc_scan.c`'s report-only callback both carry over unchanged -- neither was actually built assuming relocation. `gc_copy.c` is no longer Phase 3's collector (nothing moves under mark-sweep) but remains correct, tested logic that may still serve `value_copy.c`'s own thread-crossing case later, undecided. New: `src/gc_heap.c`/`.h` (`make test-gc-heap`) -- the one genuinely new mechanism mark-sweep needs, since `arena.c` can only bump-allocate and mass-free a whole arena, never reclaim one dead object's space individually. Segregated free lists by power-of-two total footprint (new `gc_header_span` accessor keeps the header's real size private to `gc_object.c`); `gc_heap_sweep` walks every object via the same `arena_block_content`/`gc_next_object` primitives `gc_lookup.c` already established, reclaiming unmarked objects and clearing survivors' mark bits. New `GcKind` value `GC_FREE` marks a reclaimed chunk so `gc_lookup` resolving a stray stack word to one is never mistaken for a live object. A real bug caught by reasoning before coding, not by testing after: a freed chunk's free-list link needs its own payload bytes to live in, which a near-zero-byte allocation (a real shape, `GC_VALUES`'s own empty-array case) doesn't have -- fixed with a minimum effective payload of `sizeof(void*)` before computing a size class. Also corrected in MEMORY.md: the interior-pointer problem's original "make Phase 3 a copying collector" resolution is superseded -- a non-moving collector instead resolves a string view's interior pointer through `gc_lookup.c` (built for a different reason, reused here) before marking the buffer it actually points into. Verified: 17-check standalone test (simulating a mark phase with direct `gc_set_marked` calls, the same discipline every earlier layer here was proven with first) passing first run after the fix; `valgrind` clean; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead: the real mark phase (reusing `gc_copy.c`'s own per-kind traversal structure), the collection trigger, and wiring a full end-to-end cycle. Mark phase done 2026-09-30: `src/gc_mark.c`/`.h` (`make test-gc-mark`) -- largely `gc_copy.c`'s own per-kind traversal, allocate-and-recurse replaced by idempotent mark-and-recurse (check `gc_is_marked`, skip if already set, the mark-bit equivalent of `gc_copy.c`'s own dedup map, same reason: don't retrace a shared substructure once per incoming reference). `gc_lookup.c`, built for a conservative stack scan's candidate words, turns out to be exactly the fix the interior-pointer problem needs here too -- marking can't sidestep it by copying fresh bytes the way `gc_copy.c` does (there's no copy being made), so a string's `str.ptr` or an array's `boxed_items`/packed buffer now resolves through `gc_lookup` before marking, or `gc_heap_sweep` would reclaim a buffer a live view still points into. Corrects MEMORY.md's earlier "make Phase 3 a copying collector" interior-pointer resolution, now superseded by the mark-sweep pivot, in place. An explicitly flagged, not-yet-measured performance question: `gc_lookup` was designed for a stack scan's handful of calls per cycle, not necessarily one call per string/view found tracing an entire graph -- left as-is until a real cycle exists to measure, not prematurely optimized. Also noted, not fixed: `gc_copy.c` classifies a boxed BigNum/Decimal wrapper as `GC_ATOMIC` even though it holds a `digits` pointer -- harmless today since nothing branches on `GcKind` to decide whether to trace further (`gc_mark.c` dispatches on the Ziman-level `Value.type.kind` instead), worth revisiting only if something later needs to trace from a bare `GcKind` header alone. Verified: 18-check standalone test (every `Value` kind, the interior-pointer case, an empty array, a shared-substructure termination case, a `Channel` left untouched) passing first run; `valgrind` clean; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead: the collection trigger, and wiring `gc_scan_stack_roots` + `gc_mark_value` + `gc_heap_sweep` into one real end-to-end cycle against actual interpreter roots. First real end-to-end cycle done 2026-09-30: `gc_mark_object` (new, `gc_mark.c`) bridges `gc_scan_stack_roots`'s bare-pointer roots (no Value/Type.kind available, only a GcKind header) to the same per-kind marking logic, dispatching on `gc_kind_of` instead -- including the real, not hypothetical, case of a bare `GC_VALUES` buffer found mid-construction with no wrapper around it yet, traced via `gc_size_of(obj)/sizeof(Value)` since that's the only way to know its count at all. `gc_heap_collect` (new, `gc_heap.c`) wires scan→mark→sweep into one call, skipping the whole cycle (not just the scan) if the thread's stack can't be measured -- same graceful degradation as `ziman_stack_slow`. Explicitly not yet a complete root set (this codebase's own thread-local state -- `t_defer_envs`/`t_env_pool`, `error.c`'s frame stack -- isn't walked as an explicit root source yet, the gap already flagged in MEMORY.md's own Phase 3 design notes before any of this was built) and not yet safe for real interpreter wiring for that reason; built and tested anyway to prove the pieces compose first. The actual milestone: a real graph (array containing struct containing string) reachable through one `volatile` stack root survives a genuine scan→mark→sweep cycle with its content fully intact, while an unreferenced object's slot gets reclaimed and reused -- the first point in this whole effort where "does this work end to end" has a tested answer, not a plan. A real test bug (not a code bug) caught along the way: comparing a dead object's address to a local read after `gc_heap_collect` forces the compiler to keep it live across the call, spilling it onto the exact stack the scan reads, deterministically (not the usual run-to-run staleness) -- fixed by stashing the address in a file-scope static instead. Verified: `gc_mark_test.c` grew to 24 checks, `gc_heap_test.c` to 22, both rerun five times with zero failures; `valgrind` clean; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead: the collection trigger, the explicit thread-local root walk, and wiring a real per-thread GcHeap into the interpreter's own allocation sites. Second root source found broader than expected, 2026-09-30: working out what "the explicit thread-local root walk" needed to cover before writing it found an `Env` (env.h) is never allocated in a thread's GcHeap arena at all -- it lives on the ordinary allocator's own heap, so the moment a Value is stored into a binding, its pointer fields move off the C stack (where a conservative scan could see them) into Env.items' own heap storage, invisible to a scan that only reads stack bytes and never dereferences what it finds. This affects every ordinary variable in the interpreter, not just t_defer_envs/t_env_pool (the narrower shape originally flagged) -- those two are a real instance of the same underlying problem, not the whole of it. New: src/gc_env_roots.c/.h, `gc_mark_env_chain` -- marks every Value bound in an Env and walks ->parent iteratively up to wherever the chain terminates. Built and tested standalone, before touching t_defer_envs/t_env_pool/error.c's frame tracking or the interpreter at all. Still ahead, not resolved by this increment: wiring into error.c's frame tracking (each active call's own Env*, not just one handed to it directly), walking t_defer_envs/t_env_pool the same way, g_global_env once globals route through a GcHeap (Phase 4/5, still design-only), and gc_heap_collect itself doesn't call this yet. Verified: 7-check standalone test (two-scope chain, scalar binding, no-parent scope, empty scope, a structural value composing with gc_mark_value's own recursion) passing first run; valgrind clean; full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. error.c frame tracking wired 2026-09-30: ZimanFrame gains a void* env (untyped -- error.c is shared by the lexer/parser/type checker too), set by new ziman_set_frame_env right after call_function creates its own Env (native calls leave it NULL, they have none). Read back via ziman_frame_count/ziman_frame_env_at rather than exposing the frame array, since env isn't contiguous across frames. The first change in this whole effort to touch interpreter.c/error.c themselves rather than land as a new, uncalled module -- runs on every function call now, on the real hot path, even though nothing reads it yet. Verified accordingly: full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds, and the real health-record-backend-ziman suite (19 Python files, 823 checks, including admin_test.py's own account-deletion-with-medications case) passes against a freshly built binary -- this session's standing rule for anything touching interpreter behavior. Still ahead: actually calling gc_mark_env_chain for every frame this exposes, plus g_global_env and t_defer_envs/t_env_pool, from a real collection cycle -- gc_heap_collect itself is unchanged by this increment, and the collection trigger and interpreter allocation-site wiring remain fully ahead. gc_heap_collect gains the seam for that second root source 2026-09-30, without learning what it is: a third parameter, extra_roots(const Arena *a, void *ctx) + ctx, called after the stack scan and before sweeping if non-NULL. Not a direct call into interpreter.c's own g_global_env/t_defer_envs/t_env_pool (genuinely private, and gc_heap.c has stayed decoupled from any specific caller's state through every increment so far) -- same shape gc_scan_stack_roots's own visit callback already uses. Every existing caller still passes NULL today, a real, explicitly documented gap, not an oversight. Proven with a genuine case: a string bound in an Env, referenced by nothing else, survives a real gc_heap_collect cycle specifically because the new callback (calling gc_mark_env_chain) runs. Verified: gc_heap_test.c grew to 24 checks, rerun five times with zero failures, valgrind clean, full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead: the actual interpreter.c-side caller supplying a complete extra_roots (global env, every frame's env, t_defer_envs/t_env_pool), the collection trigger, and wiring a real per-thread GcHeap into the interpreter's own allocation sites. The interpreter's own root sources aggregated, 2026-09-30: src/gc_interp_roots.c/.h, gc_mark_interpreter_roots -- marks the global scope, every active call's own frame (error.c's frame tracking), and t_defer_envs/t_env_pool, in one call. A real practical constraint shaped its signature: interpreter.c cannot be linked into a standalone unit test (it pulls in every native module, the parser, the type checker, essentially the whole program), so reading g_global_env/t_defer_envs/t_env_pool directly would have made this untestable except by exercising the real interpreter end to end. Fixed by taking that state as explicit parameters instead -- a synthetic Env**/count pair exercises the identical code path a real one would; active call frames are the one piece still read directly via error.c's already-general accessors. interpreter.c's eventual wiring becomes a thin wrapper passing its own state through. A verification bar this function can't fully clear alone, stated plainly: the standalone test proves the aggregation logic against synthetic stand-ins, not that interpreter.c will one day call it with the right state -- that last mile comes once a real per-thread GcHeap, a trigger, and the actual call_function/interpret wiring exist. Verified: 6-check standalone test (global scope, two nested active frames each with their own binding, a native call's frame with no Env, a tracked defer scope, an empty pooled Env) passing first run; valgrind fully clean (this test's type_scalar-only Values allocate nothing); full suite (829 fixtures) unchanged on both builds. Still ahead, the last remaining pieces: the collection trigger, creating a real per-thread GcHeap, and wiring interpreter.c's own allocation sites to use it. The narrowest possible slice, wired for real, and a real live bug it caught before anything shipped, 2026-09-30: src/gc_thread_heap.c (new) owns a per-thread GcHeap*; value_string_copy (value.c) now allocates through it, deliberately the one, smallest, most self-contained allocation site; a collection trigger checks a threshold at the top of exec_stmt -- the one point in this interpreter's execution proven safe from the EXPR_CALL-style temporary-buffer root-source gap (a malloc'd args array holding Values neither the stack scan nor the Env-chain walk can see into, but fully unwound and freed by the time any statement's own evaluation finishes -- verified by reading the code before writing any of this wiring, not assumed). The bug: struct Opts { flag: bool }, crossed through spawn, printed true instead of false -- deterministic, confirmed absent on the prior commit, confirmed to disappear when the trigger was disabled with everything else left wired. Root cause: the mark phase assumed every structural object it reached had been built by gc_alloc (a real header) -- false for a struct crossing spawn, built by value_copy.c's own bare arena_alloc with no header at all, and in fact false for every structural object anywhere in the interpreter at the time, since only value_string_copy had been migrated. gc_set_marked/gc_is_marked, called directly on such a pointer, wrote into whatever bytes actually preceded it -- here, the struct's own adjacent bool field, flipping it. A latent, universal gap in gc_mark.c's per-kind functions from the moment gc_mark_env_chain could reach any real Env binding, not something specific to thread-crossing; the crossing case only happened to be the first path exercised. mark_interior_capable (strings, buffers) never showed this, since it already resolves through gc_lookup first and only marks a confirmed, non-NULL result. Fix: a new is_gc_heap_object(a, ptr) (gc_lookup(a, ptr) == ptr) gates every direct mark-bit touch in gc_mark_array/_struct/_result/_enum/_closure and the boxed BigNum/Decimal cases -- when false, the object's own header is never touched, but its fields are still traced regardless, since a plain, not-yet-migrated object can still hold a field that is real gc_heap content. Costs the already-visited idempotency guard for header-less objects specifically (safe: the graph is a DAG) and adds a second, unmeasured gc_lookup cost on top of the one already flagged for interior-pointer-capable buffers. Verified as a real fix: the minimal repro reproduces true with the guards reverted, false restored, five runs each way; a permanent regression fixture, tests/fixtures/gc_collect_preserves_thread_crossed_value.zm (a spawned worker reading 3,000 lines via fs.lines to reliably cross the trigger threshold before checking its own received struct), does the identical before/after discrimination, the same discipline thread_crossing_native_handle.zm already established for the earlier native-handle bug. Also fixed proactively while root-causing this: t_heap is __thread-reachable only, the exact shape that already required GC_MALLOC_UNCOLLECTABLE for t_defer_envs/t_env_pool -- gc_heap_create now allocates the GcHeap struct itself the same way. Verified overall: all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; full suite 830/830 on both builds (829 plus the new fixture); the real health-record-backend-ziman suite (823 checks) passes; valgrind against the real interpreter running the new fixture reports zero errors. Still open: the EXPR_CALL-style temporary-buffer gap remains real for any future trigger point less restrictive than "only at exec_stmt's own entry"; migrating any allocation site beyond value_string_copy will need the identical is_gc_heap_object care, not assumed safe by extension. Still ahead: re-measuring against the concurrency benchmark once more allocation sites migrate, and migrating them (array/struct construction, then env_new/call frames, per the narrowest-possible-slice sequencing). That gap closed for real 2026-09-30, after migrating struct_obj_new (the natural next allocation site) broke two fixtures immediately: net_dropped_socket_reclaimed (a timing issue, resolved) and, more seriously, csv_extended with "internal error: field 'cells' not found at runtime" -- real corruption, confirming the temporary-buffer gap flagged as "still open" wasn't theoretical. EXPR_STRUCT_LIT's fields[] (and EXPR_CALL's args[], EXPR_ARRAY_LIT's items[], EXPR_WITH's fields[]) is a plain malloc'd buffer populated one element at a time by repeated eval_expr calls; if evaluating one element recurses into a call, that call's own body can trigger maybe_collect while earlier elements sit unrooted. Never specific to struct_obj_new: the already-shipped value_string_copy wiring has the identical exposure, just unlikely to be hit in practice. The audit stayed bounded by finding the real choke points: only three files (prelude.c, iter.c, interpreter.c) ever call back into Ziman at all, and call_function is never called outside interpreter.c, so guarding call_closure_value/native_call_closure's own internal call_function invocation protects every native callback automatically (including a native's own args[], never bound into an Env at all). interpreter.c's own four EXPR_* sites plus one inline map_filter/set_filter loop needed their own narrower guards -- six guarded regions total, via a new t_gc_unsafe_depth counter (gc_unsafe_enter/leave) maybe_collect checks. A second, milder bug from the same migration: net.Socket's own StructObj wrapper is now gc_heap-allocated into an arena Boehm also scans, and gc_heap_sweep never zeroed a reclaimed object's payload, so a disguised socket-handle address in an already-dead struct kept looking live to Boehm's scan. Fixed by zeroing payloads on sweep and adding ziman_force_collect() so os.collect_garbage() runs a real Phase 3 collection before Boehm's own GC_gcollect(). A valgrind finding (Invalid read inside Boehm's own GC_mark_from) was investigated and confirmed pre-existing (thread_crossing_native_handle.zm, which predates this session's Phase 3 work, shows the identical pattern), not caused by this increment. Verified: full suite 830/830 on both builds, stable across five repeated runs; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; the real health-record-backend-ziman suite (823 checks) passes; valgrind clean beyond the confirmed pre-existing Boehm interaction. array_obj_new migrated next (the wrapper only), after a zero-fill fix (gc_heap_alloc's reused-chunk path never cleared the free-list link written into a dead object's own payload bytes; GcHeap itself now GC_MALLOC_UNCOLLECTABLE for the same __thread-scanning reason as t_defer_envs/t_env_pool). The migration segfaulted spawn_defer_gc_stress.zm -- root-caused via a gdb backtrace to a fundamental gap in the second root source: ziman_set_frame_env only ever records a call's top-level Env (one call site), and gc_mark_env_chain only ever walks a recorded Env's parent chain upward, so a variable declared inside any nested for/while/if/block scope has been invisible to root-finding since this frame tracking was first built. Fixed in exec_stmts_in_scope (the single function every nested scope's execution funnels through): it now records the currently-executing scope for the frame, not just the call's own top one, restoring the saved value on exit. See DESIGN.md "array_obj_new migrates, and a fundamental gap...". Verified against the exact crashing fixture (3 clean runs) and the other fixture it broke (regex_cache, confirmed matching); full suite 830/830 stable across five runs on both builds; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass. Running the real health-record-backend-ziman suite next (the standing rule) failed all 823 checks at once -- a third root-source gap, found by the app itself: ArrayBuilder/MapBuilder/SetBuilder's own backing buffer (a plain malloc'd buffer reached only through a disguised int64_t handle in the builder's wrapper struct) is invisible to both existing root sources, so an appended element was only as safe as whatever else independently referenced it -- true only for the loop-local that built it. lib/http/server.zm's routes<C> builds an ArrayBuilder<Pattern> across ~100+ routes at start-up; once Pattern's own struct wrapper was gc_heap-migrated, a real collection landing mid-loop swept every earlier-appended route pattern. Fixed with a new seam, gc_mark_register_struct_content_marker (gc_mark.c/.h): a struct type registers a content-marker by name, called unconditionally (never gated by the struct's own mark bit) whenever the mark phase visits a TY_STRUCT value of that name; prelude.c registers one for ArrayBuilder and one (shared) for MapBuilder/SetBuilder. Ordinary (non-builder) Map/Set values were already safe -- a pre-existing gc_mark.c code path already walks every entry regardless of the MapObj's own header status. See DESIGN.md "A third root-source gap...". Verified: both minimal reproductions pass; the fix confirmed to actually discriminate (reverted via git stash, reproduced the failure, restored); a new permanent regression fixture (gc_arraybuilder_contents_survive_collection.zm) added and confirmed to discriminate the same way; the real health-record-backend-ziman suite now passes in full (19 files, ~950 checks, sqlite mode); full suite 831/831 (with the new fixture) on both builds; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; valgrind clean beyond the same confirmed pre-existing Boehm interaction (more occurrences only because spawn_defer_gc_stress.zm spawns 8 threads, each its own call-stack context for the identical pattern). Re-running the concurrency benchmark for the first time since any gc_heap migration shipped, the server hard-abort()-ed at c=16: Boehm's own GC_add_roots root-set table has a fixed capacity (MAX_ROOT_SETS, 2048), consumed forever by two independent, pre-existing leaks -- ziman_thread_heap()'s own per-thread GcHeap was never destroyed at thread exit (fixed: ziman_thread_heap_release(), called from spawn_thread_entry alongside every other __thread release already there), and value_copy.c's own "outbound arena," documented as deliberately leaked since the day it was built pending "a real reclamation story." Given the severity and the real design tradeoffs involved, asked how to proceed (raise MAX_ROOT_SETS as a stopgap, design real reclamation, or stop and reassess) -- chose real reclamation. value_deep_copy (one __thread arena, reused forever) became value_copy_send/value_copy_receive: each crossing value (or group -- a spawn's args plus its on_error handler) gets its own dedicated arena, lazily created only if something actually needs one, destroyed once the receiving side has its own independent copy. ChannelObj's queue now carries {Value, owner_arena} pairs, finalized transparently on every receive-side function -- no receive-side interpreter.c call site needed to change. This surfaced a second, genuinely new bug, found only empirically (a debug print made a real race vanish, confirmed by ten clean reruns): a spawned thread's pending on_error handler, once safely re-copied, was referenced by nothing but a plain malloc'd struct until thread_failed read it -- safe by accident under the old design (Boehm scans every GC_MALLOC'd block, no explicit rooting needed), a real gap the moment that string moved onto this thread's own gc_heap. Fixed with t_pending_handler, a new root source mirroring t_defer_envs/t_env_pool's own shape. See DESIGN.md's "A fourth root-source gap becomes a hard crash" entry. Verified: the fixture that caught the handler race passes ten runs in a row; both try_send failure paths (closed, full) confirmed to report correctly and not leak their crossing arena (valgrind --leak-check=full, 0 bytes definitely lost); full suite 831/831 stable across five runs on both builds; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; valgrind clean on the fixture this bug was found through. And, for the first time since any gc_heap migration shipped: tests/bench/server_concurrency.sh against the real health-record-backend-ziman app completes all four concurrency levels with zero failures and no crash (c=1 474 req/s, c=4 773, c=8 689, c=16 454) -- fixing the crash was the prerequisite for measuring the benchmark at all, not a fix for the underlying declining-past-its-peak throughput shape it was built to diagnose, which is still present and still ahead. "env_new/call frames next" turned out narrower than it sounded: every call site re-read first, found call_function's own top-level scope and every loop kind already reuse one scope across calls/iterations (env_pool_acquire/release, env_reset) -- the one unpooled, per-execution scope left anywhere was exec_block (if/else bodies, bare blocks). Flagged the re-scoping and chose pooling that (reusing the exact same env_pool_acquire/release pair call_function already has) over a full Env-to-gc_heap redesign, then re-measuring. One-line change; full suite and the real app both still pass. Re-measuring found it didn't move the needle -- three concurrency-benchmark runs at c=16 (454, 432, 528 req/s) show more swing between consecutive runs of the identical binary than any effect from the change. Rather than guess at another allocation site blind, profiled the real server under real concurrent load with perf record -g --call-graph dwarf (wrapping the server as perf's own child -- attaching to an already-running PID was silently restricted in this environment). Confirms F2's own original diagnosis in current numbers: GC_mark_from alone is 18% of sampled CPU time; Boehm's own locking/bookkeeping around it another ~26%; this session's own gc_mark_* functions (triggered because string/struct/array are migrated now) another ~7% -- close to half of all CPU time under this workload is garbage-collection-related. env_define/env_lookup/exec_stmts_in_scope together are under 1.3% of the same profile, direct confirmation Env was never where the volume lives. Caller attribution for Boehm's own allocation came back too fragmented across many small call paths to single out one obvious next site the way string/struct/array each were in turn. A second, separate, already-acknowledged cost (__strcmp_avx2 + native_lookup, ~11-13%) is F2's other half -- name-based native dispatch, not yet covered by env_lookup_resolved's own fix for ordinary variables -- explicitly out of scope here. See DESIGN.md's "'env_new/call frames' turns out to be narrower..." entry. Picked result_obj_new/enum_obj_new as the next migration anyway (the next simple wrapper constructors still on the ordinary allocator). Mechanical change, same as struct_obj_new/array_obj_new -- and csv_records.zm segfaulted immediately. Root cause: iter.c's FromState/ArrayIterState (the native state behind iter_from/iter_of_array) hold whatever Values their own closures/arrays were built with, reachable only through it->state, a plain calloc'd struct invisible to both root sources -- the identical shape to ArrayBuilder's own gap, fixed the same way (gc_mark_register_struct_content_marker, registered for "Iter"). That alone didn't fix it: the registered marker only fires from the type-directed path, and the outer Iter in a for-loop is a bare C local, found only by the type-less stack scan. Tried binding it into the loop's own Env instead; this corrupted a different program entirely -- the type checker's own env_lookup_resolved had already assigned every binding in that scope a fixed compile-time slot index with no knowledge an extra runtime-only binding would ever exist, silently shifting every later binding's real slot by one. Caught by the failure's own shape changing (garbage stack memory, not a GC_FREE header) -- a different signature was itself the sign one bug got traded for another, not fixed. The real fix: t_active_iters, a __thread stack of the Iter(s) a for-loop is currently driving on this thread, mirroring t_defer_envs's own exact shape, marked directly rather than touching Env/slot machinery at all. See DESIGN.md's "result_obj_new/enum_obj_new migrate..." entry. Verified: the crashing fixture passes clean fifteen runs in a row; a new dedicated regression fixture (gc_iter_state_survives_collection.zm) confirmed to discriminate; full suite 832/832 stable across five runs on both builds; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; the real health-record-backend-ziman suite passes in full; valgrind clean on the new fixture beyond the same pre-existing Boehm interaction (a separate, pre-existing, unrelated getline-buffer leak in csv_records.zm was checked against the pre-migration baseline and confirmed to already exist there). Took on Map/Set next -- a bigger, costlier step than the first four (MapObj wraps a persistent hash trie, MapNode/Leaf, not a flat struct; needed a real new GcKind and mark-phase logic, uncertain payoff since only the 24-byte wrapper migrates). Flagged this explicitly before starting; chose to go ahead. New GC_MAP kind, gc_mark_map mirroring every other per-kind marker, wired into both dispatch paths from the start (a direct lesson from the Iter entry: a kind reachable from only one path is a kind with a real gap). Four allocation sites migrated (mapobj_empty/set/remove/snapshot); mapobj_mut_new (the builder-private MapObj behind MapBuilder/SetBuilder) reasoned to need no migration since it's never wrapped in a TY_MAP Value directly -- missed that mapobj_mut_new is just "return mapobj_empty();", the function just migrated. Two fixtures (map_set_builders.zm, http_endpoints.zm) immediately segfaulted: GC_realloc crashing on a root pointer read out of an already-zeroed wrapper. Root cause: prelude.c's mb_mark_contents (the content-marker already registered for MapBuilder/SetBuilder since the ArrayBuilder entry) only ever traced the map's entries, never the wrapper's own header -- the third time this session found a native-handle-held Value invisible to both root sources, but the first time the handle itself, not something it points to, needed protecting. Fixed by wrapping buf->m in a throwaway TY_MAP Value and calling the already-public gc_mark_value, reusing gc_mark_map's own header handling rather than duplicating it. See DESIGN.md's "Map/Set's own wrapper migrates..." entry, including a related pre-existing inconsistency found (not fixed, out of scope) in gc_copy.c's own dead-code Map/Set handling. Verified: both broken fixtures pass clean ten runs each; a new dedicated regression fixture confirmed to discriminate; full suite 833/833 stable across five runs on both builds; all nine standalone gc_* unit tests pass; the real health-record-backend-ziman suite passes in full; valgrind clean beyond the same pre-existing Boehm interaction. The concurrency benchmark, run twice: every level at or above the best of the three pre-migration runs, c=8/c=16 by a margin clearly outside the established noise band (689->802-843 req/s, 528->591 req/s) -- a real improvement. Not done: deciding what to migrate next -- MapNode/Leaf (the trie itself, where a map's real memory lives, still unmigrated) is one candidate. **Closed 2026-10-05, as the task this entry actually states, and split: the raw-speed half is done and the concurrency half is now F7.** Measured against F2's own stated criterion ("a loop is about 28 times slower than CPython"), pinned to one core against CPython 3.14: `tests/bench/loop_bench.zm` 0.28s vs 0.16s, **1.75x**, down from the ~28x the two reviews reported. That came from four fixes in sequence -- per-loop scope reuse, slot resolution (`env_lookup_resolved`), cached call dispatch plus the per-thread `Env` pool, and finally name-based native dispatch, which this entry had itself deferred as "F2's other half ... explicitly out of scope here": `native_lookup` was a linear `strcmp` scan over all 247 registered natives run on *every* native call (1,964,293 comparisons in one `yaml_basic` run, average scan depth 205), now resolved once per declaration and cached on the `FunctionDecl`, which took `__strcmp_avx2` + `native_lookup` from 26.3% of a `csv_extended` profile to absent. See DESIGN.md's "Native dispatch stops being a 247-entry `strcmp` scan per call" entry. **One raw-speed gap this entry never named, recorded rather than closed with it:** call-heavy code is still about 8x CPython (`fib(30)` 0.43s vs 0.06s), because `fib` was never lookup-bound and nothing since the `Env` pool has addressed per-call cost. Worth its own task if it ever matters; it is not what F2 asked for.
- [x] **F3** UTF-8 correctness, unified error conventions, visibility for library internals, bit operators, local and recursive lambdas. (L P2-3 to P2-8; M each) P2-4 (unified error conventions) done 2026-09-28: a new `error_message(e)` builtin dispatches to `<snake_case_type>_message` by the enum's own name, the identical naming-convention lookup `compare`'s `Ord` dispatch already uses -- `NetError`/`DbError`/`JwtError`/`ClientError` (renamed from bare `error_message`, which the new builtin now reserves, back to `client_error_message`) all reach it uniformly, no module qualifier needed, and a user's own error enum joins in with nothing but a same-shaped function. `HttpError` deliberately stays out (it answers with a `Response`, not a string, a different job). See DESIGN.md "A unified error_message builtin"; fixtures `error_message_dispatch`, `error_message_unsupported_type_rejected`, `error_message_redefine_builtin_rejected`. `unit` (the other named part of P2-4, "unit for no-information successes") was already done independently before this session. P2-4's third clause ("one policy for programmer errors") done 2026-09-29: adopted the review's own proposal exactly -- a programmer error (a bug in the calling code's own logic) is always fatal, never a `Result` a caller could forget to check; a `Result` stays reserved for errors that genuinely come from outside the program. `collections.sort_by_keys`/`sort_by_keys_desc` were the one real violation (a length mismatch between two arrays the same program built is never "bad external data"), changed from `Result<[T], string>` to a plain `[T]`, fatal on mismatch via the same idiom `server.param` already uses. Simplified a real caller along the way: `migrate.zm`'s two call sites were switching on a `Result` outcome that was already structurally impossible (its own `versions` array is `collections.map` over the exact `items` being sorted, so the lengths can never differ), removing dead defensive code the old signature had been quietly encouraging. Searched the rest of the library for the same shape of violation and found none -- every other `Result`-returning function checked parses genuinely external, untrusted text. See DESIGN.md "Programmer errors are fatal"; fixtures `sort_by_keys_length_mismatch_rejected`, `sort_by_keys_desc_length_mismatch_rejected` (existing `collections_sort`/`collections_sort_desc`/`collections_extras`/`ord_option_result_array`/`map_set_helpers` updated for the new signature). **P2-4 is now fully done.** P2-5 (visibility for library internals) done 2026-09-28: a third visibility level, `internal` (`pub`/`internal`/private, in ascending order of reach, a C#-style scheme chosen over the review's own `pub(package)` suggestion after that specific syntax turned out to break its own motivating case -- `net.zm`/`lib/core` and `client.zm`/`lib/http` share internals across different directories, so a directory-based "package" would leave them unable to see each other). "Package" is deliberately coarse today (the bundled corelib as a whole, vs. a project as a whole -- `Module.is_corelib`, one boolean comparison in `same_package`), designed as the one seam a future manifest-based package system replaces without `internal` itself changing (see ROADMAP.md's package-registry entry, updated the same day). `internal` is a soft keyword (recognized by identifier text only immediately before `fn`/`struct`/`enum`/`val`, not reserved everywhere) after a real collision was found before writing any parser code: `health-record-backend-ziman` already has `pub fn internal<T>(...)`, called throughout the app -- making `internal` a true keyword would have broken it. Migrated the review's own flagged symbols (`net.idle_put`/`idle_take`/`idle_clear`, `db.RunRequest`/`Page`/`OpenRequest`/`FetchRequest`/`EndRequest`/`Request`/`run_request`, `datasource.PoolState`/`IdleConnection`) from `pub` to `internal`. See DESIGN.md "`internal` visibility, a soft keyword, and a package boundary designed to be replaced later"; fixtures `internal_visible_same_package`, `internal_rejected_cross_package`, `internal_as_identifier_still_works`. P2-6 (bitwise operators) done 2026-09-28: a new `bits` core module -- `byte_*`/`int_*`/`bigint_*` and/or/xor/not/shift_left/shift_right/popcount (`bigint` also gets `bit_length`), plus `BitSet` (`int`-backed, indices 0-63) and `BigBitSet` (`bigint`-backed, unbounded), both Java-`BitSet`-inspired value types with no mutable builder. `bigint`'s bitwise ops match Java's `BigInteger`/Python's `int` exactly (infinite two's complement), validated against a Python oracle. See DESIGN.md "A bits module"; fixtures `bits_int`, `bits_int_shift_out_of_range_rejected`, `bits_bigint`, `bits_bigint_negative_shift_rejected`, `bits_byte`, `bits_byte_shift_out_of_range_rejected`, `bitset`, `bitset_index_out_of_range_rejected`, `big_bitset`, `big_bitset_negative_index_rejected`. P2-8 (local named functions and recursive lambdas) done 2026-09-29 via the review's own named mechanism ("a `let rec`-style name binding for lambdas"), self-recursion only: `val fact = fn(n: int) -> int { return if n <= 1 { 1 } else { n * fact(n - 1) } }` now type-checks and runs. `val NAME = fn(...) { ... }` was already Ziman's existing idiom for "a local named function" (non-recursive); this adds the recursion half. Not added: a dedicated `fn name(...) { ... }` *statement* form for a local function declaration (still a parse error, unchanged) -- the `val`-bound lambda idiom already covers the same need. The real obstacle was a bootstrapping problem (a lambda captures by value, copied at construction time, but a self-referential value doesn't exist yet while it's still being built), not a scoping one -- solved by never trying to capture self as a value at all: `self_name` is bound directly in the lambda's own root scope (so referencing it is never a capture), and `call_function` synthesizes a fresh closure value under that name on every call, from data it already has on hand (the `FunctionDecl` plus this invocation's own leading captured args) -- no mutable cells, no new call-dispatch mechanism, dispatches through the existing `call.through_value` path unchanged. Caught and fixed one real regression during implementation: the first version set `self_name` for every `val`-bound lambda unconditionally and broke ordinary non-recursive lambdas with an inferred return type (`lambda_basic.zm`'s `val hello = fn() { print(...) }`); fixed by gating on the return type already being explicit, so a lambda that doesn't opt into one simply gets no self-reference, not a new error. Mutual recursion (two separate lambdas calling each other) is explicitly out of scope -- a harder, different problem. See DESIGN.md "Recursive lambdas: a self-value rebuilt on every call, never captured"; fixtures `lambda_recursive`, `lambda_recursive_var_rejected`, `lambda_recursive_no_return_type_rejected`, `lambda_recursive_self_param_collision_rejected`.
