P2-3 (UTF-8 correctness) done 2026-09-29, following the review's own two-part suggestion exactly: "validate at input boundaries... keep byte functions explicit." `bytes_to_string` (the one real boundary where arbitrary, possibly-untrusted bytes become a `string` at all) now rejects invalid UTF-8 -- a new, properly strict decoder (`utf8_decode_strict`, `prelude.c`, deliberately not reused from `regex.c`'s own lenient one, wrong job entirely) catches truncated sequences, bad continuation bytes, overlong encodings (a classic filter-bypass trick), surrogate halves, and anything past U+10FFFF. Every other string function (`len`, `substring`, `char_at`, ...) is untouched, still byte-oriented, exactly as documented. Three new functions -- `chars`, `char_count`, `truncate_chars` -- give the code-point-aware alternative the review's second half asked for ("no way to iterate characters or to truncate text safely"), each a runtime error on invalid UTF-8 (reachable only by chaining a byte function into one of these). See DESIGN.md "UTF-8 correctness: validate at the one real boundary, add code points as an opt-in"; fixtures `bytes_to_string_utf8_validation`, `chars_basic`, `chars_invalid_utf8_rejected`, `truncate_chars_negative_rejected`. **F3, and all of P2-4, are now fully done.**

- [x] **F6** An LLM-generation benchmark to measure the "AI-first" claims; profiler, debugger and coverage tooling. (L P2-7, D item 24; M-L) LLM-benchmark half done 2026-09-28: `tests/bench/llm_tasks/` is a fixed 10-task set (a real start, not yet the review's suggested 50), each run cold (zero Ziman context) then iterated against the compiler alone for up to 5 fix attempts, two fully independent fresh model instances per task. Round 1 (cold): 0/10 compiled -- every attempt reached for mainstream habits (semicolons, `let`, `match`, method calls, Go-style map literals). Round 2 (compiler diagnostics alone, no docs): 4/10 converged to correct output. The split is not random: diagnostics that name an exact fix (semicolons, the `match`-to-`switch` rewrite, "no methods... call X(a,...) instead") let a model self-correct in 1-3 tries every time; a plain "undefined variable"/"undefined function" with no suggestion (`let`, an exact builtin name, `Map<K,V>`'s construction syntax, `Result`'s lowercase `ok`/`err`) did not, and accounted for all 6 non-convergent cases. See DESIGN.md-adjacent `docs/reviews/llm-benchmark-2026-09-28.md` for the full write-up and per-task detail. The single highest-leverage fix the data pointed at -- "did you mean" fuzzy-name suggestions -- was implemented and re-validated the same day, twice (see ROADMAP.md's now-done entry and the benchmark report's two follow-up sections): 2 of the 3 targeted tasks (struct_distance, safe_divide_result) move from non-convergent to converging; the third (word_count_map) went through a second round after re-validation caught the fuzzy search suggesting a real but unrelated builtin (`hash` for `has`) -- fixed with a third named case pointing at `contains`/`map_get` -- and a final re-test confirmed both fixes work correctly in place but the task still needs two independent discoveries, which a 5-attempt, one-error-at-a-time budget doesn't always cover depending on which error a given run looks at first: a budget/ordering property, not a remaining diagnostic gap. Coverage done 2026-10-05: `--coverage` runs a program (or `ziman test --coverage` a whole suite) and reports to stderr, per file, how many lines of the user's own code executed, with a `not run:` list and exit 1 if any did not. (It printed to stdout as first committed and was moved the same day, so all three tools follow one rule: a tool that runs your program reports on stderr and leaves its stdout alone, while one that does not -- `--check`, `--fmt`, `--lint` -- reports on stdout.) Counting is one branch in `exec_stmt` into a per-`Stmt` counter, enabled by a flag; the report (src/coverage.c) walks the AST afterwards, which is what makes the denominator exact -- "executable line" means "a line some statement starts on", defined by the same walk that reads whether it ran rather than by a second model of the language that could drift. Bundled modules and `#[derive]`-generated code are excluded by the same `source_file`/`derived_from` test `--lint` uses, so the two tools agree about whose code it is. Overhead of carrying the counter is below this machine's noise floor (fifteen paired rounds of 80M statement executions put the medians within 0.2%, 95% interval -4.4% to +2.6%); the two smaller runs that preceded it read +1.6% and -2.0%, a reminder that a sub-1% effect needs the paired rounds. Covered by tests/fixtures/coverage_{partial,full}.zm. Profiler done 2026-10-05: `--profile` samples CPU time at 1kHz via SIGPROF and reports self time per function, hottest first, to stderr. It needs no instrumentation because error.c's frame stack -- already maintained unconditionally for error traces -- tells the handler which Ziman function is innermost; what the handler needs beyond that is one store into a slot claimed with an atomic increment. Validated against a constructed 3:2:1 workload (reported 49.8/33.3/16.9 against 50/33.3/16.7), on natives (a MapBuilder loop correctly blames `mapb_set`, not the caller), and across four `spawn`ed threads. Garbage collection gets its own row rather than being charged to whichever function allocated: without that the same map loop read `build_map` 48.6%, where a flat `perf` profile puts GC_mark_from at 27.8%. Boehm is bracketed via GC_set_on_collection_event, Ziman's own collector inside gc_heap_collect. The report also compares sampled time against CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID and says so when the kernel has coalesced signal delivery (measured 0.42s of samples against 0.90s of CPU on four threads), since the percentages stay valid there but the totals do not. Cost when not profiling is below the noise floor (+0.89%, 95% CI -0.44%..+2.22%, fourteen paired rounds of fib(30)) -- but only after a measured 9% regression was tracked down to the 4MB sample buffer being a static array, 4MB of BSS in every binary: +1.9% instructions but +10.4% cycles, and an unrelated change of the same size to the same file cost nothing, which is what ruled out code-layout luck. It is now allocated only when profiling starts. Covered by tests/fixtures/profile_stdout_clean.zm, which pins that the report stays on stderr (sample counts are not reproducible and cannot be asserted). Debugger, post-mortem half, done 2026-10-05: `--locals` prints each stack frame's variables and values under its line in a runtime error's trace, innermost scope outwards, stopping before the global scope. That is deliberately the post-mortem half and not an interactive one: a trace already says where a program died, and what it does not say is what the values were, which arrives here on the first failure with no setup and in places nothing can attach to afterwards -- a CI run, or a crash someone else reported. Stepping and breakpoints remain unbuilt, and the argument for narrowing F6 rather than building them still stands: the LLM benchmark's own finding was that diagnostics, not step-debugging, were what unblocked a model. Implementation keeps error.c's deliberate ignorance of Env and Value intact -- it holds a NULL function pointer until `--locals` installs the renderer in src/locals.c, so an ordinary run executes the same trace code it did before and the feature costs nothing when unused. Nested-block locals are visible because interpreter.c already swaps a frame's env to the innermost active scope (for the GC's root walk); shadowed names are shown once as the live binding; compiler-generated bindings are filtered on `$`, which the lexer cannot produce in a user identifier; strings are quoted and long values truncated at 160 characters. Verified on nested scopes, shadowing, truncation, struct values and a spawned thread's own trace (`x: int = 0` on a division by zero). Covered by tests/fixtures/locals_nested_shadowing.zm and locals_off_by_default.zm, the latter pinning that an ordinary trace is byte-for-byte what it was. Closed 2026-10-05, narrowed, with what it does not cover named rather than left implied. Delivered: the coverage tooling, the profiler, and the debugging that answers a failure after the fact (`--coverage`, `--profile`, `--locals`, each with fixtures and each measured not to cost an ordinary run anything findable). Deliberately out of scope, and moved to ROADMAP.md's "Larger, needs its own design pass" rather than dropped: (a) an **interactive debugger** -- breakpoints, stepping, variable inspection -- which is a project on src/lsp.c's scale and whose value the one piece of evidence here argues against prioritising, since this task's own benchmark found diagnostics rather than step-debugging were what let a model self-correct; and (b) **growing the benchmark from 10 tasks to the review's 50**, which is the honest reading of its state -- a fifth of the asked-for scope, enough to produce a real finding and not enough to separate an effect from which error a run happened to hit first. The package/version story D item 24 also names stays a deliberate non-goal, on the review's own "scope vs. bus factor" grounds. So this closes as "the measurable and the post-mortem tooling exist; the interactive and the statistical both have their own entries", not as the whole of what F6 originally asked for.
- [x] **F7** Throughput does not scale with clients in the collector build -- F2's second half, carried over because it is genuinely unfixed and because the plan it used to carry is now known to be a dead end. (was L P2-1, D) Current numbers, both builds back to back on `tests/bench/server_concurrency.sh` 2026-10-05: collector **604/1130/1005/680** req/s at c=1/4/8/16, `ziman-nogc` **787/1857/2196/2581**. Absolute throughput has roughly doubled at every level since F2's original 347/584/429/361 -- real progress from six `gc_heap` migrations -- but the shape F2 named is unchanged: the collector build peaks at c=4 and falls ~40% by c=16 while `nogc` climbs the whole way, and the gap at c=16 is **3.8x**, essentially the 3.5x F2 last recorded (and that figure was with heap tuning; this one is without). Reproduced in three separate runs the same day. **What is now ruled out, with evidence, is the entire plan F2 ended on** ("deciding what to migrate next -- MapNode/Leaf ... is one candidate"): migrating more into the per-thread `gc_heap` measures *slower*, ~10% for a `format` site, ~8-10% for `ClosureObj`, ~7% for the same `ClosureObj` change across eight threads -- the last being the case most likely to have rescued it, since the one migration ever measured as a win (`Map`/`Set`, 689 -> 802-843 req/s) was measured under concurrency. The mechanism: a `gc_heap` object pays *both* collectors, sitting inside the range Boehm conservatively re-scans as a root *and* being marked and swept by Ziman's own Phase 3, where a Boehm-heap object pays Boehm only. And it cannot be fixed incrementally, because Ziman's mark phase makes **zero** calls into Boehm, so the conservative scan is load-bearing for cross-heap retention: the scan cannot be dropped until nothing in the `gc_heap` points into Boehm's heap, and the migration cannot show a win until the scan is dropped. Each half is the other's prerequisite. See DESIGN.md's "The gc_heap migration, measured and abandoned" and "complete the gc_heap conclusion" entries. **So the open question is no longer "what to migrate next" but whether v0 should run two collectors over one object graph at all** -- and whoever opens that has to deal first with the constraint that pins the conservative scan in place: a native handle (socket, DB connection) is retained *only* by a conservative scan finding its disguised `int64`, because `handles.h` keeps the live-handle table unscanned on purpose so that being in the set does not keep an object alive. Everything cheaper has already been tried and recorded: heap tuning (a bigger initial heap is a free 30-80%, nothing else helps, incremental mode is catastrophic), `GC_FREE_SPACE_DIVISOR` (13 collections at every setting from 1 to 24 -- they are expansion-driven), and per-object root pushing (mark-stack overflow, which livelocks the collector). **Re-confirmed twice on 2026-10-05, by two independent methods, and re-scoped by what they found.** L2 §4.7 (microbenchmark): 8 threads doing 8x the work take 10.6x longer *per thread* while pure compute scales 4.7x, total allocation throughput *falls* from 2.14 to 1.62 M iter/s, and `--profile` attributes 81.2% to GC. D2 §4 (live HTTP server): 92.9% of CPU in garbage collection, authenticated-read throughput peaking at 4-8 clients then falling (801 -> 1,511 -> 763 rps at 1/4/64 clients), and *lowering* `max-connections` from 64 to 4 *raising* throughput. Both call it the defect that blocks production; L2 calls it the one that invalidates the stated goal. The re-scoping matters: throughput falling as concurrency rises, and capacity improving when fewer clients are admitted, is contention -- not the per-visit scan cost that ROADMAP.md's `gc_lookup` block-index entry was aimed at, which would not touch it. L2's interim suggestion is to cut allocation *frequency* in the two hottest allocators (`format`'s output buffer, small `StructObj`) rather than migrate more objects into `gc_heap`, which this task already measured as slower. F10 (a `metrics` module) is a prerequisite for judging any fix from inside the process: both reviews had to measure this from outside. **2026-10-07: three allocation sites removed; much faster, shape still wrong, task stays open.** L2's suggestion (`format`'s buffer, small `StructObj`) was checked rather than taken: a new `make ziman-allocprofile` counts allocations per source site, and over 2,000 backend reads the three hottest were `env.c`'s `strdup` of every binding name (25.7% of calls), `interpreter.c`'s per-call argument array (24.5%), and `mapobj_mark_leaves`'s scratch array of every leaf (24.0%) -- **which runs inside the mark phase, so every collection was allocating**. None of the three is what the review named. All three are gone: names are borrowed from the AST, argument arrays of four or fewer live in the frame Boehm already scans, and mark_leaves visits as it walks. Allocation calls fell 9.96M -> 2.57M. Paired interleaved rounds: **c=1 +10-15%, c=4 +27-30%, c=8 +18-47%, c=16 +28-69%, c=32 +66-74%** -- the gain grows with concurrency, which is what a cut to collection *frequency* should do. The gap to nogc at c=16 narrows from ~4.6x to ~2.5-3.5x. **Still open because the shape is unchanged in kind**: throughput still peaks around 4-8 clients and declines, just less steeply, and the architectural question (two collectors over one object graph) is untouched. Next by evidence: `env.c`'s bindings array, now the largest byte source at 374MB over 209K reallocs. Method notes in DESIGN.md: the profiler's own first version had undefined behaviour that reported a 32GB phantom site, and two of my first readings (+58% and "depth halved") were artefacts of an unpaired baseline and a doubling ladder. **Closed 2026-10-07: the shape is gone.** A fourth change followed the three above -- the five loop scopes in `exec_stmt` now take their Env from the same per-thread pool `call_function` and `exec_block` already used (234K Env allocations down to 107K, their `items` reallocs 209K to 145K, +14% at c=16 and markedly less variance). Full paired sweep, 2,500 requests a level: **before** 407-429 / 888-988 / 1030-1041 / 763-843 / 620-756 at c=1/4/8/16/32, peaking at 4-8 and falling 27-40% by 32; **after** 463-535 / 1293-1428 / 1522-1664 / 1429-1574 / 1491-1517, plus 1436-1495 at c=64 -- flat from 8 to 64 within noise, which is what `ziman-nogc` does too (2711/2979/2745/2652). So throughput no longer falls as clients are added, and what is left between the builds is a ~1.8x constant factor at every level, down from 4.6x at c=16: the ordinary price of a tracing collector rather than a failure to scale. Closed on that basis. **Two things it does not settle, deliberately**: the architectural question (two collectors over one object graph) is untouched and the `gc_heap` migration stays measured-and-abandoned; and this is one workload -- an authenticated read of an empty collection, new connection per request, which is the *pessimistic* case for the Env pool -- so a write-heavy or keep-alive profile is unmeasured. DESIGN.md's "Three allocation sites, and what they did to F7" carries the evidence and the method notes.


- [x] **F8** **`SYNTAX.md` has non-compiling and stale examples**, and the fix for the whole class is already proven elsewhere in this repo. (L2 P1-6, D2 item 14; S (fixes) / M (check)) `SYNTAX.md:371-374` is the primary demonstration of `Iter`/`fs.lines` and does not compile (it uses postfix `?`, which was removed); `:1022` and `:165` are also stale; `option_unwrap_or`/`result_to_option`/`option_to_result` are undocumented and the BCE claim is wrong. The library pages cannot drift because they are generated and fixture-verified -- extracting fenced `zm` blocks from `SYNTAX.md` and at least `--check`ing them would close the same gap for the reference. Done 2026-10-06. Nothing had ever compiled SYNTAX.md's examples; `tools/check_syntax_examples.py` now does, via `make syntax-check` inside `docs-check`. A fence's info string says how a block is treated: `ziman` (must compile), `ziman fragment` (statements, wrapped in a Result-returning function so a fragment demonstrating `try` compiles as written, with any leading `use` hoisted), `ziman fails` (must **not** compile -- an example whose point is that the compiler rejects it stops demonstrating anything the day it compiles, which no other check would notice), `ziman ignore: <why>` (a reason is required), and untagged for what is not Ziman. An untagged block that looks like Ziman is reported, since that is how the check would otherwise be bypassed in silence. Of 46 blocks: 29 compile, 5 fragments, 1 expected to fail, 11 ignored, 1 not Ziman. **Four genuine defects found, not the one the task cited** (whose line reference had already drifted to unrelated prose): `struct AddCase { a: int, b: int, ... }` used commas, which Ziman never accepts between fields; the properties example declared a module-level `val props`, colliding with the parameter name `#[derive(Properties)]` generates, and used `try` outside any function; and the Result-discard example annotated `try risky(-1)` inside `fn main()` as "fine" when it cannot be. Six more examples used a module without the `use` they needed -- one of them with the comment "(`use math`)" where the import should have been. Verified the gate fires on all three regression shapes: an example that stops compiling, a `fails` example that starts compiling, and a new untagged Ziman block.
- [x] **F9** **Broaden `--lint`**: unused `val`/parameter, `set(arr, i, ..)` in a loop, `unwrap` outside a test, and a `spawn` without `on_error` whose channel the parent `receive`s. (L2 P2-5, D2 item 16; S) Two rules today; the `concat`-in-a-loop rule shows the shape works. The `spawn` rule is the cheap mitigation for A12 -- it turns a silent forever-hang into a warning without touching channel semantics. One more candidate found while measuring C16: an `as` alias identical to the name it would bind anyway (`use controller/admin as admin`), which is a no-op -- 16 of them in the reference backend. Done 2026-10-07: **four of the five rules added, the fifth declined on measurement.** (1) A declaration nothing ever reads -- 2 genuine findings in the backend (a `val saved = try repository.save(...)` whose value nothing wanted, and an `if Err(failure) =` whose binder the log never mentions). Getting there cost two lessons: widening the table from `var` to every declaration segfaulted at once on `val _ = 99`, since `env_define` never binds `_`; and a call does not go through `EXPR_IDENT`, so `val to_params = crud.to_params` + `to_params(entity)` reported 19 findings in the backend, every one wrong, until a call counted as a read. Loop binders are not reported; `_` is the documented discard and works in a declaration, a pattern and a parameter. (2) A redundant `as` alias -- 15 in the backend, the 16 the review counted. Lives in the loader, because a module's `use` list is gone from the merged Module the type checker sees, and `--lint` is now enabled before the load for that reason; `UseDecl` gained `has_alias` plus the alias's own position. (3) A `spawn` with no `on_error` that is handed a Channel -- the cheap half of A12. Narrowed to channel-passing spawns so a fire-and-forget thread is not noise; the shape was chosen against `pg.zm` and `mysql.zm`, which each spawn the loop their own `requests` channel feeds. (4) `x = set(x, i, ..)` in a loop, added to the existing quadratic rule **on measurement**: 82/225/904ms at n=8,000/16,000/32,000 (time quadrupling as n doubles), while the same loop with `push` took 3/4/7ms -- so `push` is deliberately *not* reported, the shape that looks worst costing least because appending extends in place. The fixture puts the `push` line between two reported ones so its contiguous expectation proves the absence. **Declined: `unwrap` outside a test.** 23 occurrences in the backend, nearly all deliberate startup fail-fast (`unwrap(loaded)`, `unwrap(crypto.base64_decode(cfg.jwt.secret))`) -- a blanket rule would report idiomatic code as a defect. 880 fixtures in all three builds; 17 findings total on the backend, all four rules.
- [x] **F10** **A `metrics` module**, per the design already in `ROADMAP.md:1741`, with `server` recording its own. (L2 P2-7; M) Nothing in F7 is diagnosable in production without it -- both 2026-10-05 reviews had to measure the collector from outside the process. Done 2026-10-06: items 1 and 2 of the roadmap entry (3-5, runtime metrics and tracing, stay roadmap). `lib/core/metrics.zm` over `src/native_metrics.c`: counters, gauges and histograms with `Map<string, string>` labels, a 128-series cap per metric whose drops are counted and rendered as `ziman_metrics_dropped_series_total`, and `render()` in the Prometheus text format. Recording is infallible by design -- a counter increment that could fail would mean a request failing because it was measured -- so recording to an undeclared name is silently nothing, which the module documents. **Two measurements changed the design.** A mutex per metric made throughput *fall* as threads were added (3.1M increments/s on one thread, 2.1M on eight), the exact shape of F7, so an existing series is now updated lock-free with a compare-exchange on the double's bits: 3.5M on one thread, 6.7M on four, 3.8M on eight. And labelling per call cost ~4.5us against ~0.25us for the update, because `labels_key` sorts and escapes every time -- hence `metrics.series(...)`, a handle resolved once, which measured 20x faster (205 k/s to 4040 k/s). The server records through `metered_routes` (per request: method, **matched pattern**, status, plus a latency histogram) and `ServeOptions.metrics` (open connections, bytes in and out), with `server.metrics_handler` to mount `/metrics` in one line. The pattern label comes from the route table because only something that matched the request knows it; an unmatched path is labelled `<unmatched>` rather than by its path, which would fill the cap. Noted honestly: `clock.monotonic_millis` is the finest clock available, so every sub-millisecond request lands in the first latency bucket. **Two of my own bugs, both caught by insisting on seeing a number move**: `metrics.add` refuses a gauge by design, so the open-connections gauge needed its own `gauge_add`; and the decrement sat inside an `if` block, where Ziman's **block-scoped** `defer` (SYNTAX.md:2099) fired it immediately, so the gauge read 0 while connections were plainly open. 877 fixtures in all three builds. Backend wired 2026-10-07 (backend 1b03ca8): `server.metrics` (default false) turns on recording and `GET /api/admin/metrics/prometheus`, administrator-only since the page maps the API and its traffic (403 for a non-admin, 200 for an admin, both verified). Kept distinct from the existing `/api/admin/metrics`, which is Spring's JSON summary counted from the database. `routes.zm` compiles the endpoints twice, into `table` and `metered_table`, and `dispatch_for` picks by `ctx.metrics` -- two tables because both are module-level values built before `config.load` has run. The suite passes both with the default and with `SERVER_METRICS=true`, so the metered path is exercised and not just the fallback.
- [x] **F11** **Mark superseded `DESIGN.md` entries** with a dated 'superseded by' line, or generate an index. (L2 P2-8; S) The formatter entry at `:3247` contradicts `:3612`, and a reader who stops at the first hit gets the wrong answer. The file is a decision log both reviews praised; this is what keeps it one. Done 2026-10-07, three things rather than the one asked for, in order of how much each keeps working on its own. (1) **`tools/check_not_yet.py` now scans DESIGN.md**, which found two genuinely stale claims immediately (`listen_tls` named as "not yet" in two entries). Reading those lists against the code found more than the check can see: of the six gaps the HTTP-server entry named, **three** had landed -- TLS, chunked request bodies (where that entry's own test expected a 501), and the `Date` header. The extension needed two fixes, both found by the check reporting what it should not: a *quoted* "Not yet" is a citation (DESIGN.md's own entry about this check quotes the stale sentence three times), and a `**Superseded**` note ends the claim it corrects, since everything it names is what has landed. A later pass also found the pattern required a *bare* identifier, so a qualified `metrics.render` matched nothing at all -- fixed and verified both ways. (2) **A generated index** of all 114 sections (`make design-index`, gated by `design-index-check` in `docs-check`): it does not resolve a contradiction but is what makes a second entry on a topic visible. Both new gates were verified to fire. (3) **The two formatter entries point at each other** with dated **Extended by**/**Extends** lines -- the case the task cited -- and "How to read this file" at the top states the convention plus what the check can and cannot verify. **Deliberately not done**: marking the gc_heap migration's twenty-odd entries as superseding each other. They are a chronological journal of one effort, each building on the last, and "superseded" would be wrong for every one.
- [x] **F12** **`--fmt --canonical`**, normalising brace placement where the lexer can decide unambiguously. (L2 P2-9; M) The formatter is idempotent and safe but does not settle style, which is most of why gofmt is loved. Done 2026-10-07: `--fmt --canonical` moves a line-leading `{` onto the line of what it opens and an `else` onto the line of the `}` that closed its `if`. Opt-in, because moving a line break is the one thing plain `--fmt` promises not to do -- which is exactly why a file in Allman braces and one in K&R both pass `--fmt --check` today. **The rule ended up simpler than planned, not more complicated**: a first version restricted itself to braces following a keyword line, out of a worry about a bare block statement (`print(x)` then `{`), and testing that case revealed Ziman has no bare block statement at all -- a `{` cannot begin one. With the hazard ruled out, every line-leading `{` can move, which covers `switch` arm patterns and struct literals too; only a brace after a trailing comment is left alone, since it would be commented out. Verified four ways: the output still runs, it is idempotent (it only ever clears a newline), `format_source`'s token-equality self-check covers the rest, and `--fmt --canonical --check` over all of `lib/` plus the documentation examples reports no change, so the library was already in this style. A side effect recorded in DESIGN.md: adding a call next to `sort_use_runs` lost GCC the proof that `items->n` is not negative, and it warned about a `memcpy` bound of 18 quintillion bytes -- pre-existing code that only became unprovable because something new was called beside it; the bound is now guarded. 881 fixtures in all three builds.
- [x] **F13** **Machine-readable `--coverage` output** (lcov or JSON) and a way to merge runs. (D2 item 17; M) Without it coverage cannot gate anything in CI. Note the ordering: C13 has to land first, or there is nothing to measure -- the reference backend has zero `#[test]` functions today. Done 2026-10-07: `--coverage-lcov=F` writes the measurement in lcov's `.info` format (`TN:`, `SF:`, one `DA:<line>,<count>` per executable line, `LF:`/`LH:`, `end_of_record`) for genhtml, Codecov and Coveralls. Implies `--coverage` and works with `ziman test` too, which is the CI path. `LineHit` now holds a hit count rather than a boolean -- `Stmt.exec_count` already had it and the text report was discarding it, so nothing extra is counted at run time. **The merge is done in `coverage.c`, not left to the reader**: an existing F is read and this run's counts added, so several invocations accumulate. The alternative was to write a fresh file and say that concatenating `.info` files merges them because lcov sums repeated records -- true as far as I know, but a claim about a tool that **is not installed here**, so it would have been unverifiable; merging in our own code is a claim `make test-coverage` can check. The cost, stated in the help text: counts only grow, so a stale F flatters the result, which is why the path is never defaulted. Verified two ways -- a new `make test-coverage` unit test (Module built by hand; records, LF/LH, and that a second write keeps what the file had while gaining what it did not), and by hand across *processes*, which the unit test cannot stage: one two-branch program run twice into one file took LH from 4 to 6 of LF:6, lines common to both runs summing. Note on the task's own premise: it said C13 must land first because the reference backend has no `#[test]` functions. That is still true of the backend, but ziman's own `ziman test` and 881 fixtures are measurable today, so the feature was worth having now rather than after.


- [x] **F14** **Add `-Wvla` and `-Wshadow` to `PLAIN_CFLAGS`, and `make asan`/`ubsan`/`tsan` targets.** (I P2-4; S) Of roughly 520 extra diagnostics from a much higher warning level, **exactly one** points at a real bug: `-Wvla` on `src/regex.c:758`, which is A16's mechanism. The rest were triaged and rejected rather than left as a count -- 414 `-Wswitch-enum` is by design, the `-Wlogical-op` hits are the correct `EAGAIN || EWOULDBLOCK` idiom, and the `-Wjump-misses-init` targets never touch the skipped variables. The targets matter more than the flags: there is no sanitizer target today, so every review builds its own (this one had to fetch `libasan`/`libubsan` RPMs locally, since neither is installed), and a standing target would have caught A19 and A22 without a review. GCC's `-fanalyzer` is already clean on both configurations and could join `make check` cheaply. **Known noise, found while doing A16/A19/A23 and recorded here so it is not rediscovered:** a whole-binary ASan run is not usable as-is -- every regex fixture trips `gc_scan_stack_roots` (`src/gc_scan.c:32`) as a stack-buffer-overflow, and valgrind reports `gc_lookup.c:11` as an uninitialised read 22 times over the stack-trace fixtures. Both are the conservative stack scanner reading whatever is on the stack, which is what it is for. A target therefore needs either a suppression file for those two frames or the Boehm configuration (where the project's own scanner is not used); the three sanitizer builds that *are* clean and were used to verify A16, A19, A21 and A22 are the evidence that the rest works -- a standalone ASan+UBSan link of `src/regex.c` with its unit test, a TSan link of the whole binary, and a UBSan link with `-fwrapv` off, which now reports nothing across all 808 fixtures. **Reviewed 2026-10-08: the mechanism is right, the granularity is off by one level (F21), and the stated reason for one option is wrong (F22).** The premise was re-verified independently here -- dropping the attribute and changing nothing else gives **353 passed, 535 failed** with 505 ASan reports, so it is load-bearing. A detail this entry got slightly wrong: the first violation is not a caller's redzone but the scanner's **own** one-byte `probe`, whose first loop iteration reads eight bytes -- so no mechanism that leaves the loop instrumented can work, whatever it does about redzones. **Three alternatives tested and rejected, one of them dangerous**, recorded so nobody tries them again: `__asan_unpoison_memory_region` works but blinds ASan across the entire live stack above the scan point, and a deliberate out-of-bounds write in the *caller* that the attribute catches in 534 fixtures goes completely unreported; a per-file `-fno-sanitize=address` has the same blind spot as today plus a Makefile cost; and filtering with `__asan_address_is_poisoned` -- skipping words ASan considers poisoned -- is **silently unsound**: **27.6% of the collector's root hits (567 of 2056) sit in poisoned words**, all of them stack redzones holding real pointer values, and across 981 collections **33 distinct heap objects were reachable only through such a word**. The suite passes either way, so no test would ever tell you. **Done 2026-10-08, and the targets found a real race on their first run (A24), which is the whole argument for having them.** Both flags are in `PLAIN_CFLAGS` and the tree is clean under them: `-Wvla` had nothing left to say once A16 removed the matcher's VLA, and `-Wshadow`'s two hits were real shadowing in `parser.c` -- an inner `type_args` for the qualified form hiding the outer one -- now `qualified_type_args`. The build is silent again too: `allocsites.c` was tripping `-Wpedantic` on an empty translation unit in every build but the profiling one. **`make asan`, `make ubsan`, `make tsan`** each build the plain configuration and run the whole suite; `make sanitizers` runs all three, and `make analyze` keeps `-fanalyzer` out of `make check` because it takes minutes rather than seconds. Results on this tree: **ASan 887 passed / 1 skipped, UBSan 888 passed** (deliberately without `-fwrapv`, so signed overflow is checked -- it is the build that found A21), **TSan 881 passed / 6 failed**, every failure the one race in A24. Four things the targets had to learn, none of them predictable from reading: ASan is useless on this codebase without `__attribute__((no_sanitize_address))` on `gc_scan_stack_roots`, because the conservative scanner reads every word between the stack pointer and the stack top, which is ASan's definition of a stack-buffer-overflow, on the first collection of *any* program -- and stack-buffer-overflow has no runtime suppression; `halt_on_error=1` is required, or a report lands on stderr while a `.out` fixture still passes and the suite says "888 passed" over a page of undefined behaviour; `detect_stack_use_after_return=0` is required because ASan's fake stack moves locals and `src/stack.h`'s guard measures a local's address; and one fixture has to be skipped per sanitizer, for different reasons -- `stack_depth_deep_recursion_works` under ASan (instrumented frames no longer fit its own 256 MB) and `stack_depth_trace_bounded` under TSan (TSan's reporter SEGVs in `std::ostream::flush`, because the fixture deliberately exhausts the stack and the reporter needs stack of its own). `tests/run_tests.sh` gained a `SKIP="name ..."` list for that, which reports each as skipped rather than hiding it. The runtimes are not installed on this machine, so each recipe passes `$(LDFLAGS)` through and a failing link says what to install or how to point at an unpacked copy instead of printing ld's output -- which is how all three were verified here.
- [x] **F15** **Four small restorations of removed safety properties and stale documentation.** Restore `calloc`'s overflow check in `src/zgc.h`, bound the sampler index at `src/profile.c:105`, use `offsetof` at `src/mapobj.c:353`/`:366`, and correct the `"bucket\0key"` comment at `src/native_http.c:68`. (I P2-5; S each) Each is a property the C once had or a comment that no longer describes the code; none is reachable today. **Done 2026-10-08, and one of the four was reachable after all.** `src/profile.c`'s sampler claimed its slot with `__atomic_fetch_add`, which kept incrementing after the buffer was full -- about nine minutes of CPU at 1000 Hz -- and `sig_atomic_t` is `int`, so after roughly 25 days of profiled CPU the counter wrapped negative, `idx >= SAMPLE_CAPACITY` stopped being true, and the store went outside the buffer. A long-lived server with `--profile` left on is exactly that. It now claims with a compare-exchange that stops at the capacity, so the counter never wraps at all; verified by lowering `SAMPLE_CAPACITY` to 64 for one build -- 64 samples kept, 1,836 dropped and reported, no crash -- then restoring it. **`calloc`'s overflow check is back**, through `ziman_gc_calloc_at` rather than `GC_MALLOC((a) * (b))`, a function so each argument is evaluated once; A20 had already put the check on every call site that goes through `ziman_xcalloc`, so what this closes is the macro itself, which `src/regex.c` and `src/xalloc.c` still reach. Both paths were exercised directly with a product that overflows `size_t` and both now refuse it, naming the call site: `refusing an array of 4611686018427387904 elements of 8 bytes at ...: the size overflows`. The message is new too -- reporting a wrapped byte count as an allocation failure was its own small lie. **`offsetof(MapNode, slots)`** replaces `sizeof(MapNode) +` at all three sites, the correct idiom for a flexible array member: equal here, since `Slot`'s alignment leaves `MapNode` no tail padding, and not equal on a platform where it would. **The `native_http.c:68` comment** said the rate-limit key is `"bucket\0key"`; the `snprintf` eight lines below joins them with a newline, so it now says `"bucket\nkey"` and points at the line that decides it. 888 fixtures, three builds, `make check` green.
- [x] **F16** **Write down what roots a handle, and make `iter.c:41`'s finalizer unconditional.** Next to `src/handles.c:20`: a handle is rooted by being a boxed `Value`, **not** by the handle table, which is `zalloc_atomic` on purpose. (I P2-6; S) Filed `[O]` -- the argument holds today, but it has an edge worth stating, since a packed `[int]` array is `zalloc_atomic` too (`src/value.c:279`), and a `no_finalizer` iterator has no second line of defence if it ever stops holding. The audit that produced this also closed the dangerous half of the `zgc.h` macro question: all five macros expand to one call with one expansion per argument in all three configurations, so the bug shape `DESIGN.md` records cannot recur -- including `mapobj.c:353`/`:366`, which read like it and are safe. **Done 2026-10-08. The write-up is done as asked; the unconditional finalizer is declined, and the reason is now in the source.** `no_finalizer` is set for exactly one kind of iterator, `iter_from`'s, whose `release` is `from_release` -- which calls `native_call_closure`, i.e. runs the program's own `close` closure. Registering `iter_finalizer` for those would run interpreted Ziman code from a Boehm finalizer: at an arbitrary allocation point, on whichever thread happens to allocate, re-entering the interpreter from inside a collection, against an `Iter` whose owner thread is probably not that one. The flag's declaration in `iter.h` had no comment at all, which is why its absence looked like an oversight rather than the decision it is; it says so now, and so does the registration site. **The safe half of the intent is implemented**: `iter_drop_finalizer` takes such an iterator out of the live set and runs no interpreted code, so an abandoned `iter_from` no longer leaves a dangling pointer in a validation table for the life of the process. Measured with a temporary counter: over 4,000 abandoned iterators with a forced collection each, it ran **3,998 times**, where before all 4,000 pointers would have stayed. The `close` closure is still not called -- that part is the program's job and the docs say so. **On the rooting argument, one thing sharper than the task knew**: the edge is reachable halfway, because `it.handle` is an ordinary `int` field and a Ziman program can read it -- `print(it.handle)` prints a raw address -- and could put it in a packed `[int]` array, which is `zalloc_atomic` and roots nothing. It is harmless only because nothing in the language accepts a bare int as a handle; every builtin takes the `Iter` or `Socket` value, and that is what the collector sees. The comment at `src/handles.c` says that, and says that a native accepting a handle integer would break it -- which is the thing to notice before writing one.
- [x] **F17** **The formatter's refusal is unhelpful in two ways, one of which can destroy a buffer.** (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; S) First, the count mixes bracket kinds: a document refused at 202 is reported as "202 brackets open" when it is really 101 `(`, 40 `[` and 61 `{`, and the user cannot tell which to unnest. `nesting_within_limit` already walks every item, so breaking the count down is about three lines, and A18's message was otherwise judged the best of the four tools compared. Second, and worse: **`--fmt -` writes zero bytes to stdout and exits 2 on a refused document** (verified: exit 2, 0 bytes, the message on stderr). `--fmt -` is a documented stdin filter, so an editor that pipes a buffer through it and replaces the buffer with stdout -- without checking the exit status, which is the common shape -- **blanks the file**. Either echo the input unchanged on refusal, or document the exit-status contract where `--help` describes `-`. **Done 2026-10-09 -- both, since they are cheap and answer different readers.** The message now breaks the count down at the deepest point: `line 63: nesting is too deep to format (203 open: 101 '(', 40 '[', 62 '{'; the limit is 200, ...)`, snapshotted rather than totalled so the three always sum to the number beside them. That is the difference between knowing a document is too deep and knowing *which* nesting to go and unpick. **And a refused file is now printed back unchanged** on the path that would otherwise print the formatted source, so a buffer filtered through `--fmt -` survives a refusal even when the caller ignores the exit status -- verified byte-identical, 554 in and 554 out, with exit 2 and the reason still on stderr. The other three paths were checked for the same question and all behave: `--write` leaves the file alone, `--check` prints only filenames (0 bytes, exit 2), and a careful caller still sees the status. Written down in `--help` as well as in the source, since the contract is now part of what `-` means. Fixture `fmt_nesting_too_deep.err` updated to the new wording. 889 fixtures, `make check` green.
- [x] **F18** **Both new limits are undiscoverable, and two sentences in the regex manual are now wrong.** (from the A16/A18 limits review, 2026-10-08; S) Neither `FORMAT_MAX_NESTING` nor `MAX_MATCH_BYTES` appears anywhere outside `docs/reviews/tasks.md` -- not in `SYNTAX.md`, `CORELIB.md`, `docs/lib/*.md`, `README.md`, `--help`, or `DESIGN.md`'s own formatter section. `docs/SYNTAX.md:1751` already carries the sentence "A repetition count caps at 1000, a compiled pattern at 20,000 instructions", which is where the third limit belongs. Two outright errors to fix in `docs/lib/regex.md`: `compile`'s entry says **"An Err names the position of the problem"**, which is false for the budget refusal (it names sizes, not a position), and the page says compiling **"on every request is cheap"**, which wants a clause now that a pattern can be refused and that a failed compile is not cached (A26). Neither limit is worth a flag -- nobody has a legitimate 200-deep file, and a configurable depth would just move the blowup behind a knob. **Done 2026-10-09, in four places and no flag.** `docs/SYNTAX.md` now names the memory budget in the same sentence that already named the repetition and instruction limits, and says the useful thing about it -- that it is a cap on *capture groups* in practice, about 1,170, and never one on pattern length. `README.md`'s `--fmt` paragraph names the nesting limit with the reason (indentation is per-depth, so the output would be quadratic) and the scale (real code reaches about 10). `docs/DESIGN.md`'s R17 entry gets a dated note with the measurements and the comparison to gofmt, Prettier, clang-format and rustfmt, plus a pointer to A27 for the axis the limit does not bound. **Both wrong sentences are fixed at their source**, `lib/core/regex.zm`, since `docs/lib/regex.md` is generated from it: "An Err names the position of the problem" becomes "usually names the position -- except when the pattern is refused for its size, where it names the size instead", and "compile in a loop or on every request is cheap" gains the clause that makes it true, that a **failed** compile is not cached, so a caller that recompiles a bad pattern per request pays every time. 889 fixtures, `make check` green including `lib-manual-check` and the SYNTAX.md example compiler. One more line the regex manual wants, from the A16 adversarial review: the 64 MB budget is **per match call**, not a process-wide ceiling, so a server with N concurrent matches can hold N times it.
- [x] **F19** **`lib/core/net.zm` promises a blocked `accept` an error that it does not get, and `docs/lib/net.md` is generated from that promise.** (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; S) `net.zm:416` says of `close_listener`: *"Stops listening and closes the listener's socket. A thread blocked in `accept` on it gets an error."* `close` makes the same promise. What the blocked thread actually gets, verified in A31, is `accept(2)` on a recycled descriptor -- ENOTSOCK, or somebody else's connection. So the public API documentation endorses exactly the pattern `src/native_net.c`'s header comment warns against, and **three of the project's own fixtures take it at its word** -- `httpclient_basic.zm`, `httpclient_binary.zm` and `http_server.zm` each `spawn` an accept loop and then close the listener from main, surviving only because the process exits immediately afterwards. The doc comments are the deliverable here, not `docs/lib/net.md`, which is generated from them. If A31 lands, the promise becomes true and this becomes a one-line confirmation instead. **Done 2026-10-09, and that is what happened -- A31 made the promise true, so this is a confirmation plus a sharper statement.** Verified by running both shapes: a thread blocked in `read` when another thread closes the socket gets `connection closed by peer`, and a thread blocked in `accept` when the listener is closed gets `the listener was closed` -- both the `Closed` variant of `NetError`, which is what a caller can actually branch on, so both comments now name it instead of saying "an error". They also say the pattern is **supported rather than tolerated**, and why: the socket holds a reference while a call is in flight, so the descriptor cannot be closed and its number reused underneath it. That is the sentence the three fixtures were relying on without being entitled to. `docs/lib/net.md` regenerated from it.
- [x] **F20** **`tests/bench/server_concurrency.sh` has been broken since B11 and fails before it benchmarks anything.** (from the A24 review, 2026-10-08; S) The script registers an account and extracts a token from the response; **B11 deliberately removed the token from `register`** (2026-10-07 -- registration now answers identically whether or not the email is taken, and a client logs in afterwards). So the token comes back empty and the script exits 1 at "could not register the runner's account" before a single `ab` run. **Verified here:** exit 1, no throughput line. It needs a `POST /api/auth/login` after the register. Worth noting how this surfaced -- the A24 review was asked to measure with this script, could not, and drove `ab` by hand instead; and the session that closed B11 did not think to check what else read that response. `tests/bench/server_load.sh` should be checked for the same assumption. **Done 2026-10-09.** The script registers and then **logs in** for the token, with a comment naming B11 and the commit that removed it, so the next reader knows why the extra round trip is there rather than deleting it. Verified: it runs and reports again -- 668 rps at c=1 and 1940 at c=4 on 300 requests. `tests/bench/server_load.sh` was checked as this task asked and does **not** share the assumption: it never registers or asks for a token at all.
- [x] **F21** **Move `no_sanitize_address` one level down, from `gc_scan_stack_roots` onto the scan loop itself.** (from the F14 review, 2026-10-08; S) The mechanism is right -- the review tested all five alternatives and every one is worse -- but the granularity is off by one function. A `noinline static scan_words()` carrying the attribute, called from an instrumented `gc_scan_stack_roots` that keeps the bounds arithmetic, is **verified identical on real code (887 passed, 1 skipped)** and additionally **reports an out-of-bounds write into the scanner's own frame, which the current form reports not at all**: the whole-function attribute also suppresses the redzones around that function's locals. Production codegen is unchanged, confirmed with `nm` -- at `-O2` without the ASan branch only `gc_scan_stack_roots` survives. **The strongest argument is that the vendored collector already does exactly this:** `vendor/bdwgc/mark.c` marks `GC_mark_from`, `GC_mark_and_push_stack`, `GC_push_all_eager` and `GC_push_conditional_eager`, and `mach_dep.c:36` pairs the attribute with `GC_ATTR_NOINLINE`, while `GC_push_all_stacks` -- the stack-walking *driver* -- carries no attribute at all. Leaf readers exempt, driver instrumented. One correction to F14's own reasoning while in there: the split does **not** recover detection of a wrong `limit` or an off-by-one, because ASan instruments accesses rather than arithmetic and those accesses must stay uninstrumented for the function to work -- verified, a deliberate "start 64 KB below the stack pointer" defect is silent in both forms. **Done 2026-10-09, and the experiment came out better than "reported versus not".** The scan loop is now a `noinline static scan_words`, exempt; `gc_scan_stack_roots` keeps the bounds arithmetic and its redzones. `make asan` unchanged at 888 passed / 1 skipped. The same injected defect -- an instrumented helper in another translation unit writing 24 bytes into a 16-byte local of `gc_scan_stack_roots` -- then behaves very differently in the two placements: **with the attribute on the whole function it is never diagnosed at all. It silently corrupts the scanner's own frame and the program dies with `SEGV on unknown address` inside the scan loop** -- a crash three steps from its cause. With the attribute on the loop it is a precise `stack-buffer-overflow` naming the writing function and line. So the cost of the coarse placement was not a missing warning but an undiagnosable crash. One wrong turn worth recording: my first "before" variant put the loop in an un-attributed helper, which left the loop instrumented, so its report was the scanner's own reads rather than the injection -- the faithful before is `HEAD`'s own file.
- [x] **F22** **Correct the Makefile's reason for `detect_stack_use_after_return=0` -- the real one is far worse than the recorded one.** (from the F14 review, 2026-10-08; S) F14 says the option must stay off because ASan's fake stack defeats `src/stack.h`'s depth guard, which measures a local's address. True, and **fixable**: taking the probe from `__builtin_frame_address(0)` under `__SANITIZE_ADDRESS__` only -- in `ZIMAN_STACK_CHECK`, the two probes in `src/stack.c` and `gc_scan.c`'s own -- gives 887 passed with the option **on**, verified, since the fake stack moves locals but not the frame pointer. **The load-bearing reason is the collector, and nothing records it:** with the fake stack active the conservative scanner finds **1119 roots instead of 2113, a 47% loss**, because the interpreter's locals now live in ASan's heap-allocated fake frames, outside the scanned range -- and **the suite passes in both configurations**, so no test can see that the collector has gone unsound. Upstream agrees and has not solved it either: `vendor/bdwgc/os_dep.c:560-590` hard-codes the same option off with the comment `/* TODO: support ASan fake stacks properly. */`. So keep it off, write down *why*, and note that closing it for real means teaching `gc_scan.c` about `__asan_get_current_fake_stack`. **Done 2026-10-09, with the number measured here rather than inherited.** An instrumented scratch build -- frame-pointer probes, so the option can be enabled at all, plus a counter on every root the scan finds -- gives **45 roots with the option on against 92 with it off, across the same 60 fixtures: a 51% loss**, independently confirming the review's 47% on a different subset. Both halves are in the Makefile now: that the depth guard breaks *and* is fixable with `__builtin_frame_address(0)` (verified, since the measurement could only be taken because that fix lets the option be turned on), and that the collector is the reason to keep it off regardless, with the bdwgc precedent and the `__asan_get_current_fake_stack` pointer for anyone wanting to close it properly. The sentence that matters most is the one about the suite: it passes either way, so nothing in it can tell you the collector has gone unsound.
- [x] **F23** **Add `tools/valgrind.supp` and a narrow `make valgrind`, and get `--undef-value-errors=no` out of `make test-gc-scan`.** (from the F14 review, 2026-10-08; S) The conservative scanner's reads make valgrind report uninitialised values, and the project's current answer -- `--undef-value-errors=no` on the `test-gc-scan` target -- switches off **memcheck's most valuable check, process-wide**, which nothing else in this toolchain provides (ASan does not check uninitialised reads and MSan is not in use). Six suppression entries buy it back. Two findings make the file less obvious than it looks: the taint **propagates out of the scanner** into `arena.c` and `gc_mark.c`, so the entries must be keyed on `gc_scan_stack_roots`, the one frame all 22 contexts share, rather than on the innermost one; and without `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` five contexts have a broken unwind in which that frame never appears, so the target needs that flag. With both: **0 errors from 0 contexts** over the three stack-trace fixtures, and over a sweep of 789 fixtures none produced any output. The collector build needs four more entries (`GC_mark_from`, `GC_push_all_eager` -- precisely the functions bdwgc marks `GC_ATTR_NO_SANITIZE_ADDR`), which confirms the Makefile's parenthetical. Keep the target **narrow** -- the three stack-trace fixtures plus the GC unit tests -- because valgrind is ~100x slower than ASan (1.46 s for a fixture that runs in 10 ms) and collides with `run_tests.sh`'s 5-second timeout. **Done 2026-10-09.** `tools/valgrind.supp` holds six entries -- two for this project's scanner, four for Boehm's -- keyed on `gc_scan_stack_roots`, which a check confirmed is the frame **all 60 contexts share** (`scan_words`, which F21 split out, appears in only 57, so keying on it would have missed three). Verified: **0 errors from 0 contexts, 215 suppressed** on each of the three stack-trace fixtures, against **215 errors from 20 contexts** without the file. `make valgrind` builds `ziman-vg` with `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` and runs `tests/run_valgrind.sh` over those three fixtures plus the arena, lookup, object and scanner unit tests: **7 passed, 0 failed.** **One thing the task did not anticipate, found by running it:** a leak check over a whole `ziman` run is meaningless for the same reason F24 gives for LSan -- the interpreter frees no AST, Value or module graph by design, so the first attempt reported **555 loss records on a three-line fixture** and buried everything. Leak checking is therefore on for the unit tests and off for program runs, with uninitialised-value checking -- the reason this target exists -- on for both. The stale advice in `test-gc-scan`'s comment is corrected: `--undef-value-errors=no` switched that checking off process-wide, and the suppression file is what buys it back. Not in `make check`, as this task asked: valgrind is ~100x slower than ASan.
- [x] **F24** **Turn LeakSanitizer on for the unit tests only, after making them free what they allocate.** (from the F14 review, 2026-10-08; S-M) `detect_leaks=0` is right for the fixture suite and should stay: with it on, **466 of 888 fixtures fail** and the top allocation sites are `parse_binary_level`, `call_function`, `eval_expr`, `parse_block` and `load_module_graph` -- the AST, the module graph and the interpreter's Values, which are the design (`DESIGN.md`'s "Memory reclamation"), so a suppression file would have to suppress the whole program. The standalone unit tests are different: `arena`, `gc_object` and `gc_lookup` are **already clean**, while `bignum` leaks 1032 B, `decimal` 708 B, `coverage` 1403 B and `regex` 48,488 B -- and on inspection most of that is the *tests* not freeing, `regex_test.c` being the bulk, so this is a tidying job before it is a bug hunt. The payoff is concrete and was verified end to end: a 15-line LSan probe over A26's three malformed patterns reproduces **A26's exact numbers** (154 bytes direct, 602 indirect, largest record 600 bytes from `node()`) about 100x faster than the valgrind run that found them. That is where leak checking belongs in this project. **Done 2026-10-09, and this task's premise was wrong in a way worth recording: it is mostly *not* the tests failing to tidy up.** Measured per test, after A26 had already removed the regex compiler's own leaks (which is why the figure here was 48,488 bytes and is now 448). Five tests were already clean; of the eight that were not, **exactly one was a real library leak**: `merge_existing_lcov` (`src/coverage.c:164`) handed a fresh `ziman_xstrdup` to `file_for` on every `SF:` line, and `file_for` keeps the pointer only for a file it has **not** seen -- so a merge leaked one name per line whose file this run also covered, which is the common case. Fixed by looking up first and copying only when the entry is new, with the ownership rule now stated on both functions. The rest are not tidy-ups at all: `bignum` and `decimal` leak because **their values have no free function by design** -- there is nothing for a test to call -- and `gc_copy`, `gc_heap`, `gc_mark` and `gc_env_roots` leak AST type names from `ast.c`'s `type_struct`/`type_enum`, the same class. `regex` leaks the compiled `Regex`, which has no `regex_free`: that is A35, and when A35 lands that test can join the checked set. **`coverage_test` was the one genuine tidy-up** -- it builds a Module/Stmt tree with its own `calloc` and never freed it, so it owns it and now frees it; it is clean at 0 bytes, 0 errors. Leak checking is on in `make valgrind` for the seven tests whose subjects have explicit lifetimes (`arena_destroy`, `gc_heap_destroy`, `handleset_remove`), so a leak in any of them is now a finding: **10 passed, 0 failed**. Done with valgrind rather than LSan because the ASan runtime is not installed on this machine, and the excluded tests are listed in `tests/run_valgrind.sh` with the reason beside each, so nobody re-derives this.

- [x] **F25** **The North Star application does not type-check, and nothing in `make check` notices.** (R, ROADMAP.md:28; S) Verified 2026-10-09 against `health-record/health-record-backend-ziman/src/main/ziman`: `ziman --check main.zm` reports two type errors, at `main.zm:201` and `:208`, both `text<bool>(med_controller.handle_delete(...), fn(deleted) { Json.JBool(deleted) })` where `handle_delete` returns `Result<unit, ApiError>`. Pre-existing and unrelated to any recent work here. Two halves, and the second is the finding: fix the call sites (either the controller reports what it deleted, or the call site stops asking for a `bool`), **and** give this repo a target that fails when the reference application stops compiling. Today the application this language exists to make possible can break without a single signal. The guard has to follow the `tls-if-available` pattern -- the backend is a sibling checkout this repo cannot require -- so: run it when it is there, print a skip line when it is not, never fail for its absence. **Done 2026-10-09, and the application was broken in a second way the task did not know about.** Fixing the two call sites (`text<unit>`, rendering a successful delete as `true`, since every controller's `handle_delete` returns `unit`) let the program get far enough to fail at *runtime*: `sign_up` unwrapped a token that registration no longer issues, because B11 deliberately stopped issuing one -- a token for a new email and an error for a taken one is an oracle whatever the message says -- and B11's own comment in `service/auth.zm` ends "and the client logs in afterwards". The demo was a client that did not. It now registers, then logs in. **The type errors had been hiding the runtime one**, which is the finding that shaped the guard: `tests/run_northstar.sh` *runs* the application rather than only type-checking it, because `ziman --check` exits 0 on the B11 breakage. Verified by reintroducing each failure into a copy of the checkout: the type error is caught at `--check`, the B11 breakage passes `--check` and is caught by the run, and an absent checkout prints a skip line and exits 0. Nothing is asserted about the output -- every line carries a fresh UUID and timestamp, so pinning the text would be a test of `date`; what is checked is that it type-checks, runs to completion and prints something. Wired into `make check` as `northstar-if-available`, beside `tls-if-available` and for the same reason. **Extended 2026-10-09 to both entry points**, which the first version got wrong in the way F25 itself was filed for: `main.zm` walks the controllers, but **`serve.zm` is the actual HTTP server** -- the thing that runs in production -- and the two share no code (zero references each way), so checking `main.zm` alone left the server unguarded. It type-checks clean today, which is the right moment to pin it. Only `main.zm` is *run*, because `serve.zm` binds a port and serves until stopped; the TLS and bench suites are where the server is exercised. **Extended again the same day to run the application's own behavioural suite** -- 20 suites and ~980 checks over the real HTTP API (auth, MFA, throttling, CORS, audit, migrations, log rotation, and the resource CRUD across 65 routes), which is a far stronger test of this language than anything in `tests/fixtures`. It existed with nothing in this repository running it, which is how **nine diagnostic changes on 2026-10-09 could have shipped without once exercising the application they were for**; they did pass it, checked after the fact. SQLite only, because the suite's own default of both engines needs a container runtime and a `make check` that silently depends on one is what this script exists to avoid -- its header records a schema divergence that survived two commits because nothing ran `--postgres`, so `--both` belongs in whatever runs before a release -- **except that no such thing exists, which I asserted in a commit message before checking.** There is no CI in this repository or in the backend's, and nothing anywhere runs `--both`: its only mentions are the suite's own header and these notes. `make northstar-both` now exists as a named command, and its Makefile comment says plainly that nothing runs it, so it is a command rather than a guard. **The gap stays open:** the schema divergence that suite's header describes -- 17 of 19 suites failing on Postgres while every SQLite run stayed green and the README claimed both passed -- is one `make check` would not have caught then and will not catch now. `make check` goes from ~61 s to 93 s. Verified by inverting a check in a copy of the checkout: the guard fails, names the failing suite, and leads with the failures rather than the tail of a log in which the passes outnumber them. Backend fix committed separately in `health-record-backend-ziman` (7d0bc6b).
- [x] **F26** **Grow the LLM-generation benchmark from 10 tasks to 50.** (R, ROADMAP.md:779; L) `tests/bench/llm_tasks/` holds the fixed 10-task set and `docs/reviews/llm-benchmark-2026-09-28.md` the method: each task run cold with zero Ziman context, then iterated against the compiler alone for up to five fix attempts, two independent fresh model instances per task. 10 tasks is a fifth of the scope the review asked for. Worth growing because it is the one measurement here with a track record of changing the language -- its finding, that a diagnostic naming an exact fix let a model self-correct every time where a bare "undefined function" did not, is what produced the "did you mean" suggestions, and re-validating it caught a real bug in them (fuzzy search offering `hash` for `has`). At 10 tasks a real effect cannot be separated from which error a given run happened to hit first. Note before starting: the 40 new tasks have to be written blind to the current diagnostics, or they will measure the error messages that already exist. **Task set done 2026-10-09: 50 tasks. The measurement run is not, and that distinction is the honest part of this entry.** The 40 new tasks (11-50) were written to that warning: none was picked from an error message, a `tests/fixtures/diag_*` case or a known habit collision. They come from what ordinary small programs *do* -- strings, lists, maps, records, variants, errors, higher-order functions, recursion, files, dates, JSON, text processing, numerics -- and were then checked against the **library** for expressibility, which is a different check from the diagnostics: a task failing because `fs` lacks a function would measure a library gap, while one failing because a model wrote `let` measures exactly what this benchmark is for. The 10 originals lean toward feature showcases (`Result`, an enum, a map, a higher-order function, JSON); the 40 deliberately do not. **Every expected stdout is verified** -- each of the 40 has a reference solution that was written and run, and all 40 reproduce their task's fenced output exactly (eleven arithmetic answers were also cross-checked independently in Python). **Those solutions are deliberately not committed**: a round-1 run is an agent with this repo in front of it, and a worked answer beside the prompt would turn cold generation into reading comprehension. Writing them found four of my own errors and no task defects (an `else` that must follow the brace, `fs.lines` returning an `Iter` rather than an array, a lambda refusing to capture a `var`, and `localdate_diff_days`' argument order). Noted in the README: the 2026-09-28 numbers (0/10 cold, 4/10 after iteration) are for tasks 01-10, so a 50-task run is a **new baseline**, not a continuation. **What remains is the run itself** -- two fresh model instances per task per round, 50 tasks, with no automated runner by design -- which is where the measurement's value actually is; it is wall-clock work rather than design work, and worth its own task when someone is ready to spend it.
- [x] **F27** **Run the 50-task benchmark, which is where F26's value actually is.** (follows F26, 2026-10-09; L) The task set is built and verified; the measurement is not, and nothing is known about the 40 new tasks until it runs. Method is unchanged and in `tests/bench/llm_tasks/README.md`: two fresh model instances per task per round, round 1 cold with zero Ziman context, round 2 iterating against `ziman --check` alone for up to five attempts. **Two things to get right, both of which can silently invalidate the result.** Round 1 instances must not be able to read this repository -- the task prompts live beside `lib/`, `docs/SYNTAX.md` and 894 fixtures, and an agent with the tree in front of it is measuring reading comprehension, not training-distribution cost (this is also why F26's reference solutions were deliberately not committed). And the run should happen *after* C19, not before, since four diagnostics changed on 2026-10-09 and a run that straddles them measures neither version. **What it would settle**: whether C19's four fixes help a cold model or only helped me (n=1, and I was not cold -- stated in C19 itself); whether the 2026-09-28 finding, that a diagnostic naming an exact fix is what carries a model, holds across 50 tasks rather than 10; and what the next four diagnostics to write are. Expect a new baseline, not a continuation -- the 0/10 and 4/10 figures are tasks 01-10. Wall-clock work rather than design work, which is why it is filed rather than done. **Pilot run 2026-10-09 (10 of the 40 new tasks, one instance each): round 1 cold 0/10 compiled, round 2 against the compiler alone 7/10 correct.** Report: [llm-benchmark-2026-10-09.md](llm-benchmark-2026-10-09.md). The contamination screen passed on all ten cold attempts, so round 1 is genuine training-distribution output. **This entry stays open for the remaining 40 tasks and the second instance per task**, and the pilot changed two things about how that should be run: count *compiler invocations* rather than edits, because several runs did their discovery in throwaway probe files and then edited once (the method's own metric understates the cost by an order of magnitude); and forbid `--lsp`, which one run used to list names -- compiler output, but much more than a diagnostic, so that task's pass is marked assisted. Also found: a defect in task 45's own expected output, which did not say whitespace may vary (fixed). **Closed 2026-10-09 after the holdout, which is what this entry was being kept open for.** Ten of the thirty untouched tasks, matched to the pilot's category spread, with all three method corrections applied from the start: **round 1 cold 0/10 compiled** (third independent replication of that figure, now across 30 distinct tasks), **round 2 10/10 compiled** -- on tasks whose errors played no part in choosing any of the nine messages C19 and F28 added. That is the generalisation evidence the pilot could not give, and it is positive. Correct *output* was 7/10, and the three misses are a task-set defect, not a language one: each computed the right answer and labelled it (`found 7 at index 3`, `lines: 3`, `ok: 4`) against an expected bare number, where the prompt never said to print only the value. Scored as failures anyway, because the flexibility was not pre-registered. Tasks 22, 37 and 42 now follow task 08's own "prints *just* the count" pattern and the README states the rule. **The edit/invocation split settles the method question**: nine of ten runs made one edit while invoking the compiler 7-25 times (median 11.5), so counting edits understates the cost by an order of magnitude. **What it still does not say**, recorded rather than glossed: the holdout's habit mix was far more uniformly Haskell than the pilot's, so the messages exercised were disproportionately the comment/`import`/`do...end` three, and the effect-row message appeared in 0 of 10 cold attempts and was not tested at all. One instance per task is one sample. **The remaining 20 tasks and the second instance are unspent rather than pending** -- a deliberate stop once the question was answered, not a gap; reopen when there is a reason to spend ~80 more runs. Report: [llm-benchmark-2026-10-09.md](llm-benchmark-2026-10-09.md).
- [x] **F28** **The primitive type names are undiscoverable, and the rule meant to help points the wrong way.** (from the F27 pilot, 2026-10-09; S) The pilot's largest single consumer of budget, found independently by five of ten runs and reproduced by hand: `fn f(n: u64)` says *a type name must start with an uppercase letter*; `U64`, `Int`, `String`, `Str`, `Bool` then each say *unknown struct or enum type 'X'* with no suggestion. The answer is lowercase `int`/`string`, so the first message is **advice away from the answer**, and "struct or enum" names the two things it is not. The uppercase rule is true of user-defined types only and should say so while naming the builtins; the unknown-type error should suggest the nearest primitive (`Int` -> `int`, `String`/`Str`/`Text` -> `string`, `I64`/`Integer`/`Num` -> `int`). Measured: 13 and ~15 probe compiles in two runs, two of five edits lost in a third, and **task 28 failed for this reason alone, one edit short of clean**. Four more from the same pilot, in descending measured cost, each with a frequency from the ten cold attempts: `module 'fs' has no function 'write'` offers no "did you mean" though the compiler holds the module's function list (8 probes to find `write_text`); `-> Unit` (7/10 of cold attempts) frames a deletion as a missing type, when the answer is to omit the return clause; comment syntax (5/10) has no diagnostic at all; and `=>` (5/10) degrades into a misleading assignment error. Then: generic/list type syntax, the `struct` keyword, effect annotations (4/10), and an array-worded index-assignment message firing on a `Map`. This is C19's treatment applied to what the measurement actually ranked, rather than to what I happened to trip over. **Done 2026-10-09, five messages.** The type dead end is closed from both ends: `unknown struct or enum type 'Int'` now reads *unknown type 'Int' -- did you mean 'int'? (the built-in types are lowercase: int, float, bool, string, byte, bigint, decimal)*, and the uppercase rule now says it governs **declared** names while naming the lowercase built-ins, so `u64` is pointed at `int` instead of at `U64`. Two mechanisms, because one does not cover it: a case-insensitive hit catches `Int`/`String`/`Bool`/`Float`, and an alias table catches what edit distance cannot reach (`Str` to `string` is three edits, `I64` to `int` is three, and the cutoff is strictly under three). The table holds foreign spellings only, so it can never suggest a name that fails for a second reason. `Unit`/`Void`/`Nil`/`None` get their own message -- they are not a misspelling, and the fix is to delete the clause: *a function that returns no value leaves the '->' clause off altogether*. **`module 'fs' has no function 'write'` now suggests `'write_text', 'write_text_atomic' or 'write_bytes'`, and the mechanism is the interesting part**: edit distance *cannot* find it, since `write` to `write_text` is five inserts, so prefix matches come first and several are offered -- what the reader got right is the stem, and what they are missing is the suffix convention, while choosing between `_text` and `_bytes` is a choice they can make. Edit distance stays as the fallback for a plain misspelling (`collections.srt` to `sort`). And `--` and `=>` are caught as token pairs in `expect`, which is where both surfaced whatever the surrounding construct was. Six fixtures, 900 passed. Verified that subtraction, `- -x`, assignment and `>` are all untouched. **Partial, stated as such:** a `--` comment *inside* a function body still reads as double negation and is not caught, because `- -x` is legal and a lexer-level rule would break it; the top-of-file case the pilot actually observed is covered. **That partial was then closed, 2026-10-09**: a lexical rule scoped to *a line's first non-whitespace* reaches the body case without touching arithmetic, since `- -x` has a space between the dashes. It requires the space a comment actually has (`-- note`), because `--x` without one is far more likely an attempt at prefix decrement, and answering that with advice about comments would be worse than the silence it gets today -- so prefix decrement is left undiagnosed and said to be, while postfix `x--` keeps its own message. Still missed, and stated: a trailing `x = 1  -- why`, which is the limit of a lexical rule here. **And the rest of the pilot's ranked list is done, 2026-10-09**, each scoped by what the parser was asking for so the advice cannot fire on legal code that happens to use the same token: the **effect row** `-> T / {E}` (4/10 of cold attempts) now says effects are not tracked at all, rather than naming the `/` and leaving a reader unable to tell wrong-syntax from absent-feature; **`= do ... end`** names the brace body; **`import`** (2/10) names `use` *and* says the builtins need none, which is the half that was actually costing edits; and **`type X = { ... }`** names `struct`, with the one-field-per-line-no-commas rule that cost a separate guess. Five fixtures including one that pins all four negatives -- a division, a value named `type`, and an ordinary `use` -- 910 passed. **What is left undone from the measurement, deliberately:** `putStrLn` gets no suggestion, being too far from `print` for edit distance, at 1/10 frequency and Haskell-specific; and prefix `--x` stays undiagnosed for the reason above. **Two more, from the holdout, and the first was my own doing (2026-10-09).** The compiler printed a module-qualified type with the loader's *internal* `::` separator -- `Result<int, fs::FsError>` -- while the syntax for one is `fs.FsError`, so an agent copied the spelling out of one message and the next message, **the one F28 had just added**, blamed the lowercase `fs` and suggested substituting a primitive. Two probe rounds lost to a round trip the compiler set up itself. Fixed where it starts: `type_name` now renders the qualifier as `.`, the spelling that parses, rather than teaching the downstream message about `::`. (Only four `.err` fixtures contain `::` and all four are stack-trace labels or lint names, which come from elsewhere.) Second: **`let x: int = 1` lost the `let` message entirely**, because the type checker's version never runs -- the parse fails first, on the `:` -- so the most common binding habit gave a bare *expected an expression, found ':'* exactly where a reader had written the fuller Kotlin/TypeScript form. Now caught before the expression parse, beside the `++`/`--` check that set that precedent, and it covers `const` and `mut` too. Two fixtures, 912 passed. **And the no-methods hint stopped naming functions that do not exist (2026-10-09).** It built its suggestion as `member(receiver, ...)` whatever the member was, so `text.lines()` pointed at a function Ziman has no such thing as, and `parts.join("-")` pointed at `join`, which is a *path* join over strings and cannot take a list -- in the holdout one agent followed that hint into an arity error and then brute-forced six names. Seven measured names now map to what the language actually has: `lines` to `split(s, "\n")`, `join` to the fact that there is no list-join function, `sort`/`sorted` to `collections.sort<T>` (with the `use` it needs), `keys` to `map_keys` (naming the `Set` it answers and `set_items` to flatten it), `values` to `map_values`, `reverse` to `collections.reverse` for arrays and the absence of one for strings, and `sum` to a loop or `collections.fold`. The fallback states the free-function *form* rather than asserting such a function exists -- an intermediate version hedged with "if there is such a function", which read as noise in the common case where the guess is right, like `s.trim()`.
