One fixture, 913 passed. **Validated by re-running the three tasks that failed, from the same cold attempts and the same five-edit budget: all three now compile and all three are correct, taking the pilot's round 2 from 7/10 to 10/10** (18 in 4 edits, 23 in 2, 28 in 1 -- it had failed one edit short, and the lowercase-types list handed it `bigint`, collapsing a hand-written decimal-digit multiplication into a three-line loop). Measured on the same ten tasks that produced the ranking, so it shows the fixes address what was measured, not that they generalise. **Two more gaps the validation itself surfaced, also fixed:** the `bigint`/`decimal`/`byte` literal suffixes (`var x: bigint = 1` named no path to `1n`, and `bigint(1)` is rejected too), and reading a map -- `get(m, k)` suggested `set`, the *mutator*, after which an agent brute-forced six names before `map_get`, while `m["k"]` said only *cannot index into Map (not an array)*; both now name `map_get` and its `Option<V>`, and the set case names `contains`. Nine fixtures in all, 903 passed.
- [x] **F29** **A packed byte-buffer type for `net` -- already done, and the roadmap entry asking for it was stale.** (R, ROADMAP.md:1622; S) The entry read "`[byte]` is an array of interpreter values -- wasteful for bulk data", which has not been true for some time: `[byte]`, `[bool]`, `[int]` and `[float]` are stored packed, one real byte/byte/int64/double per element rather than an 80-byte boxed value, and `docs/SYNTAX.md` "Arrays" says so. **Measured rather than taken on the doc's word:** two million `[byte]` elements cost **40.9 MB**, two million boxed one-field structs **540.2 MB** -- 13x, or about 20 bytes per element including the doubling growth slack, against about 270. So there was nothing to build. The fourth stale roadmap entry found in two days (after the map type, the North Star architecture list, and the benchmark task count), and the one shape my `check_not_yet.py` extension cannot catch: the text neither claims absence with "No X" nor defers with "Still ahead", it asserts a fact about the implementation. Recorded in the entry with the measurement, so the next reader does not re-derive it.
- [x] **F30** **Unix-domain sockets in `net`.** (R, ROADMAP.md:1622; S-M) `net.connect_unix(path)` and `net.listen_unix(path)`, which `open_socket` could not reach -- it resolves a host and a port through `getaddrinfo` and a Unix socket has neither, only a filesystem path. A local PostgreSQL or MySQL usually listens on one, which is the concrete consumer the entry named. The `Socket` behaves identically afterwards: `read`, `write`, `lines`, `set_timeout`, `close` and even `start_tls` are unchanged, because they all work on the descriptor. **Two differences from a TCP listener, and both are reported rather than papered over.** `sun_path` is 108 bytes including its terminator, far under `PATH_MAX`, so a long path is refused with a message naming the limit *and* saying it is the operating system's -- silent truncation would leave a server bound to a path it did not ask for, which is the classic failure here. And `bind` fails when the path already exists, including on a socket left behind by a process that has gone: **it is deliberately not unlinked first**, because a stale socket and a live one look identical from inside the call and removing the wrong one would take down a running server, so the error names `fs.remove` and says why it will not do it for you. One thing found by testing rather than by reading: `listener_port` answers **-1** for such a listener, not the 0 my own doc comment first claimed -- the comment and SYNTAX.md now say -1, which is what the code does. Fixture `net_unix_socket` covers the round trip, the refused rebind and the over-long path; 914 passed.
- [x] **F31** **`Map`/`Set` constant factors: the ranked first item is mis-ranked, and the one cheap fix left for the real cause does nothing.** (R, ROADMAP.md:714; M) No code shipped, which is the right outcome and took four measurements to establish. The entry ranks "`get` allocates an `Option` and `contains` does not, which costs ~9x" as "the largest and clearest win available", attributing it to "the wrapper allocation and the collection work it causes". **Reproduced the gap and then took it apart:** `map_get` is 6,424ns net against `map_get_or`'s 106ns, a 60x rather than 9x. But an `Option.Some` allocation measured *on its own* is **94-123ns**, so the wrapper is ~2% of that, not nine tenths. The actual mechanism: the same enum allocation costs **165ms per million with nothing else alive and 6,829ms with a 100,000-entry map alive -- a 41x slowdown of an allocation that never touches the map**. So (1) is not a separate win at all; it is (2), the `gc_lookup` block scan, seen through whatever happens to allocate. Removing the `Option` would move the cost onto the next allocation in the loop. Also worth keeping: **`map_get_or` is already the non-allocating path at 106ns, against CPython's 87ns for `dict.get`** -- the row the entry reports as 21-34x is 1.2x if you use it. **Then the one fix the entry suggested for (2) was built and reverted:** a one-entry last-block-found cache in `gc_lookup`, guarded by a new arena block-list version counter so a reclaimed block could not dangle. Paired interleaved rounds: set 231->216ms, get 565->570ms, the 41x case 6,782->6,821ms -- every one inside the run-to-run spread, so it does nothing, because marking's locality is not block-local the way the entry assumed. Reverted, like the three before it; the finding is what is kept. Four cheap fixes for (2) are now measured and ruled out, leaving only the per-arena sorted block index, which nobody has tried and which is not cheap. **One correction to my own reasoning, recorded because it nearly stopped the investigation:** I first concluded the cost was *not* collection because `ziman-nogc` showed the same figure -- forgetting that `nogc` names the absence of *Boehm*, not of collection; that build uses the project's own mark-sweep and collects just as much. 914 passed.
- [x] **F32** **Runtime metrics through the `metrics` module (the plan's item 3).** (R, ROADMAP.md's metrics entry; S) `metrics.record_runtime()` samples four gauges -- `ziman_gc_heap_bytes`, `ziman_gc_collections`, `ziman_threads`, `ziman_open_descriptors` -- over two new natives for the collector's numbers; threads and descriptors already existed in `os`, so the native surface grew by exactly what was missing. **`server.metrics_response` calls it**, so a scrape carries them without every application remembering to, which is what Prometheus' own client libraries do with their process and GC collectors -- and an application that does not want them can still call `metrics.render()` itself. I wrote that into the doc comment *before* wiring it up, then caught it: the comment claimed `metrics_response` did this while it did not, which is the same class of false claim this session has spent its time deleting elsewhere. **Gauges rather than counters, even for the monotonic collection count**, because a counter in this module is something *we* add to and these are read out of the runtime; modelling one as a counter would claim an accounting we are not doing, and Prometheus computes a rate over a gauge happily. **The two builds answer differently and say so:** heap bytes are the process's mapped heap under the default build and the *calling thread's* reserved bytes under `nogc` (the heap is per thread there), and the collection count is -1 under `nogc` -- -1 rather than 0, since 0 would read as "no collections have happened", a claim that build cannot make. Verified on both. **Channel depths deliberately omitted:** a useful depth needs per-channel identity, so a label per channel, and an unbounded label set is precisely what item 1's 128-series cap exists to stop; `channel_len` already answers for a channel you hold. Fixture `metrics_runtime_gauges` pins the shape rather than the values, since only the `nogc` -1 is fixed across machines. 915 passed.
- [x] **F33** **A project-root marker, as a search path rather than a new root.** (R, ROADMAP.md's `ziman test` entry; S) `ziman.toml` marks a project's root, and its directory joins the `use` search path -- after the importing file's own directory, before `ZIMAN_MODULE_PATH`. So a test under `test/` can write `use src/util/math`, the same project-relative path a file beside the subject would. **The design changed once I read the code, and that is the substance of this task.** The entry asks for a marker "whose directory is where `use` paths start instead of the entry file's own directory" -- a new root. But `ZIMAN_MODULE_PATH` already exists and already solves the stated use case (the loader's own comment on it names the reference backend's missing unit tests as the motivation), so a marker that changed the root would have been **a second way to resolve a module**, against the principle that keeps this language small. I built the root version first, then reverted it: the marker now feeds the one search path that exists. Two ways to point at a directory -- a variable for a harness, a file in the tree for everyone else -- is not two mechanisms. Marker before variable, because a file committed in the tree describes that tree better than something a harness happened to export. **Contents are not read**, and SYNTAX.md says so plainly: the name is taken now so a later manifest lands in the file already marking the root instead of a second file appearing beside it. The search stops after eight levels, the same bounded-walk reasoning as lsp.c's six-level `main.zm` hunt, so a stray marker in a home directory cannot quietly claim everything beneath it. **Covered by `tests/run_module_path.sh`, a script rather than a fixture for a reason worth recording:** `run_tests.sh` runs a directory fixture's entry at `name/main.zm`, the top of that directory -- so the marker's directory and the entry's directory are the same one and the marker could never change an answer. The whole point is an entry *below* the root. Five cases: refused without a marker (naming where it looked), resolved with one, beside-the-file still winning, and `ZIMAN_MODULE_PATH` still working both with and without a marker present. In `make check`; 915 passed.
- [x] **F34** **`ziman --api [module]`: what a module offers, because name discovery was the benchmark's largest measured cost.** (tier 1 of the 2026-10-10 priorities; S-M) The roadmap never asked for this -- the 2026-10-09 generation runs did. With no documentation to hand, cold models brute-forced **eight** candidate names before reaching `fs.write_text`, **six** before `map_get`, and **thirteen to fifteen** probe compiles hunting `int` and `string`; one reached for `--lsp` completion *precisely because no command-line way to ask existed*. Diagnostics can name a fix for a habit somebody anticipated (which is what C19 and F28 did); they cannot enumerate a library. `--api fs` prints the `pub` types and function signatures in the language's own notation, `--api` alone lists the bundled modules and says the builtins need no `use`, and a `.zm` path works too, so it answers for a project's own modules. **Printed from the parsed declarations, not scanned out of text**, so it is what the compiler sees -- and `pub` only, since nothing else is callable from outside anyway (`net`'s `connect_unix_raw` stays out, and the test asserts that). **Type parameters and their bounds are printed, which is the detail that makes it worth having:** `fn sort<T: Ord>(items: [T]) -> [T]` is the signature, and the runs had to *probe* to discover that `collections.sort` needs its type argument spelled out -- a signature without them could not have told anyone. Enum variants carry their payload (`Io(string)`), and struct fields print `name: type`, the way Ziman writes them rather than the way C does, which the first version got backwards. `tests/run_api.sh`, 12 cases, in `make check` as `api-check`. **And my own guard caught me:** `check_xalloc.py` refused two bare `malloc` calls in the new code, which is the second time this session a house guard has rejected my own work rather than someone else's. 915 passed.
- [x] **F35** **The missing obvious library functions: `lines`, `reverse_string`, `collections.join`, `collections.sum`.** (tier 2 of the 2026-10-10 priorities... tier 1 item 2; S) Not a roadmap item either -- four functions a cold model reached for in the 2026-10-09 runs and this library did not have. A model assumes a stdlib, and an absence costs it more than a bad message does: one run brute-forced six names before `map_get`, another wrote a loop with `format` because there was no list join at all. **`lines(text)` is not `split(text, "\n")`**, which is what the F28 hint used to recommend: `split` leaves a `\r` on every line of CRLF text and leaves a trailing empty piece after a final newline, so `"a\nb\n"` splits into three where it has two lines. A final newline *terminates* the last line, which is how every line-oriented tool reads a file; a blank line in the middle is real and stays. **`reverse_string` reverses code points, not bytes** -- the byte version is the classic way to corrupt UTF-8, and `chars` already answers in code points, so `"añb"` comes back `"bña"` rather than mojibake. **`collections.join(items, sep)`** puts `sep` between rather than after. **`collections.sum(items: [int])`** is integers only on purpose: `+` traps on overflow here, so the answer is exact or an error -- verified, it stops with *integer overflow in '+'* rather than wrapping -- where a float total depends on summation order, which is a decision (naive, pairwise, Kahan) rather than a default, and `fold` expresses whichever one a caller wants. Placed where the language already puts their kind: `lines`/`reverse_string` beside the other string functions in the prelude, needing no `use`, following `result_to_option`'s precedent for Ziman-level prelude helpers; `join`/`sum` in `collections` beside `fold` and `frequencies`. **Four F28 hints now point at these instead of at workarounds**, and the fixture pinning the old `split(text, "\n")` wording failed when I changed it -- which is what pinning a message's text is for. 916 passed.
- [x] **F36** **Benchmark wave three: ten more tasks, and the first run with `--api`.** (tier 1 item 3; M) Ten of the twenty untouched tasks, one instance each, bringing the set's coverage to **30 of 50**. **Round 1: 0/10 compiled** -- fourth replication across 40 distinct tasks now, all clean on the screen, with this batch skewing Rust/Python (semicolons 3/10) where the holdout skewed Haskell, which is further evidence no single batch is the distribution. Five of the ten first-errors were messages written the day before. **Round 2 was allowed `ziman --api`, so this measures F34 rather than only the diagnostics: 10/10 compiled, 9/10 correct, and median compiler invocations fell from the holdout's 11.5 to 6** -- roughly half the traffic for the same outcome. Two cases were decisive and identical in shape: a run had hand-rolled a loop, `--api collections` showed the function that already did it (`distinct`, `frequencies`), and the program collapsed to one call. **The single wrong answer is the most valuable result:** task 25 compiles and dies at runtime on *split: separator must not be empty*, because it wanted a string's characters, `chars(s)` is exactly that, and **`--api` never showed it -- `chars` is a builtin and `--api` lists modules**. The same hole made task 46 conclude "there is no `keys` function" when `map_keys` is a builtin, and task 12 said `--api` "would have misled me". So the feature has a gap exactly where it was meant to help; closing it is the next commit (done: `ziman --api builtins`). Also reported independently by three runs: **one bad `let` yields two or three cascading errors**, so three bad lines gave seven -- the first is the good message and the rest is noise. Remaining: 10 tasks untouched (14, 17, 20, 34, 38, 39, 43, 47, 49, 50), and still one instance per task throughout.
- [x] **F37** **`ziman --api builtins`, closing the gap wave three found in `--api` itself.** (from F36, 2026-10-10; S) `--api` listed the bundled *modules*, so the things that need no `use` were invisible -- which is how a run hunting a string's characters never found `chars` and used `split(word, "")` instead, type-checking and then failing at runtime, and how another concluded "there is no `keys` function" when `map_keys` is a builtin. A command that answers "what exists" has to include them. **Two wrong turns on the way, both worth recording.** First I kept a list in `main.c` built from typecheck.c's `BUILTINS_NO_NAMED_ARGS` -- and `chars` was not in it, because that table is the subset of builtins taking no *named arguments*, not the builtins. An incomplete answer to "what exists" is worse than none: it reads as "`chars` does not exist", which is the exact failure being fixed. Second, printing `lib/prelude.zm` instead -- which declares 66 natives with real signatures, so `chars` appeared -- still missed `map_keys`, because *that* one is dispatched by name in the interpreter and declared nowhere. So the answer is both, and labelled: the prelude's declarations with their signatures, then the interpreter-dispatched names under a line saying they are names only and have no declared signature to print. Verified that both `chars` and `map_keys` are now findable. `tests/run_api.sh` grew to 15 cases; 916 passed.
- [x] **F38** **String interpolation: measured, and the AI-first case for it does not exist.** (tier 1 item 4; S -- a measurement, no code) I put this in tier 1 on the reasoning that "a model writes `f\"...\"` by reflex, so accepting the habit beats diagnosing it", and I had added a diagnostic for `f\"...\"` the day before on the same assumption. **The assumption is wrong.** Across all 30 cold attempts from the three benchmark waves, no interpolation form appears once: `f\"...\"` **0/30**, `\"${x}\"` **0/30**, `\"#{x}\"` **0/30**, a backtick template 1/30. What cold models actually reach for is an operator for concatenation -- first recorded here as `+` **16/30** and `++` **8/30**, 24 of 30 between them, **and re-counted on 2026-10-10 (F48) as `+` 6/30 and `++` 8/30, 14 of 30**, because this count had taken every `+` token rather than the string concatenations among them -- which is a different habit entirely. **And that one is already handled exactly as this language's thesis prescribes:** `'+' doesn't join strings ... use concat(a, b), or format(\"{}{}\", a, b) ...; convert a number with str(n)` fails at compile time with the sentence that fixes it, and several runs named it the best message in the set. So the roadmap's own framing of interpolation -- is it a second way to do what `format` does? -- is untouched by the evidence, and the entry stays open on its own terms rather than being promoted on an AI-first argument that the data does not support. **What the measurement does raise, as a decision rather than a task:** 24 of 30 looked like a strong habit (it is 14 of 30, and 6 of 30 for `+` itself -- see F48), and accepting `+` on strings would remove the most common collision there is -- against which stands an explicit, documented decision (`+` stays numeric-only "per principle #2", with `concat`/`str` as the one way, and overloading called "implicit coercion by another name"). That is a language-design call with a stated principle on the other side, so it is recorded with its evidence rather than taken. The tier ranking in memory is corrected.
- [x] **F39** **Two stale roadmap entries in tier 2 itself -- closures understated, generics finished.** (from the tier-2 review, 2026-10-10; S) Asked to show tier 2 item 5 with an example, I ran the cases instead of describing them, and the entry was wrong in the direction that matters: **capture by value has shipped.** A lambda captures a `val` (`fn(n) { n * factor }`), and may outlive the function that built it -- `multiplier(by)` returning `fn(n) { n * by }` captures a parameter and still works when called afterwards. The entry said "function types and references to named top-level functions, **no capture**". **What is actually left is one thing:** a lambda cannot capture a `var`, so it cannot hold mutable state, and the classic counter is inexpressible -- deliberate, since captures are by value and capturing a `var` would silently snapshot something expected to stay live. Not academic either: `lib/core/metrics.zm`'s own header gives exactly this as the reason its counters, gauges and histograms are native. So the open question is narrower and sharper than "closures": capture a `var` by reference, what owns the cell, and what it means across threads. Also found: **`multiplier(3)(5)` does not parse** (*expected ')', found '('*) -- calling a returned function needs it bound first, a parser limitation independent of capture, recorded separately. And **the generics entry's heading outlived its contents**: all five stages are struck through as implemented, while the heading still read "in progress, now a five-stage plan" -- the residue is only the stated limit that a generic body can do nothing with an abstract `T` beyond `Eq`/`Hash`/`Ord`, which was the `Row` bound, withdrawn by C18. **Both pinned by fixtures now** (`closure_capture_by_value`, `diag_lambda_captures_var`), 918 passed. **Worth recording about me rather than the file:** I had put "generics, remaining stages" in tier 2 by reading the heading rather than the struck-through body -- the same mistake behind four earlier stale-entry findings, made by me this time, and the shape `check_not_yet.py` cannot catch, since "in progress" is neither an absence claim nor a "still ahead" list.
- [x] **F40** **Tier 2's last item dropped: mutable state in a lambda is already expressible and already guarded.** (from the tier-2 review, 2026-10-10; S) Asked where capturing a `var` becomes *necessary*, I went looking for a pattern that is blocked rather than merely awkward, and there is none. **Capture is by value and what gets copied is a handle**, so a lambda holding a `MapBuilder` mutates shared state through it: a memoising closure with a live cache -- `mapb_contains`/`mapb_get_or`/`mapb_set` inside the lambda -- works today, no language change (fixture `closure_builder_capture`). The accumulate shape that looks like it needs mutable capture is what `fold` is for, with the accumulator in the signature rather than hidden in a capture. **And the unsafe case is a compile error:** a lambda that captured a builder carries `Type.func_captures_thread_bound`, set where the lambda is checked, so `spawn` refuses it even though its written type (`fn()`) says nothing about a builder (A14; fixture `diag_lambda_builder_crosses_thread`). So the `var` rule costs a spelling, not a capability -- and it buys something, since captures are by value: allowing a `var` would leave `fn(n) { n * factor }` ambiguous about whether it sees later writes, where a handle puts shared mutable state at its declaration. **Two corrections.** `metrics.zm`'s header gave the capture rule as the reason its counters are native; that overstates it, and I had repeated it -- the binding reason is that a builder cannot cross a `spawn` at all, so thread-shared state needs native atomics regardless. Comment fixed. **And a real gap found next door, a parser one:** a function type cannot be written as a *type argument*, so `Channel<fn(int) -> int>` is inexpressible, while `f: fn(int) -> int` as a parameter and `val f: fn(int) -> int` as an annotation both work. The consequence is that the `send`-side thread-bound guard is **present but unreachable** -- written for a case the parser forbids, which is why its comment describes a failure nobody can currently reproduce. Recorded in the roadmap so whoever fixes the parser knows the check is already waiting. 920 passed. **Tier 2 is now empty.**
- [x] **F41** **`log`: an optional JSON line format carrying a request id (tier 3 item 8, the metrics plan's item 5).** (2026-10-10; S) `log.logger(..., json: true)` writes one JSON object per line -- `time`, `level`, `prefix`, `request_id`, `message` -- and `with_request_id(l, id)` stamps every line a logger writes from then on, so a request's handler sets it once rather than threading an id through every call. Same shape as `with_prefix`, and an empty id clears it. **The design decision that mattered: escape through `json`, not by hand.** `log`'s own `single_line` writes a control byte as `\xNN`, which is not valid JSON, and a hand-rolled escaper here would have reproduced what A8 fixed -- invalid UTF-8 flowing into a document other parsers reject. `json_to_string(Json.JString(s))` quotes, escapes and substitutes U+FFFD. No cycle (`json` imports only `number.float_to_text`); the cost is that a program using `log` now loads `json`, which is the right trade against emitting malformed JSON. **Two details found by looking at the output rather than assuming it:** `level_name` pads to five characters so the *text* format's columns line up, and the prefix carries a trailing space for the same reason -- both would have arrived in a log index as part of the value, so JSON trims them and lowercases the level, which is what every JSON log schema uses. And `prefix`/`request_id` are omitted when empty rather than written as `null`. **One refactor it forced, worth keeping:** `with_prefix` named all of `Logger`'s fields by hand, which silently drops anything added later -- exactly how `json` and `request_id` would have been lost -- so it now uses `with`, and the file/rotation/console sink moved into a shared `write_line` so the two formats cannot drift on rotation, fallback or the console rule. Backward compatible: the new parameter is a trailing `Option` and the reference backend calls `logger(...)` with named arguments, so it type-checks untouched. Fixture `log_json_lines` logs to a file and prints it, so the harness compares stdout and the file sink is exercised; its four JSON lines were independently parsed with Python, and the escaped message round-trips to `he said \"hi\"\nand left`. 921 passed.
- [x] **F42** **W3C Trace Context in `http` (tier 3 item 9, the metrics plan's item 4).** (2026-10-10; M) `traceparent_of(req)` answers the incoming trace or a fresh one, `traceparent_header_for(p)` is the `Header` to send onward -- same `trace_id`, new `span_id`, sampling **inherited rather than retaken**, which is the whole point of propagating it -- and `p.trace_id` goes to `log.with_request_id` (F41), so one request's lines share an id across services. Built on what was already there: `http.header` to read it, `random.random_bytes` plus `number.byte_to_hex` to mint ids. **Four rules the spec states and implementations routinely miss, all enforced and all in the fixture:** an all-zero trace or span id is invalid *even though it is well-formed hex*; version `ff` is reserved; an upper-case hex field must be **rejected rather than lower-cased**, because the header is compared as bytes elsewhere; and undefined flag bits must be **ignored rather than refused**. Against those, a *future* version parses when the rest of the value does -- refusing it would break against a future sender for no gain, which is what a forward-compatible receiver owes. And a malformed header is **not an error**: the spec requires a receiver that cannot parse it to restart the trace, so `traceparent_of` returns a new one and the caller is none the wiser. Fixture `http_traceparent` runs the spec's own example value plus all four rejection rules, checks that a child keeps the trace and changes the span (asserting the parent's span id is *absent* from the child), and ends with the JSON log line carrying the trace id -- written to a file and printed, so the assertion is on stdout. 922 passed. **Still deferred, as the entry always said:** the OTLP exporter, which needs a background thread to batch.
- [x] **F43** **An `Encoder` (and `Decoder`) from a `#[derive(Json)]` pair (tier 3 item 10).** (2026-10-10; S) `server.json_decoder(from_json)`, `server.json_encoder(to_json)` and `server.json_list_encoder(to_json)`, so a typed JSON endpoint is one call per direction instead of a closure spelled out at every route. **Promoted out of the reference backend rather than designed here**, which is the part worth recording: it had already written these three itself, and its own comment is the evidence -- *"every endpoint below used to spell these out, 105 times between them"*, the list form running to seven lines each, *"which is 105 places for the media type or the JSON writing to drift apart"*. Any second JSON service would have written the same three functions, and that is what makes them the library's job rather than each application's. **`media` is deliberately not a parameter:** it is `application/json` by construction, an endpoint answering anything else wants `encoder`/`decoder` directly, and a media argument here would only let the pair disagree about what they are for. The two failures a caller can cause stay distinct, which the fixture pins: a body that is not JSON gives *malformed JSON: invalid value 'not' at position 0* (the parser's own message, worth passing on), while a body that is JSON but not the model gives the derived decoder's *missing field 'y'* -- different mistakes, and only the first is the parser's to explain. Costs `server` a dependency on `json`, which any JSON service loads anyway. Fixture `server_json_endpoint_helpers` round-trips a derived pair through both directions, covers the empty list, both failures, and the media type both sides declare. 923 passed.
- [x] **F44** **Indexed placeholders `{0}`/`{1}`: checked, still not needed, and the trigger is further off than the entry implies (tier 3 item 11).** (2026-10-10; S -- a measurement, no code) The entry says the justifying case is "a message catalog whose languages put the pieces in different orders". **There is no message catalogue anywhere** -- not in this library, not in the reference backend; the only mentions of one are `format_dynamic`'s own doc comment and `time.zm`'s note that it has **no locale, month-name or weekday support at all**. So the case is not merely absent, the groundwork is: indexed placeholders would be among the *last* pieces an i18n story needs, not the first, which sequences them behind a localisation decision nobody has asked for. **No AI-first case either:** `{0}`-style placeholders appear in **0 of 30** cold benchmark attempts, the same answer interpolation got (F38). `format_dynamic(template, args: [string])` exists and remains where they would land when something finally wants them. **Tier 3 is now complete**, and two of its four items ended as measured declines rather than code -- which is what ranking by evidence looks like when the evidence says no.
- [x] **F45** **A function type could not be a generic call's first type argument, and that position had no workaround.** (2026-10-10; S) Investigating the "parser gap" noted at the end of tier 2 found the note itself was wrong -- `Channel<fn(...)>` *is* expressible, and A14's send-side guard *is* reachable (see F46) -- but a real and narrower bug sat next to it. `next_looks_like_generic_call`, the one-token lookahead that tells `ident <` apart from a real comparison, did not list `TOK_FN`. So a function type parsed as a type argument everywhere *except* first in a generic call: `mapb_new<string, fn(int) -> int>()` worked (the leading `string` carried the lookahead and `parse_type` handled the rest) while `ab_new<fn(int) -> int>()` did not, failing as `expected a parameter name, found 'int'` -- raised by the *lambda* parser, several tokens from the real problem. **That position had no workaround**: dropping the type argument reaches the inference error, which says to "write it explicitly, e.g. 'ab_new<...>(...)'", the exact thing the parser refused -- so an ArrayBuilder of callbacks, or any builder whose first type parameter is a function, was inexpressible. Fixed by adding `TOK_FN` to the lookahead, which is no more ambiguous than the `[` and `Result` already there: a lambda is never a numeric operand, so `x < fn(...)` is not a real comparison in any program that type-checks. One fix covered all three call sites, since the uppercase ones commit on casing alone. Fixture `fn_type_as_generic_call_type_arg` covers first and second type-argument position, a function type round-tripped through a generic function, and that a real `<` is still a comparison.
- [x] **F46** **A14's send-side thread-bound guard was reachable and correct, and completely untested.** (2026-10-10; S) The tier-2 note claimed this guard was unreachable because `Channel<fn(...)>` could not be written. **Both halves were wrong.** `channel_new()` takes no type arguments -- the element type comes from the annotation -- so `val ch: Channel<fn() -> string> = channel_new()` has always parsed, and sending a builder-capturing lambda over it is correctly refused today with the full message naming `sb_to_string`/`ab_to_array`/`mapb_to_map`. What was actually missing was a **test**: every existing thread_bound fixture went through `spawn`, or sent a builder *directly* as the element type (`thread_bound_channel_rejected`); none sent a *lambda that had captured* one, which is the one case the `send` branch exists for and the one typecheck.c records as a real bug that once "type-checked and failed on the receiving thread instead". A working guard with no fixture is a guard that only looks like protection, which is the same shape as the recurring lesson about the second entry point -- so `try_send`, whose own `reject_thread_bound` call is marked only `/* see 'send' */`, got a fixture in the same pass. Three fixtures added: the `send` rejection, the `try_send` rejection, and `channel_of_lambdas` for the other half (a lambda capturing nothing thread-bound crosses a channel, is received on the far thread, and is called there).
- [x] **F47** **The "built-in generic type, not a function" diagnostic named a construction form that does not exist for three of its five types.** (2026-10-10; S) Found while verifying F45: `channel<fn(int) -> int>()` now reaches this message instead of a parser error, and the message was wrong. It printed one sentence for all five of `Map`/`Set`/`Option`/`Result`/`Channel` -- "construct one with 'X<...> { ... }', e.g. 'Map<string, int> { \"key\": value }'" -- which is right for Map and Set and **a dead end for the other three**: `Option<int> { }` is "no struct named 'Option'", and `Result<int, string> { }` and `Channel<int> { }` are syntax errors. A model that wrote `channel(...)`, followed the advice, and wrote `Channel<int> { }` earned a *second* failure, which inverts the stated goal that a habit should fail with the sentence that fixes it -- the sentence has to be true for that to mean anything, and here it also named a `Map` example while talking about a `Channel`. Now per-type: Map gets the literal and `mapb_new<string, int>()`, Set the literal, Option `Option<int>.Some(4)`/`.None`, Channel `channel_new()` and where the element type comes from, and Result the lowercase functions `ok(value)`/`err(problem)` **plus the note that `Ok(v)`/`Err(e)` are variant names in a `switch` arm, not constructors** -- the trap that `Ok(1)` gives "did you mean 'ok'?" rather than anything about Result. Fixture `generic_type_construction_forms` runs every form the message recommends, so the advice breaking is a test failure next to the message that gives it; `diag_builtin_generic_type_called` and `diag_result_is_not_a_function` pin the two least obvious sentences.
- [x] **F48** **`+` on strings: the standing decision written down on the right reason, and the frequency behind it re-counted (it was wrong).** (2026-10-10; S -- a measurement and a doc) Asked for an opinion on allowing `+` to join strings. Two things were wrong with the record this would have been decided from. **(1) The reason.** The backlog called `+` on strings "implicit coercion by another name", which does not survive being read closely: `"a" + "b"` converts nothing, so the coercion objection reaches only `"a" + 1`, while the habit being argued about is mostly the first form. What actually rules it out is **principle 1, "no operator overloading", by name** -- one token meaning numeric addition and string joining, selected by operand type, is exactly that -- plus principle 2 (`concat` is already the one way, and principle 2 forbids a second without removing the first), the quadratic-accumulation trap `--lint` already flags, and the point that accepting `"a" + "b"` while rejecting `"a" + 1` teaches "sometimes" where a uniform refusal teaches the fix. **(2) The frequency.** "24 of 30 cold attempts reach for `+`/`++`" was the strongest argument *for* changing it, and re-counting the surviving round-1 attempt files gives **14 of 30**: `++` 8/30 (the recorded figure, correct) and `+` on strings **6/30**, not 16/30. The original had counted `+` *tokens*, and `i = i + 1` or `(lo + hi) / 2` appears in most of these files. So allowing `+` would address **6 of 30 attempts, not 24**, and the majority of the remaining operator habit is `++`, which nobody proposes adding. **Cost of refusing, measured:** **14 of 14** attempts that reached for an operator resolved it -- 8 to `format`, 5 to `concat`, 1 restructured -- and **none kept the operator**. Recorded as a new DESIGN.md section; ROADMAP.md and the F38 entry that first published 16/8/24 are corrected in place. **Not measured, and named as the thing that would reopen the cost side:** how many edits a model spends on this specific message, which the surviving captures cannot show -- `diag.txt` holds only the *first* diagnostic and exists for only one of the three waves, so an earlier syntax error usually masks it. Decision: **unchanged, `+` stays numeric-only.**
- [x] **F49** **The LLM-generation benchmark is complete: all 50 tasks run.** (2026-10-10; M) Ran the last ten (14, 17, 20, 34, 38, 39, 43, 47, 49, 50), one instance each, same method as wave three (cold round 1; round 2 with `--check` and `--api` and a five-edit budget). **Round 1: 0 of 10 compiled**, all clean on the contamination screen -- a fifth independent replication, and the one that closes the set at **0 of 50**. **Round 2: 10 of 10 correct**, graded by script rather than by the agents' own reports. This batch's habit mix was the most uniform yet: **`let` in 10 of 10**, semicolons 5, `mut` 4, methods 3, capitalised `Int`/`String` 3; `--` comments, `++` and every interpolation form absent from all ten, consistent with F38/F48. **Process failure worth recording:** the first batch of ten was generated from prompts I transcribed by hand instead of each task's verbatim `## Prompt` block, and four diverged enough to change the problem (17 a different list, 20 a different range, 34 different states and step count, 49 missing the "space-separated" clause the expected output depends on). The whole batch was discarded rather than the four, and the wave re-run with each agent reading its own `prompt.txt`; the voided attempts are kept with a note. **Also corrected in the report:** I first wrote a "37 of 40" round-2 aggregate that was never measured -- the waves do not report a common measure (some counted compiled, some correct), so the report now states 40/40 compiled on tasks 11-50, 29/30 correct where correctness was reported, and 4/10 for the pre-fix tasks 01-10, rather than one invented 50-task figure. **Remaining gap, unchanged:** every per-task number is n=1; a second instance per task is still unrun.
- [x] **F50** **The "Ziman has no methods" message does not survive method chaining -- the common shape gets the useless error.** (2026-10-10 benchmark wave four; M) A single method call produces the diagnostic earlier reports single out as the standard the rest should meet: `s.trim()` gives *"'s' is not an imported module, and Ziman has no methods -- if 's' is a value, call a function on it instead"*. A **chain** does not: `s.to_upper().len()`, `s.slice(0, 1).to_upper()` and `s.a(1).b(2)` all give `expected ')', found '('`, a bare positional error naming nothing, because the chain fails in the parser and never reaches the typechecker that produces the good message. **This is the wrong way round**: chaining is the *more* idiomatic form in the training distribution -- `text.split(" ").map(f).join(" ")` is how Python, JS, Rust and Kotlin all write it -- so the shape a model is most likely to produce gets the worst message. Two of ten cold attempts in wave four hit exactly this (tasks 14 and 47) and neither saw the sentence that would have fixed it. Done 2026-10-10: `parse_postfix` now raises `ziman_report_method_chain_call` when a `(` follows a `.field`, which is safe because **no legitimate form of `expr.field(` existed** -- `s.a().b()`, `o.inner.go(1)` and `hs[0].go(1)` were all bare syntax errors, verified before touching it. The message names **both** readings, because the parser cannot tell them apart and both are true: if a method was meant there are none, and if the field holds a function value that is real (C15) but callable only on a plain variable, so *binding the receiver* is the fix either way and one sentence serves both. The loader's measured hint table (`join` -> `collections.join`, `keys` -> `map_keys`, and the rest from F28) was factored into `method_call_hint` and is reused here rather than duplicated, since naming the wrong function costs a reader a round whether the receiver is a variable or an expression. Three fixtures: the chained call, the nested-field call, and `method_call_on_variable` for everything that must keep working (a function-valued field on a variable, a plain field read, a field bound to a val and called).
- [x] **F51** **`type X = A | B` and the `|>` pipeline both get `unexpected character '|' (did you mean '||'?)`, which is never the right reading.** (2026-10-10 benchmark wave four; S) `'|'` appeared in two of ten cold attempts in unrelated roles and both got the same suggestion of the boolean-or operator: `type Light = Red | Green | Amber` (task 34), a sum type in ML/Haskell/TypeScript style, where Ziman writes `enum Light { Red, Green, Amber }`; and `|> filter(...) |> map(...) |> sum()` (task 39), the F#/Elixir/OCaml pipeline. A habit message already exists for `type X = {...}` but not for the `A | B` form. Suggesting `||` cannot be right in either case -- a boolean operator where a type declaration or a pipeline was meant -- so both now get their own sentence. Done 2026-10-10: the `|` is fatal **in the lexer**, which is why neither ever reached the parser's existing "Ziman has no 'type' declaration" message -- a message that already names `enum` and would have been the right answer. Rather than move that logic, the lexer now distinguishes three cases at the point the `|` is read: `|>` names the nested-call and one-`val`-per-step forms; a `|` on a line opening a `type` declaration (`line_opens_type_decl`, which also accepts `pub type`) names `enum Light { Red, Green, Amber }` and `switch`; and the remaining bare `|` keeps a message that now names **`bits.int_or(a, b)`** first -- the likeliest intent for `a | b` between ints, and a function that actually exists -- alongside `||`, `enum` and the absent pipeline. Verified against the two real cold attempts that produced the finding, not only against hand-written cases: both now get the correct sentence. Three fixtures, one per branch; `||` and the `bits` functions unaffected.
- [x] **F52** **F33's project marker did nothing when the entry file was named by a relative path -- which is how it is always named.** (2026-10-11; S) Found while starting the tier-4 backend-test migration, by trying the thing rather than trusting the note. `find_project_marker` walks up from the entry file's directory looking for `ziman.toml`, and the walk ends at `strrchr(dir, '/') == NULL`. For an absolute path there is always a leading `/` to walk down to, so it reached the root; for a **relative** path -- `ziman src/test/ziman/x_test.zm`, the spelling the README and every handwritten invocation use -- it ran out of slashes at `src` and **never probed the current directory**, so a project's own marker was invisible. F33 exists precisely so a project's tests can import its modules, and that is the single invocation where it silently did nothing. Fixed by emptying `dir` once more and probing `.` before giving up. **The reason it survived review is the recurring one:** every case in `tests/run_module_path.sh` named the entry file *absolutely*, so the guard covered the shape its author had in hand. Three cases added -- a relative entry path from the project root, the same spelled `./...`, and the limit stated as a decision rather than left as a surprise: a relative walk cannot rise above the directory it starts in, because a relative path cannot spell its parent, so run from the project root or name the entry file absolutely. **Also fixed, in my own first attempt at the fix:** the probe buffer was sized from `dir` while `"."` was substituted for it, one byte longer, so the path silently truncated to `./ziman.tom` and the current-directory case still failed -- the test case passed only for `./a/b` spellings where `.` was a real component. Caught by running all three path forms rather than the one I had changed.
- [x] **F53** **`f(args...)` said `expected ')', found '..'` when the answer is that nothing is missing.** (2026-10-11; S) The first finding from tier-4 item 1, and found the way that task was ranked first to find things: by writing real code against the language. Reaching for a spread at a call site is a Rust/JS/Python habit, and the old error named the token rather than the fix, several characters from the real question. **The fix is that there is no question: a variadic parameter takes an array directly**, so `total(args)` with `args: [int]` works, as do loose arguments, no arguments, and forwarding one variadic to another (`fn forward(ns: int...) { return total(ns) }`). `parse_call_args` now says exactly that. **Worth recording because I believed the old message:** writing the backend's first Ziman test I read `expected ')', found '..'`, concluded an array could not reach a variadic, wrote four INSERT calls out longhand with a comment asserting the limitation, and committed it. I only found out while writing this entry up -- checking the claim before recording it, which is the one habit that would have saved the detour. The backend commit is corrected and the inserts now loop. Two fixtures: the diagnostic, and `variadic_takes_an_array` for all four forms that work.
- [x] **F54** **Ziman could not start another program, which made a test harness, a build script and a deploy tool all unwritable in it.** (2026-10-11; M) The blocker tier-4 item 1 ran into, and the reason that item was ranked first: **19 of the backend's 20 test suites start the server as a child process**, and the one already migrated is the only one that does not. `os` had `getenv`/`exit`/`pid` and nothing to spawn with. New `process` core module (`lib/core/process.zm`, `src/native_process.c`): `start`/`run`/`output`, `wait`, `terminate`/`kill`, `release`, `pid`, with per-call environment overrides, a working directory, and redirection of the child's stdout and stderr. Three decisions worth keeping. **(1) Output goes to a file, never a pipe**, and there is deliberately no pipe-reading API: a pipe nobody drains fills and blocks the child, so a server logging to one deadlocks after a few kilobytes and "read it after waiting" *is* the deadlock; a file has no limit and outlives the child, which is when a test wants it. **(2) posix_spawn, not fork/exec** -- the interpreter is multithreaded, and allocating an argv between a fork and its exec is already undefined. **(3) `wait` reaps and ends the handle**, so a second call is a runtime error naming both causes rather than an invented -1. Exit codes follow the shell convention, `128 + signal`, so `terminate` then `wait` reads as 143. **Two self-inflicted faults caught by running it rather than reading it, both the session's recurring lesson applied to code minutes old:** a `reaped` check inside `wait` was **dead code**, because `wait` drops the handle before any second call can reach it -- removed, and the message that does fire now carries the guidance; and the `posix_spawn_file_actions_addchdir_np` call needed `_GNU_SOURCE`, which the default build passes in CFLAGS and **the nogc build does not**, so it compiled one way and failed the other until `make check` caught it. Three fixtures (run/exit codes/missing program, start-signal-wait with env and cwd and redirection, the double-wait diagnostic) and `process` added to the lib-manual GROUPS table.
- [x] **F55** **`process` needed a wait with a deadline and a SIGINT, both found by the suite that asserts on them.** (2026-10-11; S) The backend's `lifecycle_test` distinguishes SIGINT from SIGTERM (a program with a shutdown handler treats them differently) and gives every wait a timeout. F54 had neither. **`wait_for(p, millis) -> Result<Option<int>, string>`** answers `Some(code)` if the child ended in time and `None` if it is still running, reaping only in the first case so a caller can escalate to `kill` and ask again. **It matters more than its size:** `wait` on a child that never exits blocks for good, so a test that should report "the server did not shut down" would instead never finish, which is the worst way for a test to fail -- the module's doc comment now says to prefer `wait_for` wherever a hang is possible. Implemented by polling `waitpid(WNOHANG)` on a 10 ms tick rather than with a signal handler or `waitid`'s timeout, because it has to work from any thread of a multithreaded interpreter without installing anything process-wide. **`interrupt(p)`** sends SIGINT; the module now offers exactly three named signals -- interrupt, terminate, kill -- rather than a raw number, which would be both less readable and less portable. Fixture asserts the timeout path, `128 + 9` after a kill, a prompt child, and that SIGINT ends a child -- deliberately *not* the exit code there, since whether a non-interactive shell runs its INT trap or dies of the signal is the shell's business and not this module's.
- [x] **F56** **The per-arena sorted block index: collection was quadratic in live heap size, and is not any more.** (R, ROADMAP.md:714 item 2; M) The one fix for `gc_lookup`'s linear block walk that nobody had tried, after F31 measured and reverted four cheaper ones. `gc_lookup` asks "which block holds this address" once per live object during marking, and answered it by walking the arena's block list -- so the cost was objects x blocks, both of which grow with the heap. **The entry's own numbers were wrong in a way worth fixing first:** its headline 6,829ms "with a 100,000-entry map alive" reproduces at **200,000** entries, not 100,000, and the real shape is not a flat 41x toll but a quadratic -- 215ms with nothing alive, 1,787ms at 100k, 6,303ms at 200k, 23,852ms at 400k, four times the cost for twice the heap. **Fixed** with an address-sorted array of block pointers per arena, binary-searched by a new `arena_block_containing`, maintained eagerly on each block add and each reclaim. Paired interleaved rounds: **100k 1,831-1,967 -> 531-551ms, 200k 6,472-6,527 -> 1,189-1,263ms, 400k 23,950-24,636 -> 2,614-2,801ms** (8.8x), `map_get` 184-234 -> 66-78ms, `map_set` 108-117 -> 63-66ms, and an allocation with nothing alive **unchanged** at 221-251 -> 241-250ms, which is the control that says a one-block arena pays nothing. **Three decisions:** the index needs **no atomics or versioning**, because the owning thread is the only one that allocates, reclaims, collects and reads it -- Boehm's root walk, the one concurrent reader of an arena, walks the *list* and never the index; it is maintained **eagerly** rather than rebuilt lazily behind a version counter, since a block add is rare and eager maintenance avoids mutating a cache through the `const Arena *` that `gc_lookup` holds; and the first 8 entries live **in the `Arena` struct**, because `arena_create_scanned` runs once per `Channel` message and an index that allocated would tax every one of them. **Verified, not assumed:** the whole fixture suite re-run with `ARENA_INDEX_INLINE` forced to 1 so every arena takes the mmap growth path (943 passed), a new `gc_block_index_churn` fixture that grows past the inline capacity and drops it three times while holding substrings and slices -- the interior-pointer path that depends on having found the right block -- identical under both builds and under the baseline binary, the six `test-arena`/`test-gc-*` unit targets, and valgrind against the baseline: **zero invalid reads or writes in either**, the only findings being the conditional-on-uninitialised noise a conservative stack scanner inherently produces, which the baseline has at its own comparison line too. Also corrected: the entry pointed at a DESIGN.md section, "MapBuilder vs CPython dict", that does not exist -- the record is F31.
- [x] **F57** **Fixtures can assert *where* a diagnostic was reported, which 452 substring fixtures could not.** (R, ROADMAP.md "A real test harness"; S) The entry asks for "line/col assertions once diagnostics get richer", and the richness was already there: `ziman --json` has emitted `phase`/`line`/`col`/`message` per diagnostic since principle 5, and `tests/run_tests.sh` ignored all of it, comparing a substring against stderr. **So this needed no compiler work at all** -- a third fixture kind, `name.diag`, holding `<phase> <line>:<col>` and an optional message substring, one line per diagnostic in order, compared by `tools/check_diag.py`. The harness adds `--json` to a `.diag` fixture's run itself, since forgetting it would fail the fixture for a reason unrelated to its subject. **Why it matters, shown rather than asserted:** a `.err` fixture says a message appeared somewhere in stderr and nothing about its position, so a diagnostic that names the right problem several tokens from it passes -- which is precisely what F50 (`expected ')', found '('` from the lambda parser) and F53 (`expected ')', found '..'`) each were. Verified by **reintroducing the fault two ways**: a deliberately wrong expectation, and a real one-column shift in `src/parser.c`'s own call to `ziman_report_method_chain_call`. The `.diag` fixture failed with `col is 29, wanted 28`; **the old `.err` fixture, restored against the same faulty compiler, passed.** That is the gap. **Second benefit, which was my own framing of this item before reading it:** omitting the substring asserts position alone, so a position fixture stops pinning wording as a side effect -- the churn that made `diag_map_as_function` fail on a reworded-but-correct Map hint hours earlier. A trailing `...` allows more diagnostics after the listed ones, which cascades need: one stray `let` produces five, the noise the 2026-10-10 benchmark reported as "the rest is noise a blind fixer can chase", and that cascade is now pinnable. **Scope kept deliberately small:** four fixtures converted, the ones where position *is* the subject (`diag_method_chain`, `diag_spread_in_call`, `diag_sum_type_with_pipe`, `diag_pipeline_operator`); converting the other 448 would be churn with no evidence behind it, and `.err` remains right for a fixture that only cares that something was refused.
- [x] **F58** **`#[deprecated]` on a struct and an enum, with the one case that cannot work yet saying why.** (R, ROADMAP.md "`#[deprecated]` and `#[experimental]`"; S) The entry's remaining "still open" list, minus the part that is blocked. **First, a correction to my own tier-4 table:** I had this item down as "currently inert", which is wrong -- `#[deprecated]` on a *function* has been linted cross-module since 2026-10-05 and `#![experimental]` generates the manual's callout. What was open was narrower. **Done:** `StructDecl` and `EnumDecl` now carry the attribute (both already had the `source_file` the same-module rule needs), the parser records it instead of refusing it, and `lint_deprecated_struct`/`lint_deprecated_enum` warn from `check_struct_literal` and `check_enum_access`. **The one design choice, and its reason:** only sites a programmer *writes* are linted -- a struct literal, an enum access -- and **not a type annotation**, because a function migrating away from a deprecated type still has to name it to accept one, so warning there would be loudest in exactly the code doing the migration. Linting inside `find_struct`/`find_enum` instead was rejected for the same reason: 12 call sites each, most of them resolutions nobody wrote. **`val` stays refused**, but the message now states what it would take: a module-level val becomes a plain binding in the global env (`check_global_vals`), so a reference is an identifier lookup with no link back to the declaration; supporting it means carrying the deprecation on the `Binding`. Refusing with a reason beats a half-feature that warns in some places and not others. **Verified by reintroducing two faults**, not by reading: removing the struct lint call (fixture caught it, "expected 4 diagnostic(s), got 2") and dropping the same-source check so the library's own internal uses warn too ("expected 4, got 5") -- the second is what proves the same-module silence is actually tested rather than merely asserted in a comment. The cross-module fixture is the first real use of **F57's `.diag`**, since a deprecation warning on the wrong line is as unhelpful as one that never fired and a substring match cannot tell those apart. **Still blocked, and left alone:** whether a deprecation should warn by default or be an error under a "deny" policy needs "warnings/notes as a separate severity", which does not exist -- a language-wide decision about severities rather than a deprecation question.
- [x] **F59** **`server`'s CORS was missing `Vary: Origin`, and a fixture had recorded the omission as correct.** (R, ROADMAP.md "Accounts and tokens for backends"; M) The entry asks for "interceptors for CORS and request logging, as a wrapper around `dispatch`". **Two of its premises have moved.** The CORS surface already exists (`CorsPolicy`, `cors_policy`, `cors_preflight`, `cors_apply`), and it deliberately rejected the wrapper shape with a reason worth keeping: "a wrapper would have to return a closure and allocate one per request, and this composes with anything by sitting either side of it". So the shape was not the work. **What was the work: the generalisation had never been exercised by the code it was generalised from.** `lib/http/server.zm` says its CORS was "generalised from the reference backend's own `route_cors`", and the backend went on running its own `route_cors`/`with_cors`/`origin_allowed` and never called a single library CORS function. Comparing the two found the library emitting **two** of the backend's three headers: **`Vary: Origin` was absent from the entire CORS section**. That matters -- without it a shared cache may hand an allowed origin's `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` to a page that must not read the answer, or hand a cached refusal to an origin that should have been allowed. **And the fixture is the sharper half of the finding:** `http_server_cors` already printed every header of every case, so it was not an untested path -- its recorded `.out` *was* the defect, a test that locks a mistake in rather than one that misses it. **Fixed** with a shared `cors_vary` helper so the ordinary answer and the preflight refusal cannot drift apart: `Vary: Origin` now goes on every answer `cors_apply` touches *and* on `cors_preflight`'s 403, since whether `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` is present is itself what varies by origin; omitted only for a wildcard policy, where the answer genuinely does not depend on the origin. **Then the backend was migrated onto it**, deleting `route_cors`'s body, `with_cors` and `origin_allowed` -- 52 lines out, 16 in -- which is what now keeps the library exercised. The library's defaults turned out to match the hand-rolled values exactly (methods, `Authorization, Content-Type`, 3600, credentials). **Verified three ways:** `cors_test` byte-identical in both languages *and* the Python's own output unchanged from before the migration; the library fixture rewritten against the corrected behaviour; and, because no test asserts the newly-covered paths, a live server checked by hand -- a disallowed origin and a request with no `Origin` now both answer with `Vary: Origin` where they were previously bare. **Still open from this entry:** request logging as an interceptor (`ServeOptions.log_requests` covers the server-level case today), an OAuth2 login on `client`, and RS256/ES256, which needs public-key operations in `crypto`.
- [x] **F60** **`client`'s `timeout_ms` did not bound the connect, so a 1-second timeout took 136 seconds -- and then said "request timed out".** (R, ROADMAP.md "`net` follow-ups"; M) The entry's remaining half was "UDP, and a connect timeout beyond the OS default". The connect timeout turned out to be a measured defect rather than a missing nicety. **`client.open_socket` connected with a plain `net.connect` and called `set_timeout` *afterwards***, so the caller's budget covered reads and writes and not the connect, which fell back to the OS's SYN retry budget. Measured against TEST-NET-1: `timeout_ms: Some(1000)` **took 135,942ms** -- 136x over -- and reported `request timed out`, which reads as though the caller's deadline had fired rather than the kernel giving up two and a quarter minutes later. **Fixed** with `net.connect_timeout(host, port, millis)`: a native non-blocking connect, `poll` for writability, then the socket's own `SO_ERROR`, with the fd restored to blocking either way because everything downstream is written against a blocking socket. **Three decisions worth keeping.** The budget is **shared across `getaddrinfo` candidates** -- a host resolving to three addresses gets `timeout_ms` in total, not three times it, which a per-attempt deadline would have given away quietly. It is a **separate function, not an optional parameter** on `net.connect`, because an optional parameter can be omitted only in a *named* call, so adding one would have broken every existing positional `net.connect(host, port)`. And the **TLS path connects then hands the socket to `start_tls_with`** rather than calling `connect_tls_with`, which is that same pair with an untimed connect inside it -- so https is bounded too, not just http. **Verified:** the probe went **135,942ms -> 1,001ms**; a real `https://example.com` fetch still answers 200 through the rewritten branch; `make test-tls` passes; and a deterministic fixture covers the two cases any machine can assert -- a live listener connects, and a closed port comes back **refused rather than timed out**, which is what shows the non-blocking path reads `SO_ERROR` instead of calling every non-immediate answer a timeout. The timeout path itself cannot be a portable fixture (it needs a host that silently drops SYNs; a sandbox with no route answers instantly), so it is measured by hand and recorded here instead of faked. **UDP: deliberately not done.** Nothing in `lib/` or the reference backend uses datagrams, and this project ranks capability last of its three priorities -- adding a surface with no consumer is how a library grows code that is never exercised, which is exactly what F59 had just finished cleaning up in `server`'s CORS. Left open with that reasoning rather than silently skipped.
- [x] **F61** **Opt-in response compression, and the `ServeOptions` break that made it the last one.** (R, ROADMAP.md `http` follow-ups; M) The entry lists "multipart, compression, `Date`/`Server` headers". **Two of the four were already settled, checked on the wire rather than from the comments:** `Date` is sent on every response (`Date: Mon, 12 Oct 2026 00:36:44 GMT`), and `Server` is a decided **no** -- the library omits it because "a `Server` header only tells an attacker what to look for", and the backend's `headers_test` asserts its absence, so building it would reverse a decision and fail a passing test. Client-side compression was done too (asks gzip/deflate, decodes, caps on expansion); the server half genuinely was not -- `Accept-Encoding: gzip` got no `Content-Encoding` and an unchanged `Content-Length`. **Built as `server.compressed(req, resp, min_bytes)`**, a function rather than something `write_response` does, so it composes the way the CORS pair does -- in front of whichever code writes -- and `write_response`'s public signature is untouched. **Off unless asked for (`ServeOptions.compress_responses`), which Menderes chose when I put it to him**, and the reason is security rather than CPU: compressing a response holding a secret alongside attacker-influenced text leaks it through the compressed length (BREACH), and an API whose login answer carries a JWT is that shape. Four conditions all have to hold -- the request offers gzip (`gzip;q=0` is read as the refusal it is), the handler has not already set `Content-Encoding`, the body is at least `min_bytes`, and the gzip actually came out smaller. **`Vary: Accept-Encoding` goes on every answer it touches**, compressed or not, for the identical reason F59 added `Vary: Origin`: whether the body is gzipped is itself what varies by the header. **The side effect worth more than the feature:** adding a field to `ServeOptions` broke eight construction sites (four fixtures, two doc examples, the shutdown test server, the backend), because every field of a struct literal is required. So `pub fn serve_options()` now returns the defaults and every site was converted to `with server.serve_options() { ... }` -- which makes this the *last* time a new field breaks a caller. **Verified:** 947 fixtures in both builds; a fixture covering all seven decisions including an incompressible body (whose first version used `(i * 7919) % 251`, which looks unstructured and gzips to a seventh of its size, so it asserted the opposite of what it claimed -- now genuinely random, printing only the decision since the gzipped length of random input varies); and on the wire, 13,200 bytes -> **117** with `curl --compressed` decoding it correctly, against 13,200 and no `Vary` at all with the flag off, which is what shows the opt-in default really holds. **Multipart: not in this commit.** I argued against it on the grounds that nothing in `lib/` or the backend parses one and the backend's own stats hardcode `documents: 0`, so the feature it would serve does not exist -- the same reasoning that left UDP alone in F60. Menderes decided to build it anyway, which is **F62**; file upload being on the roadmap makes a parser worth having before its first caller, which is a fair answer to "no consumer yet". **The backend is left with compression off**, since turning it on for an API that returns JWTs is its decision and not the library's.
- [x] **F62** **Reading a `multipart/form-data` body, and the two faults a copy of the algorithm would have hidden.** (R, ROADMAP.md `http` follow-ups, the one item F61 left; M) **I argued against building this** -- nothing in `lib/` or the backend parses a multipart body, and the backend's stats hardcode `documents: 0` -- and Menderes decided to build it anyway, which is the better call: file upload is on the roadmap, and a parser is worth having before its first caller rather than written under one. **`server.multipart(req) -> Result<[Part], HttpError>`**, with `multipart_with` for a different part limit, `part`/`part_text` for lookup by field name, and `multipart_max_parts = 128` so a body of nothing but boundary lines cannot make one value per 40 bytes. **It works in bytes throughout**, never text: a part may be a file, so the fixture carries a NUL and a `0xFF` through untouched, and `part_text` keeps three answers apart -- text, no such field (`Ok(None)`), and a field whose bytes are not text (`Err`), the error outer so a caller already returning a `Result` can `try` it. **`multipart_boundary` is a separate function on purpose:** a request that is not multipart at all is a **415** and every rejection of a body is a **400**, and a single function could not say which. **`Part.filename` is documented as the client's claim, not a path** -- it is the name another machine's filesystem had, and may be `../../etc/passwd` -- which is the one field in this API that invites a traversal. **Two decisions earned by testing rather than argument:** the `Content-Disposition` line is *scanned* rather than split on ";", because a real browser sends the filename the user had, semicolons and escaped quotes included; and the body gets a CRLF prepended before the search, because every boundary but the first is preceded by one that belongs to the delimiter. **Both were verified by reintroducing the fault in the library and rebuilding** -- not in the standalone copy I had prototyped in, which would have proved nothing about the shipped code. Dropping the quoted-value branch truncates `filename="one;two.txt"` to `"one` and leaves quotes on every value; dropping the prepend **silently loses the first part of every body** (2 parts read as 1) and turns two of the four 400s into wrong answers. Three prototype errors worth recording, all from assuming rather than checking: `pub const` is not a declaration (module constants are `pub val`), a module-level `pub val` is not reachable as `module.name` and needs `use server.multipart_max_parts`, and **the bundled `lib/` is embedded into the binary as a C string at build time**, so a library edit does nothing until `make ziman` -- which cost twenty minutes of a struct that "did not exist". Also fixed two stale claims F61 left: the module header still said it did not do multipart or compression, and the compression note named `ServeOptions.compress` for a field called `compress_responses`. `docs/lib/server.md` and `docs/CORELIB.md` are generated from the source, so the prose lives in the library's own comments. **Verified:** 948 fixtures in both builds; `http_multipart` covers 28 lines -- a field and a file, binary and empty parts, awkward filenames, case-insensitive names and bare parameter values, a preamble and an epilogue (RFC 2046 says to ignore both), a body of only the terminator, four malformed bodies, a non-multipart body, the part cap, and a repeated field name.
- [x] **F63** **`Accept` quality weights, ranked -- and the specificity rule that turned out to be load-bearing for behaviour that already shipped.** (R, ROADMAP.md "Content types on routes", the first of its two follow-ups; S) The entry was accurate for once: `accepts` answered yes/no, so **the server's own encoder order decided what a client got** and the weights it sent were read only for a refusal. `http.quality(headers, media)` now gives the weight as a number -- 1.0 with no `Accept` header, the `q` of the matching range otherwise, 0.0 when none matches -- `http.best_media(headers, candidates)` picks the best of what a handler can write, and `negotiate` ranks an endpoint's encoders by it. An endpoint offering `text/plain` then `text/csv` answers `Accept: text/csv, text/plain;q=0.5` with **CSV**; the endpoint's order now breaks ties only, which keeps it as the server's preference among equals, and since nothing is ever chosen at zero, `q=0` is a refusal rather than a last resort. **The half that was not on the entry matters more:** the **most specific** matching range decides (`type/subtype` > `type/*` > `*/*`), per RFC 9110 -- so `Accept: */*, text/*;q=0` refuses `text/plain` instead of letting the wildcard rescue it. **That rule subsumes a special case that already shipped:** the old code made two passes precisely so an exact `q=0` beat a wildcard, and the fault check proved the point -- dropping specificity does not merely lose the new behaviour, it regresses `application/json;q=0, */*` back to accepting JSON, which the module's own comment had promised it would not. **Both guards verified by reintroducing the fault in the library and rebuilding:** first-match-wins answers `text/plain` to a client that asked for CSV, and no-specificity turns the `*/*, text/*;q=0` endpoint case from 406 into 200. One deliberate reversal: `is_zero_quality` avoided parsing a float on purpose, its comment arguing that a malformed weight should not fail the whole header; ranking needs the number, so the parse is now real and that tolerance is a fallback to 1.0 instead -- one bad parameter in one range is not a reason to answer 406 to a client that did say what it wanted. A weight outside 0..1 is used as sent rather than clamped: `q=5` outranks `q=1` because that is plainly what was meant, and a negative one refuses. **Verified:** 949 fixtures in both builds; the new `http_accept_ranking` covers 16 header forms against three media types plus 9 `best_media` cases and `accepts`'s own five documented ones, and `http_endpoints` gained four endpoint-level cases -- its `.out` diff is **four pure additions, nothing existing changed**, which is the strongest statement that ranking altered no previously recorded answer.
- [x] **F64** **Mutual TLS: a listener that authenticates its clients by certificate, and the authentication bypass a missing flag would have opened.** (R, ROADMAP.md "Database access follow-ups" item 1, which tier-4 #9 names; L) The entry listed three things still open -- "server-side TLS (`listen_tls`), client certificates, ALPN" -- and **the first had already shipped**: `net.listen_tls` exists, has its own TLS suite test, and `http`'s own docs tell you to hand `serve` a listener from it. Ninth entry in a row with a drifted premise. **Built, both sides:** `net.TlsServerOptions` (`client_ca_file`, `require_client_cert`) with `tls_server_options` and `listen_tls_with_options` makes a listener ask for and verify a client certificate; `net.TlsClientOptions` (`verify`, `ca_file`, `cert_file`, `key_file`) with `tls_client_options`, `start_tls_with_options` and `connect_tls_with_options` presents one; `net.peer_certificate_subject` gives the peer's one-line subject, which is the identity an application authorizes against. The existing positional `start_tls_with` / `connect_tls_with` / `listen_tls` are unchanged and now delegate, and the options structs follow F61's lesson -- a constructor plus `with`, so the next field added here breaks no caller. **Two findings the test transcript records because neither is obvious.** A client whose certificate the server refuses **still gets `Ok` from the handshake** and only learns on the first read, because TLS 1.3 validates the client certificate after the client's Finished message -- so "connected" is not "authenticated", which is now stated in `TlsClientOptions`'s own comment. And requiring a certificate with no CA file to verify it against is refused at `listen`, because OpenSSL would otherwise demand a certificate it cannot check and fail **every** connection with a complaint about the certificate rather than about the configuration. **Both guards verified by reintroducing the fault and rebuilding, and the second is the serious one:** without the missing-CA guard the listener comes up and rejects every client; without `SSL_VERIFY_FAIL_IF_NO_PEER_CERT` a listener configured to *require* a client certificate **serves an anonymous client** -- a silent authentication bypass, which is the worst thing this feature could do and exactly what the fixture catches. **The test harness needed no changes:** `run_tls_tests.sh` already generates two self-signed certificates, and because each is its own CA, `b` serves as both a client certificate and the CA the server trusts, while `a` -- the server's own -- is the untrusted client certificate. **Not done, with the reason recorded: mutual TLS through `http.client`**, which threads a lone `ca_file` positionally through six call layers; the right fix is to thread a `net.TlsClientOptions` instead, mechanical but wide enough to be its own task. **Verified:** the TLS suite is 6 tests, 0 failures (it was 5), the new `tls_client_certificate` covering a trusted client, an anonymous one, an untrusted one, the same two against a listener that requires a certificate, the configuration error, a plain listener a client offers a certificate to anyway (which is never sent, as documented), and a certificate given without its key; output identical across three consecutive runs before it was recorded.
- [x] **F65** **ALPN, and the preference order that OpenSSL's own helper would have left to a coin flip.** (R, ROADMAP.md "Database access follow-ups" item 1 and the HTTP/2 entry's step 1, both of which tier-4 #9 names; M) `TlsServerOptions.alpn` and `TlsClientOptions.alpn` offer protocol lists, and **`net.negotiated_protocol(sock) -> Option<string>`** reports what the handshake agreed -- which is the signature the HTTP/2 roadmap entry had guessed, down to the name. The lists are fields on the options structs rather than parameters on `listen_tls` and `connect_tls_with`, so those keep their signatures and the structs stay the one place new TLS settings go. **The selection callback is written out rather than left to `SSL_select_next_proto`**, and that is the substance of this task: that function's two list parameters are named for NPN, where the *client* chose, so which list's order decides is exactly what a reader of the callback would have to guess -- and guessing wrong silently hands protocol choice to the client. Written out, the listener's order is visibly its preference, which is what RFC 7301 leaves to the server, and the test pins it by having the two sides disagree on purpose: a client offering `["http/1.1", "h2"]` to a listener offering `["h2", "http/1.1"]` gets **h2**. **No overlap is a fatal `no_application_protocol` alert**, not a connection without ALPN: a client that offered only protocols this server does not speak has said it cannot use the connection, and serving it anyway looks like success and then fails as a protocol error on the first byte. **Both verified by reintroducing the fault:** swapping the loop nesting makes the client's order win (`http/1.1` instead of `h2`), and returning `SSL_TLSEXT_ERR_NOACK` lets a client that offered only `spdy/3` complete an exchange with no agreed protocol. **Lifetime, reasoned rather than tested** since no output would show it: the listener's wire-format list lives on the `NetHandle` and is freed beside its `SSL_CTX`, because the callback is registered on the context and reads the list through its `arg`, so it must outlive every handshake; `*out` points into that list rather than into the callback's `in`, which does not outlive the call. The client side needs no such field -- `SSL_CTX_set_alpn_protos` copies, and answers 0 for success unlike almost everything else there. One self-inflicted fix: the name-length error first echoed the offending name, and a 256-byte one pushed the explanation off the end of the buffer, so the message said what was wrong in bytes the reader never saw; it now reports the length and no name. **A process lesson, after `fmt-check` failed F64's gate at the twelfth minute:** `make fmt-check` is a seconds-long pre-flight and now runs before the gate, not inside it. **Verified:** the TLS suite is 7 tests, 0 failures (it was 6), the new `tls_alpn` covering the disagreeing orders, each side offering one protocol, neither offering any, a listener that offers none at all, no overlap, and a name too long and one empty; output identical across two consecutive runs before it was recorded, and unchanged by the formatter afterwards.
