- [x] **F66** **A variant's payload is an expected type, which it always should have been.** (R, ROADMAP.md's `Result<T, E>` entry, last bullet; tier-4 #10's one implementable item; S) `Holder { items: [] }` worked and `Json.JArray([])` did not, although a variant has exactly one declared payload type and it is as statically fixed as a struct field's. The bullet asked for "a general mechanism extending the constructor's own declared field type to variant construction" -- **there was nothing to design: the mechanism existed and one call site did not use it.** `check_enum_access` called `check_expr` with no expected type where `check_value_against_param_type` was right there, already serving call arguments, `val` initializers and parameter defaults. **So the fix brought six forms, not one:** the empty `[]`, an unannotated lambda (its parameter types now come from the payload type), `ok(...)`/`err(...)`, `channel_new()`, an `if`/`switch` used as a value, and a bare value auto-wrapped to Option or Result were all equally refused at a payload, and all now work. The bullet had noticed only the array. **Two mistakes of my own, both from asserting rather than checking.** I wrote a comment claiming a `require_not_void` call after the helper preserved the void message -- false: the helper raises that error itself, so my call was **dead code** (the same fault I deleted in F54) and the message had silently changed from "as a variant payload" to "as an argument". The honest fix was to give the helper the context word, which it now takes. And I counted its call sites with one grep pattern, got four, and the build found **three more** spelled differently -- the recurring failure this project keeps teaching me. Giving each its own word improved two messages beyond this task's scope, both checked by hand: a void `val` initializer said "as an argument" and now says "as an initializer", and a void parameter default now says "as a default value". **Verified:** 949 fixtures green before the negative fixtures were added, so no pinned message moved; `enum_payload_expected_type` covers all eleven accepted forms; two `.diag` fixtures hold the refusals, and the position assertion was shown to bite (`col is 22, wanted 23`) rather than being vacuous; and reverting the one call site makes the fixture fail on its first line with the original "cannot infer the element type" error.
- [x] **F67** **Tier-4 #10's design pass: four of its five items were not open, and the fifth is answered by a count.** (R, ROADMAP.md's enum and `Result<T, E>` entries; tier-4 #10; M) The item read "language gaps needing a design pass first: trait/interface polymorphism, `match`/patterns, `?` propagation, `Option<T>` follow-ups, empty-array-literal inference". **Checked one by one against the tree, as every entry in this tier has needed.** (1) **trait/interface polymorphism is a decided no**, not a gap: DESIGN.md's "traits/interfaces stance" (F1) rejected a general system -- coherence rules, orphan rules, default bodies, eventually dynamic dispatch -- and the one narrow extension it approved, `Row` on the closed `Eq`/`Hash`/`Ord` list, was **withdrawn by C18** because `from_row` cannot dispatch the way `compare` does. The roadmap already recorded that; nothing to do but stop listing it as open. (2) **`?` propagation is done, as `try`** -- `val s = try connect(host, port)` yields the payload and returns the `Err`, used throughout `lib/`; the bullet claiming "real, Go-style verbosity, accepted for now" had been stale for some time. (3) **`Option<T>`'s follow-up is done and the bullet's own guess was right**: it predicted Option would "very likely use this exact same trick" as `ok`/`err` inference, and it does -- `peer: None`, `cert_file: Some(\"client.pem\")`. (4) **empty-array-literal inference was the one implementable item, done as F66.** (5) **`match`/patterns was the only genuine design question, and it is now answered with a measurement:** across `lib/` and the 13,000-line backend there are **304 `switch` sites, of which 4 immediately re-destructure**, so nested patterns -- `match`'s headline feature over `switch` -- would flatten four places in about 25,000 lines. Guards, struct/array destructuring and or-patterns show no demand either. **The part worth recording is what the measurement cannot see:** literal patterns (`match code { 404 { ... } }`) are structurally invisible to it, because an if-else chain on a string does not look like a failed `switch` -- so this does **not** claim they are unwanted, only that evidence for them must come from a writer's judgement rather than a grep. Written up as DESIGN.md's "Pattern matching, measured rather than argued", so the next reader inherits the number instead of repeating the argument, and the number to re-run is in the entry. **Also recorded: most of what the `match` bullet asked for arrived by other routes** -- `switch` is exhaustive with no default arm, usable as a value in most positions, and `if Some(x) =` binds -- which is why the bullet read worse than the language is. **Verified:** no code changed, so the gate is a documentation check: `design-index-check` regenerates DESIGN.md's index (123 sections) and the three roadmap bullets are struck in place with their original text preserved beneath, per this project's annotate-rather-than-delete rule.
- [x] **F68** **A deprecation warns on every compile, and the blocker I recorded for it did not exist.** (R, ROADMAP.md "`#[deprecated]` and `#[experimental]`"; tier-4 #4's remainder; S) **First, a correction to my own record.** F58's note and the roadmap annotation I wrote both said this was "blocked on something that does not exist: warnings/notes as a separate severity". **That was false.** `ziman_warning` has existed throughout, emitting `{\"severity\":\"warning\",\"phase\":\"lint\",...}` in `--json` and `file:line:col: warning: ...` in plain mode. The real situation was narrower and duller: the deprecation checks shared the gate `g_lint_active` with the *style* lints, and that gate is `g_lint_on && user code`, so a deprecation only reported under `--lint`. **The change is splitting that gate**: `g_user_code` (user code, not a derive expansion) now drives the deprecation checks, `g_lint_active` keeps the style lints behind the flag. **Menderes chose "every compile"** when I put the three options to him, over lint-only and over adding a deny flag. The argument that decides it is this item's own justification: the benchmark found **diagnostics** are what let a model self-correct, and a warning gated behind a second command is invisible to anything that just runs the program. **A warning never changes the exit code** -- verified directly, not assumed: a plain `--check` and a plain run both exit **0** with the warning printed, while `--lint` exits **1**, which is right because there finding things is the request. **A deny policy was left out on purpose:** a flag with no consumer, and nothing in the tree or the backend wants to enforce a migration. **The consequence I did not foresee, and it falsified my own harness:** `.diag` fixtures required a **non-zero** exit, because when F57 added them every diagnostic was an error. A warning on a successfully compiling program breaks that assumption, so the `.diag` branch now honours `name.exit` the way `.out` already did, defaulting to the old "must have failed". **Also fixed: SYNTAX.md was stale twice**, and the second one is mine -- it still said the attribute "is rejected" on a `struct` or `enum`, which **F58 made work** and did not document, so F58 updated the roadmap and left the language reference wrong for a day. **Deliberately not migrated: the three fixtures that now warn.** `http_server_hardening`, `http_head_falls_back_to_get` and `metrics_server` use `server.open_session_with_timeout`, and they are the only coverage of a function that is still public -- migrating them would leave the deprecated path untested. The replacement is also not a drop-in: `open_session_with_timeouts` sets `fresh: true` as well as a head timeout, so a substitution would change the first-byte wait in fixtures that exist to test timeouts. Their `.out` files compare stdout only, so they pass with the warning on stderr -- which is incidentally the proof that warnings do not pollute stdout. **Verified:** 953 fixtures in both builds (952 plus the new one), 7 TLS tests, and the backend checked clean of deprecated uses; the new `deprecated_warns_without_lint` passes `--lint` nowhere and asserts both the warning's position and `exit 0`; **both guards verified by reintroducing their faults** -- setting the fixture's `.exit` to 1 fails with "exit=0, wanted 1" (so the harness extension is not vacuous), and putting the deprecation check back behind `g_lint_active` fails with "expected 1 diagnostic(s), got 0".
- [x] **F69** **`#[deprecated]` on a `val`, without widening the interpreter's hottest allocation.** (R, ROADMAP.md "`#[deprecated]` and `#[experimental]`"; tier-4 #4; S) The last of the four declaration kinds. F58 refused it with a message naming what it would take -- "carrying the deprecation on the binding" -- and **that prescription was the expensive way to do it**: `Binding` is allocated once per binding per call and was measured as the interpreter's single hottest allocation (DESIGN.md "Binding names are borrowed"), so widening it by two or three fields for a rare attribute is the wrong trade. **A side table keyed by name avoids it**, and is sound on two things the language guarantees rather than hopes: the loader **merges every module into one** and self-qualifies global-val names, so a name is unique program-wide; and a local can never shadow a global val (ast.h's own comment), so an identifier matching one of those names *is* that val. The read is caught at `EXPR_IDENT`, which its own comment already called "the one place a name is read" -- so no new traversal was needed either. **A cross-module val can only be reached by importing the symbol** (`use mod.NAME`): Ziman has no dotted `mod.NAME` bare access for vals, so the import is the only way to write the case at all, which is why the fixture is shaped that way. **One limit recorded rather than left to be discovered:** a read inside *another* module's global val initializer does not warn, because `g_user_code` is set per function and global vals are checked before any function body; fixing it means tracking the declaring file through `check_global_vals`, which is more machinery than the case is worth until something chains deprecated vals across modules. **A fixture changed meaning rather than being deleted:** `diag_deprecated_on_val_refused` existed to pin the refusal, so F69 made it fail. Its program -- a deprecated val read in the file that declares it -- is exactly the same-module silence case, so it became `deprecated_val_same_module_silent`, whose `.diag` expects **no diagnostics at all**. That is not a formality: `check_diag.py` compares counts, so a stray warning fails it, which a `.out` fixture could not catch since those compare stdout only. **Verified:** 954 fixtures in both builds; **both guards verified by reintroducing their faults** -- removing the `EXPR_IDENT` hook fails with "expected 3 diagnostic(s), got 0", and removing the same-source check fails the silence fixture with "expected 0 diagnostic(s), got 1", which is what proves same-module silence is tested rather than merely asserted in a comment (the same pairing F58 used, for the same reason). Each read is its own site, so a val read twice warns twice, which the fixture pins at two different lines.
- [x] **F70** **`#![deprecated]` on a whole module, reported where the dependency is written.** (R, ROADMAP.md "`#[deprecated]` and `#[experimental]`"; tier-4 #4, the last piece; S) The refusal said "what a deprecated *module* should warn about -- every `use`, or every call into it -- is undecided". **Decided: the `use` line**, because it is the one place the dependency is written and so the one line that dropping the module means editing; warning at every call into it would repeat the same answer until it was noise. **Both import forms count** -- the whole-module `use oldmod` and the per-name `use oldmod.name` -- since each is a line that has to go, so importing two names from one deprecated module warns twice. That is the honest count, not a bug. **It lives in `loader.c`'s `resolve_named_imports`, and reading the tree is what put it there:** the redundant-`as` lint sitting a few lines below already records *why* -- "a module's `use` list belongs to the loader and is already gone from the merged Module the type checker is handed, which cost a first attempt at this rule". Putting module deprecation in `typecheck.c` would have repeated a mistake already paid for once. **Not behind `--lint`**, per F68's decision, and gated on the importing file not being a bundled module, which lands the same same-source rule the other four declaration kinds use: a user file importing a deprecated module warns, one bundled module importing another stays silent. **Two fixtures changed meaning rather than being deleted.** `module_attr_deprecated_rejected` pinned the refusal, so F70 made it fail; its program -- `#![deprecated]` on a single-file program -- turned out to be exactly the case worth pinning instead, since **nothing imports an entry file, so the attribute warns nowhere there**. It became `module_attr_deprecated_on_entry`, whose `.diag` expects no diagnostics, so the silence is tested rather than merely explained in a comment and cannot later be "fixed" into a warning pointing at no actionable line. `module_attr_unknown_rejected` pinned the message "(the only one is 'experimental')", now "(the two are 'experimental' and 'deprecated')". **Verified:** 955 fixtures in both builds; **both guards verified by reintroducing their faults** -- dropping the `!target->deprecated` test fails with "expected 4 diagnostic(s), got 5" because the undeprecated `newmod` import starts warning too, which is what proves the fixture tests *selectivity* and not merely that warnings fire, and removing the warning fails with "expected 4, got 0". **Tier-4 #4 is now complete:** all four declaration kinds plus the module form carry `#[deprecated]`, every one reports on an ordinary compile, and the only thing still refused is nothing.
- [x] **F71** **Request logging as a field, not an interceptor -- and the format that had no coverage at all.** (R, ROADMAP.md "Accounts and tokens for backends"; tier-4 #5, the half F59 left; M) The entry asked for "request logging as an interceptor, as a wrapper around `dispatch`", and **the shape is wrong for the same reason F59 recorded when it rejected it for CORS**: a wrapper would have to return a closure and allocate one per request. Worse here, a wrapper around `dispatch` cannot see what `serve` knows -- the peer, the bytes actually written *after* `compressed` has had its say, and the moment the read finished. **Built as `ServeOptions.on_request: Option<fn(RequestLog)>`**, one function value stored once, called per finished exchange. `log_requests` stays as the zero-config line and the two are independent. **`RequestLog` is a struct and that is the point:** the library builds it and the application only reads it, so a field added later cannot break a caller -- the exact inverse of F61's `ServeOptions` problem, where every field of a struct the *caller* builds is required. **`duration_ms` is the time to serve**, from the request being fully read to its response being written: not the client's slowness in sending, nor the idle wait on a keep-alive connection, neither of which is work the server did. **The library's first callback that returns nothing**, so the spelling is set here: `fn(RequestLog)`, not `fn(RequestLog) -> unit`, because a void function's type *is* the former and an application should not have to remember an annotation its own function does not carry. **A finding worth more than the feature: nothing in the tree had ever set `log_requests: true`** -- the one request-log format the library ships had **no coverage whatsoever**, which is why moving it after the write was safe to discover rather than to break. The new fixture covers both halves. **The logging point moved after `write_response`**, where the status and the bytes that actually went out are both known; before it, a compressed body's size was not yet decided. **A weak assertion caught and strengthened:** the fixture first printed "duration plausible" for a non-negative value under a minute, which **would have passed with the field hardcoded to 0**. It now asserts a band -- a handler that sleeps 150ms must land at 50ms or more, the three trivial ones under 50ms -- so a dead timer fails the first and a duration that accidentally measured the whole connection fails the others. **Verified:** 956 fixtures in both builds; **both guards verified by reintroducing their faults** -- unwiring `options.on_request` makes all four hook lines vanish, and hardcoding `duration_ms: 0` flips `/slow` to "under 50ms", which is what proves the band is load-bearing. A `HEAD` request logs 38 response bytes it does not send, which is the documented behaviour and now pinned. **The backend is left on `log_requests`:** it drives the flag from its own config, and choosing a structured format through the `log` module is the application's decision, not the library's -- the hook is there for it to adopt.
